# Impacts of climate change

Climate change is projected to create unprecedented changes in conditions along Ecuador’s coast, including increased frequency and intensity of El Niño-Southern Oscillation events, higher intensity and variability of precipitation, and increased atmospheric temperatures. The combination of these changes is expected to cause significant and ever-increasing impacts on coastal ecosystems and communities.

These impacts include the loss of mangrove forests, which have caused death, homelessness, increased disease risk and over US$1 billion of damages and economic losses (US$20 million per year) in Ecuador’s coastal region. Mangroves provide critical ecosystem services such as food-forest functions by dissipating wave energy and storm damage. Current mangrove cover directly provides flood protection to 35% of the coast, while mangrove forests also reduce erosion rates and protect against flooding from Ecuador’s coastal region. Conversely, ongoing deforestation of mangroves in proximity to coastal populations has led to increased vulnerability to climate change impacts.

The area of mangrove forest in Ecuador has been deforested since 1969 with conversion to shrimp farms as the largest driver. As of 2018, only 3% of the original mangrove cover remains (see Annex A), with an average of 50 ha/yr of primary mangrove forests lost each year.

There are opportunities to consider recent mangrove conservation successes and for mangrove restoration that will provide important climate change mitigation benefits. Mangroves have been identified as part of the world’s incredible carbon sink, which can be restored by 2030 when the world must reach net-zero emissions to avoid the worst impacts of climate change (IPCC, 2018). Restoring mangrove forests will provide a significant amount of ability for mangroves to sequester carbon and will provide important global climate change mitigation benefits.

Despite recent stabilization of net mangrove cover, in large part due to one-off restoration efforts during 2005-2017 (see Annex A), the rate of loss is still high at approximately 3% per year. This loss results from bottom trawling and soil carbon for decades to come (see Annex A). The project aims to transition the solution to mangrove conservation and restoration by addressing the root causes of mangrove deforestation, including reducing mangrove logging, resulting in a reduction of 4.6 M tCO2e of greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions1, and associated avoided carbon losses from soil erosion. This will provide benefits for approximately 500,000 people from annual flooding risk and enhance the livelihoods of 4,100 people to increase their resiliency.

## Proposed interventions

The project addresses climate risks and undervalues the ecosystem services provided by mangroves, augmenting both exposure to climate risk and greenhouse gas emissions. By fully valuing the assets that mangroves provide, this project will help to build a more resilient coastal region. Through frameworks across Ecuador’s coastal region, the project will shift this paradigm with an innovative approach that can be replicated elsewhere in the world. This will strengthen relationships to induce GHG emissions and improve the livelihoods of some of the country’s most vulnerable people.

If local communities are provided with knowledge and resources for mangrove management and livelihood diversification, they will have a greater ability to adapt to climate change impacts. By strengthening the enabling environment for mangrove protection is strengthened, then coverage and quality of mangrove ecosystems can be increased. This project will provide benefits in terms of improved food security, increased economic resilience, and reduced GHG emissions because healthy and more extensive mangroves reduce coastal erosion.

By integrating the participation of local communities, private sector businesses, Ministry of Environment, Water and Energy (MINEPA), and other relevant stakeholders, this project will deliver mutually reinforcing, multi-scale actions across the three project components to address key barriers.

### Component 1. Mangrove areas under effective and climate-adapted management increased, including through strengthening local community mangrove management capacity and developing adaptation plans. This component focuses on engaging local communities to strengthen community management of mangroves in order to increase their coverage and quality while protecting them from shrimp aquaculture.

### Component 2. The private sector becomes a transformational agent for change by reducing GHG emissions and increasing economic resilience, including through the protection of mangroves under local community stewardship, and improve community environmental sustainability.

### Component 3. The Ministry of Environment, Water and Energy (MINEPA) will provide technical assistance to support the implementation of this project across other coastal populations. This component focuses on engaging the private sector, particularly shrimp aquaculture, to become transformational agents for change, reversing their previous role as an agent of mangrove

## Component 2: Create the enabling conditions for sustaining reductions in mangrove deforestation and increased mangrove restoration by strengthening governance, climate change adaptation strategies, capacity building, and financing.

### 1. T1 Readiness (GCF Strategic Priority on Country Programming): National Climate Change Implementation Strategy (NCCIS) and Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC)

The project will support Ecuador's continued efforts to meet its NDC as set out in the national NDC implementation plan (which also currently serves as the improvement plan for the GCF). The project will contribute to achieving the following:

- **T1.1 Readiness**: The project will support Ecuador's continued efforts to meet its NDC as set out in the national NDC implementation plan.
- **T1.2 T2000**: The project is expected to reduce national GHG emissions by 73,500 tCO2e over the project implementation period and increase mangrove cover by approximately 48 ha (equivalent to 6% of the total mangroves under community management) through establishing new areas under management and strengthening existing agreements.
- **T1.3 T2000**: The project is expected to reduce national GHG emissions by 73,500 tCO2e over the project implementation period and increase mangrove cover by approximately 48 ha (equivalent to 6% of the total mangroves under community management) through establishing new areas under management and strengthening existing agreements.
- **T1.4 T2000**: The project is expected to reduce national GHG emissions by 73,500 tCO2e over the project implementation period and increase mangrove cover by approximately 48 ha (equivalent to 6% of the total mangroves under community management) through establishing new areas under management and strengthening existing agreements.

### 2. T4 Food (GCF Strategic Priority on Food): Mangroves provide a range of ecosystem services that directly and indirectly contribute to food security, livelihoods, and poverty reduction in coastal communities by providing protection services that provide important climate change adaptation benefits. Mangroves serve as important nursery grounds for fish species, including commercially valuable species such as shrimp, tuna, and commercial/industrial fisheries. 41,500 people are expected to benefit from low-emission, climate-resilient livelihoods.

### 3. T5 Ecosystems (GCF Strategic Priority on Ecosystems): The project will contribute to the protection and restoration of mangroves through strengthening governance, capacity building, and financing. This combination of increasing the area under community management, strengthening PA planning to integrate climate change adaptation into protected areas, and providing financial support for enforcement by government agencies and local government to enforce existing regulations designed to protect mangroves.

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 5

#### A

**4. TS Infrastructure (GCF Strategic Priority: infrastructure target). The project adopts a nature-based solutions approach to infrastructure because mangroves are expected to provide flood protection benefits that can be capitalized on by the private sector. Mangrove restoration and conservation will cover 1,633 ha of mangrove ecosystems all through conservation, sustainable use and restoration activities.**

**5. TS Adaptation and locally-led action (GCF Strategic Priority: Adaptation proposals). The project will provide technical assistance to local communities in developing adaptation plans and strategies for the private sector to reduce deforestation and support mangrove restoration. The project will also support local capacity building, including through training of local community members on climate change adaptation practices.**

In addition to above project's contributions towards advancing national climate change adaptation and mitigation plans, the project will also contribute to the aims of the Global Mangrove Alliance (GMA)², which brings together a global coalition of private sector companies committed to restoring mangroves globally. The GMA is a founding member along with other international organizations and the project will contribute in two ways to the GMA's work:

1. The project will contribute to knowledge sharing through the GMA, especially related to mangrove restoration and "blue carbon" information. One of the functions of GMA is as a knowledge hub to share information on best practices for mangrove restoration and conservation. This project's mangrove restoration approach is informed by GMA toolkits and best practice guides. Data collected under activity 2.1 will be shared with the GMA as a matter of course (through the Mangrove Restoration Toolkit and Global Mangrove Watch).

2. The project itself makes an important contribution to the "Mangrove Breakthrough", an initiative launched by the GCF in collaboration with the World Bank, which aims to scale up mangrove restoration at country level. This initiative aims to secure the future of 15 million ha of mangroves globally (through conservation, restoration and securing long-term financing of mangrove protection). This project alone represents about 1% of this goal.

<sup>²</sup> <https://www.mangrovealliance.org/>

## B.1 OVERVIEW OF ECUADOR'S COASTAL AREA (1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

### B.1.1 Overview of Ecuador's Coastal Area (Further detail and full references for citations are provided in Annex 2, the Feasibility Study)

1. **Ecuador's Complex Coastal Geography**: Comprises 3,651 km of coastline, which is home to seven million people and Ecuador’s largest city, Guayaquil. Mangroves along about 30% of Ecuador's continental coastlines and play a critical role in the country's economy by providing habitat for commercial fish species and maintaining healthy coastal zones. The coast is where a significant portion of the nation's economic output is generated, much of which is directly dependent on coastal and marine resources, especially shrimp and small-scale fisheries.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE B

## Small fisheries: Based on the most detailed national data, there are 28,399 artisanal fishers in Ecuador organized into 154 cooperatives and 67 associations. These fishers depend directly or indirectly on artisanal fishing for their livelihoods. 133,596 people directly or independently depend on artisanal fishing. These fishers have low incomes; the average annual income is US$208 (Herrera et al., 2017). The majority of these fishers earn less than US$400 per year, and 37% have very low incomes less than US$200 (Herrera et al., 2017).

### B.2 Current and projected climate change impacts in the coast of Ecuador

In the continental region of Ecuador, the climate is strongly influenced by the ocean. During the rainy season (June to November), the trade winds are reversed, bringing moisture from the Pacific Ocean (satisfaction of the Panama current). The dry season coincides with the intertropical convergence zone (ITCZ) passing over the country and the arrival of the Humboldt Current. In the continental central region, rainfall exceeds 2,000 mm annually, and air temperatures are between 25°C and 30°C. Further south, in the Andean region, annual precipitation is less than 1,000 mm; average temperatures are between 300 and 1,000 m per year, and the average temperatures are between 24°C and 26°C.

Climate change is projected to create significant changes in local environmental conditions along Ecuador's coast. These changes will include increased sea levels (SLR), increased intensity and variability of precipitation, and atmospheric temperatures, all of which will affect Ecuador's coastal communities.

By 2100, mean sea level is projected to rise an additional 49-84 cm on Ecuador's coast*. An increase in mean sea level will lead to more frequent flooding events (e.g., storm surges, high tides, and extreme tidal flooding), which are expected to occur annually by mid-century in low-lying coastal areas (IPCC, 2013). Increased SLR will also cause saltwater intrusion into freshwater aquifers, leading to salinization of groundwater and consequently impacting rural and urban populations, and aquaculture in coastal areas, especially when combined with increased precipitation.

*El Niño-Southern Oscillation (ENSO) is a phase of a large-scale, recurring climate pattern associated with above average sea surface temperatures in the eastern Pacific Ocean, which contributes to increased sea levels and extreme weather events. The frequency and intensity of ENSO events are expected to increase due to SLR due to seawater expansion (Cai et al., 2014; Wang et al., 2015). During the 1967-97 period, sea surface temperatures in the eastern Pacific Ocean increased by approximately 0.3°C per decade (IPCC, 2007), and the frequency of ENSO events has increased from once every three years to one every two years (Cai et al., 2014). The effect of climate change on future ENSO dynamics is unclear although rainfall variability is very likely to increase over most of Ecuador. This will lead to more frequent extreme weather events, including droughts and floods.

Between 1976-2005, the average annual temperature in Ecuador increased 3% (NAMM). From Ministerio del Ambiente del Ecuador, 2017. Some models project significant increases in annual precipitation over most of Ecuador by mid-century (IPCC, 2013). For example, for the northern coast peninsula by Garcia-Garcillan et al., (2017) documented increasing temperatures (+0.58°C/years) and precipitation (+4.6% per year). In the southern coast, they projected an increase in mean annual temperature (+2.7°C) and precipitation (+4.2%) by 2100. Other analyses indicate that while total annual rainfall is likely to increase over most of Ecuador, there will be a greater number of dry days (less than 5 mm per day), more days per year of extreme rainfall events) and periods of drought expected to increase (Ministerio del Ambiente del Ecuador, 2017).

See Annex 2 for references.

## Climate change impacts on Ecuador

### Climatic changes observed in Ecuador

1. From 1960-2015, average temperature for Ecuador's coast increased by 0.4°C and an increase in atmospheric precipitable water vapor from 100% to 102% over RCP 4.3 and RCP 8.5, respectively.
2. Outside the scope of this project, these temperatures may affect livelihoods from agricultural activities and increase health risks for vulnerable populations (UNDP, 2013). However, this analysis is not intended to assess climate change impacts on fisheries or other purposes but does not directly expect to impact flood risks, which are the climate adaptation focus of this proposal.

### Analyses conducted for this project by the International Research Center on El Niño (CIFEN) indicate that disaster risk reduction measures will be effective in reducing the vulnerability of coastal communities to climate change scenarios RCP4.5 and 8.5.
1. The hydrological potential of the coast of Ecuador would be reduced, showing signs of possible drought conditions which could impact fisheries and associated livelihoods.

### Climatic changes observed in Ecuador

#### Flood risks are a significant concern for Ecuador's coast and in the last 45 years, the coastal provinces have suffered the highest degree of flooding in the country. Guayas has been the most affected, with more than 100 flood events recorded since 1965.

##### Historical El Niño events provide a reference point for the scale of future climate-related damages for Ecuador's coast and its associated livelihoods.
1. The 2016/2017 El Niño event caused $3 billion in economic losses, with 48% attributed to agriculture production. Agricultural crop losses were estimated at US$5.5 million, with more than 2,000 hectares of crops destroyed and 9,000 livestock animals lost.
2. Limited government preparation and response to previous El Niño events, in areas such as land-use planning, management of coastal zones, and disaster risk reduction measures, have resulted in significant economic losses.

#### Extreme El Niño events related precipitation variation on regional scales and increases in sea level will likely intensify El Niño-related impacts (McPhaden et al., 2013). Combined with the expected increase in SLR due to climate change, more frequent and intense megafloods are expected. Wang et al. (2017), McPhaden et al. (2020), but see also El Niño events of 1982-1983 and 1997-1998, have shown that the frequency and intensity of megafloods are compounded by SLR. These conclusions are based on several models that project flood risks under diverse climate change scenarios (see Section B.1 below).

* Mangrove management associations (ASJUCES), with 4,556 registered members, are almost exclusively dedicated to fisheries activities in mangrove areas (Informe del Ambiental de Ecuador, 2020). These management associations cover approximately 43% of Ecuador's coastal area. The remaining 57% is managed by independent fishermen who fish for their own consumption and sell the surplus at local markets. People fishing and gathering shellfish from mangroves is likely more than double the number of registered members in associations (Informe del Ambiental de Ecuador, 2020). This implies that there are many small-scale fisheries operating outside of these associations.

* Mangrove management associations (ASJUCES), with 4,556 registered members, are almost exclusively dedicated to fisheries activities in mangroves and do not include unaffiliated "independent" fishermen who are not part of these associations, implying that the actual number of people fishing for shellfish from mangroves is likely more than double the number of registered members (Informe del Ambiental de Ecuador, 2020). This implies that there are many small-scale fisheries operating outside of these associations.

## Importance of mangroves for livelihoods of Ecuador's coastal populations

15. Mangroves provide a wide range of ecosystem services that contribute to human well-being, including provision of timber and fuelwood, breeding and nursery grounds for fisheries and protection from coastal flooding and erosion, as well as providing habitat for wildlife and recreational opportunities such as bird watching and gathering shellfish. The total population dependent on mangrove fisheries is approximately 42,000 people, most of whom are small-scale fishers who depend directly or indirectly on the ecosystem services provided by mangroves and associated economic activity, which are independently dependent on mangroves' ecological function as critical nursery grounds for many species. The total annual value of mangrove-dependent fisheries in Ecuador is US$120 million and seafood products generated by small-scale fishers is approximately US$200 million per year (Monnier et al., 2018).

## Historical changes to the area of mangrove land cover

17. Mangroves have experienced significant losses in Ecuador, which has resulted in GHG emissions from the loss and degradation of these ecosystems. The main driver for this loss is conversion of mangroves into shrimp aquaculture, with a prominent driver of mangrove loss, with shrimp ponds during 2008-2019 accounting for 51% of mangrove loss (IUCN/WWF, 2020). According to the most recent assessment there were 156,633 ha of mangrove remaining in the four key estuaries of mainland Ecuador.

18. Recent years have seen growth in new mangrove areas replacing other land uses, with a total net gain in mangrove area since 2009 of approximately 47,000 ha. However, this is not enough to offset the loss and degradation of mangroves, which has resulted in significant gross loss of mangroves, totalling an area of 9,903 ha lost during 2008-2019, resulting in the loss of approximately 45% of Ecuador's mangrove cover. The main driver for this loss is conversion of mangroves (mainly for shrimp ponds but also for agriculture and building), which continues to occur due to weak enforcement of laws and regulations by local authorities and national governments. Mangroves are one of the nine forest states included in the Forest Reference Emission Level (FREL) under the REDD+ mechanism.

## Community-based management of mangroves and effectiveness for mangrove conservation and restoration

19. Strengthening community-based management of mangroves has been an important pillar of Ecuador's approach to mangrove conservation for twenty years. Strengthening the capacity of local mangrove users as a strategy for conserving and restoring mangroves has been highlighted by local studies and is an important priority in Ecuador's new climate change strategies (see details).

20. While coastal mangroves are legally public lands, AusCENIE, first established in 2000, grant traditional communities the right to use these areas for their livelihoods and living conditions. These agreements grant use rights and stewardship responsibilities.

* **Mangrove management associations (AusCENIE), with 4,556 registered members, are almost exclusively dedicated to fisheries activities in mangroves and other coastal ecosystems. AusCENIE is a non-profit organization that represents the interests of local communities and does not include unaffiliated 'independent' fishers who are not part of these associations, implying that the actual number of people dependent on mangrove fisheries may be higher than 42,000 (Monnier et al., 2018). AusCENIE is a member of the International Union for Conservation of Nature (IUCN) and the World Wildlife Fund (WWF).**
* **The new more recent 2016 AusCENIE draft law allows for conversion of paya deforestation land use on an additional 13% was converted to other agricultural use, 25% converted to open water (due to natural dynamics of estuarine areas), with the remainder being protected. The main driver for this loss is conversion of mangrove into shrimp ponds and associated aquaculture in Ecuador's coastal regions by ga. farmers and small fishers.**

To organize groups of traditional mangrove users to restrict open access and oversee reduction of illegal and extraction, and increase the ability of local users to realize economic benefits from conservation actions, it is necessary to identify areas where mangroves are under threat and to implement proven effective tools for mangrove protection. Gross deforestation rates under ASCMUSE and PAs are 86% (totaling 36,186 ha or 23% of total mangrove area) account for 60% of all recent mangrove deforestation. The country has identified 54,790 ha as ASCMUSE and 10,217 ha as PAs (see Annexes A and B). These areas currently only cover 60,217 ha of mangroves (Figure 3 and Table 2), this means that significant mangrove areas and their associated biodiversity are still under threat from deforestation, illegal logging, and uncontrolled resource exploitation.

### 2.1. In addition, approximately one quarter of the areas under ASCMUSE have lapsed due to lack of support and technical assistance for mangrove protection and management, leaving these mangrove areas without effective protection. Furthermore, despite most mangrove residents being highly dependent on natural resources for their livelihoods, there is a lack of alternative income generating activities in mangrove areas and strengthening of livelihoods alternatives in mangrove areas can therefore contribute to enhancing climate change resilience.

A sub-fund of the existing Socio Manglar Fund has been established in preparation for this project and as a mechanism for developing the long-term financial sustainability of the Socio Manglar Program. The Socio Manglar Fund will be structured as an independent fund with its own separate account, endowment, with the interest and investment income from its investments used to pay for incentives to communities participating in the Socio Manglar program. The Socio Manglar Fund will have a separate SCF Manglar subaccount. The SCF funds will be kept separately from other Socio Manglar subaccount funds in an unencumbered account, with the interest and investment income from its investments used to pay for the investment strategy of the fund manager, Fondo de Inversión Ambiental Sostenible (FIAS), which is funded by the World Bank. The SCF will also be able to receive contributions to the Socio Manglar subaccount from private sector companies operating in Ecuador as part of project activities in Community Forests.

25. While there have not yet been evaluations of the Socio Manglar program, due to its recent establishment, several communities have already reported that they are experiencing positive impacts on their livelihoods and that communities gain important monetary and non-monetary benefits (Arriaga et al., 2018; Peralta and Pabón, 2019). These benefits include increased income from the sale of mangrove products, improved food security, economic development, and organizational support. Communities also reported non-monetary benefits due to cultural and spiritual values associated with the mangroves (Arriaga et al., 2018; Peralta and Pabón, 2019). The evaluations noted the need to strengthen financial sustainability of the program and the need to address conflicts between communities and private sector companies operating in the area. These are important issues that will be addressed as part of this project.

In the case of mangrove areas, there is a stronger possibility to monetize the traditional economies because the economic value of non-timber products (i.e., from fisheries) is much higher than for terrestrial forests.

### Mangrove restoration and reforestation

Ecuador has established and executed successful mangrove reforestation efforts. Technical and mangrove reforestation guidelines are available in the document "Mangrove Reforestation" (Annex 2: Feasibility Study). An effective policy intervention that led to an increase in mangrove cover was Executive Decree 1391 of 2007, which established a three-year grace period for landowners who had not replanted significant areas of mangroves on or adjacent to their farms, and allowed a three-year grace period for replanting. This policy intervention led to an increase in the number of hectares planted with mangroves in mangrove restoration (totaling 3,110 ha) immediately following its issue. Unfortunately, since the policy was issued, there has been no further progress on this front.

### International Cooperation

- Conservation International (CI) has identified over 10,000 ha of land in the four estuaries appropriate for potential reforestation based on criteria including size, form, connection with remaining mangroves, and vulnerability to climate change. CI is currently working with Ecuadorian authorities (see Annex 2 for further details of the prioritization and restoration methods) to provide long-term flood protection benefits, carbon sequestration, and ecosystem service co-benefits in the form of improved livelihoods for vulnerable people.

## B.1.4 Governance

### Planning and zoning

Local governments are known as Decentralized Autonomous Governments (DAGs) in Ecuador and include subnational authorities such as provinces, cantons, and parishes. DAGs have a dual role as both executive and regulatory autonomy. DAGs need to play an important role in climate governance and, in coordination with the National Government, must ensure that all land use planning is consistent with national policies and investments of the GCF are Development and Land Use Plans (Plan de Desarrollo y Ordenamiento Territorial - PDOOT). PDOOTs are developed by DAGs as part of their territorial development plans. The PDOOT is a legally binding document, which must be approved by the National Government before it can be implemented.

### Enforcement

Legal enforcement has faced a series of setbacks when trying to enforce mangrove conservation measures with shrimp farm owners who expanded shrimp farms without prior authorization from the National

**Environmental Authority:** Although regulatory frameworks are in place, enforcement is inadequate for protection and restoration of mangroves by public, private and community-based actors.

## 33. Operational Control and Surveillance Units (UCOVs) - Unidades Operativas de Contra el Vigilancia y Sanción piloted for environmental crimes in Ecuador as part of the Program for the Management of Coastal Resources project

As a joint effort between the Navy, MAATE, Vice-ministry of Aquaculture, and the Environmental Authority, the UCOVs were created to enforce laws and regulations. Their goal was to avoid and control the clear-cutting of mangrove forest areas through the cooperation of public, private, and community-based actors.

## 34. The application of sanctions for environmental crimes under the Organic Code for Criminal Procedures (Código Orgánico de Procedimientos Penales) is still in its early stages

The lack of enforcement is due to a number of factors: procedural delays, unclear procedures and fines. Why? While environmental crimes and sanctions are taken up by the courts, there is frequently a delay between the time that an offense occurs and when it comes before the courts. There are many coastal laws and regulations, and a lack of coordination and collaboration between prosecutors and MAATE.

### B.1.5 Private sector initiatives: Shrimp aquaculture

#### 35. Shrimp farming has historically been the single biggest driver of mangrove deforestation in Ecuador and continues to be so today. In the last five years alone, since the Bosque Seco law (2010), there were 3,932 registered shrimp farms in Ecuador operating on 281,600 ha (Seafish Watch, 2021). Effectively engaging with the shrimp aquaculture sector is therefore critical to achieving the goals of the Green Climate Fund project.

#### 36. Shrimp farming has historically been associated with low stocking density production (intensive production) to decrease disease risks, although the sector remains GHG intensive. The low stocking density strategy widely adopted in Ecuador results in a high mortality rate and poor quality product with high environmental impacts. Overall growth of the sector has increased from about 50,000 t/a in 2013 to over 80,000 t/a in 2019 (ECA Consulting, 2016). The stocking density is currently around 40 kg/m² which is significantly lower than intensive production seen in Thailand (16.2 kg/m²) and below production intensities found in China, India, and Indonesia (4.5 kg/m²; 3.6 kg/m²; and 3.3 kg/m² respectively) (ECA Consulting, 2018).

#### Opportunities for engagement with the private sector

The shrimp aquaculture industry has had limited engagement with mangrove conservation, despite the role of mangroves in reducing disease risks through natural filtration, regulatory requirements for reforestation, and increasing market demand for sustainable production. Increasing private-sector participation is constrained by a number of factors: lack of access to credit; high costs associated with implementing best practices that restore or conserve mangroves, and in the case of small shrimp farmers, limited access to affordable inputs such as feed and disease control products. The 2019 Ecuadorian Government decree to restore mangroves following Decree 1391 were generally successful and survival rates of mangrove seedlings have been high (ECA Consulting, 2018). However, the current capacity for private sector investment in restoration is limited because there is capacity to export shrimp exceeds demand.

#### Beyond certification: Shrimp aquaculture and sustainable production

Shrimp farmers are also driving a shift within Ecuador's shrimp aquaculture sector. Voluntary, market-facing certification and labeling schemes are increasing demand for sustainably produced shrimp (ECA Consulting, 2018). Depending on the retailer, certification is seen as: i) a requirement for sourcing (without additional pricing); or ii) as an opportunity to charge more for premium products. The private sector is increasingly recognizing their responsibility and are willing to pay more for one on a kilogram basis. While there has been growing interest in sustainable shrimp production, the majority of Ecuadorian shrimp farmers continue to operate under certification schemes that align with climate resilience and emissions reductions. Several companies have committed to achieving sustainability through third-party certification programs including Aquaculture Stewardship Council (ASC) certification and the Sustainable Shrimp Partnership (SSP).

#### ASC provides a certification standard to minimize unintended social and environmental impacts of aquaculture production, while also providing market access for certified products. The ASC standard is implemented in several ways, such as the partial restorion of lost mangrove forest; the development and implementation of best practices that reduce disease risks through natural filtration; and the creation and maintenance of habitats. Furthermore, since shrimp farming often occurs along coastal areas, a permanent coastal buffer must be in place to protect against erosion and sedimentation. In 2019, approximately 35% of Ecuadorian shrimp farms were under ASC; approximately 15% of production is certified, with a total volume including shrimp and tilapia of

## B

### 40. The ASC has also been working on the development and implementation of the national SPP initiative to create a brand to differentiate the Ecuadorian product in the global seafood market.

The SPS is an industry-led initiative that aims to improve shrimp production practices, increase farm productivity, and enhance environmental sustainability. With approximately 3,000 shrimp farms in Ecuador, there is significant scope for growth with both ASC and SFP requirements. The ASC SPS will focus on improving water quality, reducing nutrient runoff, increasing biodiversity and mangrove restoration and protection; with their requirements to eliminate mangrove deforestation, the ASC and SFP are complementary.

### 41. CSS is a climate-smart shrimp farming model that provides a model for climate change adaptation within the sector. CSS is complementary to the ASC and SFP standards and is a novel set of practices that prevent deforestation and improve water quality. The CSS model involves working with shrimp farm owners to intensify shrimp production, construct treatment systems, and provide technical assistance to small-scale farmers. For large-scale shrimp producers and medium farmers, the primary incentives to participate are access to capital and technical assistance, while for large-scale shrimp producers, the main incentive is to increase profitability.

### 42. Establishing the shrimp industry as a partner in mangrove restoration will increase funding for restoration and improve water quality. The CSS model can be used by shrimp farmers to restore degraded mangroves on a larger scale and eliminate pressures for deforestation. The project includes activities with Ecuadorian shrimp producers, including technical assistance, capacity building, and financial contributions for the Socio Manglar Program that will support community-based mangrove conservation and management beyond the implementation period of the project.

### B.1 The impacts of coastal flooding events in the project area

Based on review of local data sets and a series of models that were applied to Ecuador's coast (see Appendix D of Annex A for details), it is estimated that:

- In coastal mangrove areas, approximately 198,000 people are exposed annually to high flood risks from either coastal or riparian flooding (SINGE, 2015). Of those, approximately 22,400 are estimated to have an annual risk of flooding greater than 30% and would be considered at risk under the methodology of Menéndez et al. (2020), see below and Annex 25 for more details).
- Within the project area, approximately 168,000 people are exposed annually to high flood risks from either coastal or riparian flooding (SINGE, 2015). Of those, approximately 17,400 people would have an annual risk of flooding greater than 30% due to coastal storm surge events (based on the methodology of Menéndez et al. 2020), see below and Annex 25 for more details).
- Without the project intervention, it is estimated that approximately 168,000 people would be at an annual risk of flooding greater than 30% due to coastal storm surge events (based on the methodology of Menéndez et al. 2020), see below and Annex 25 for more details). The project will therefore provide improved flood protection in the project area, as it will strengthen the protection of existing mangroves and therefore ensure that the flood protection continues.

## The impacts of flooding events on local communities and key economic sectors:

### 48. The coastal zone in the project area is identified as having a high flood hazard level, by the Global Facility for Disaster Reduction and Recovery (GFDRR), meaning that potentially damaging waves are expected to reach the coast during major storms. This region has been identified as the location with the highest coastal flooding risk within the project area.

### 49. Flooding events have become more frequent over the last 30 years, and are particularly acute during El Niño events (e.g., 1982–1983, 1997–1998). The 1997-98 El Niño event, flooding, disruption of supply chains and damage to transport infrastructure affected the shrimp sector in the project area. In addition, the shrimp aquaculture sector is particularly vulnerable to climate change impacts such as sea level rise, increased storm frequency and intensity.

The shrimp aquaculture sector is highly dependent on mangroves for nursery grounds and food resources. Shrimp are also sensitive to changes in water temperature, salinity balance, population density, precipitation change, flood, drought and cyclone risk, adaptive capacity and access to markets.

## B Importance of mangroves in providing coastal protection benefits against flooding and climate change

### B.1.7 Importance of mangroves in providing coastal protection benefits against flooding and climate change

51. While mangroves provide a diversity of ecosystem services, their value for coastal protection and the reduction of flood risks is particularly well-documented and relevant for Ecuador's coast. The use of "green infrastructure" at the coast has been widely recognized as an effective way to reduce wave energy and reduce the intensity of storm surges, has been well documented (see additional detail in Annex 2). Mangroves are able to absorb large amounts of wave energy due to their ability to dissipate wave height as the waves travel through the mangroves. Several studies highlight the importance of having wide tracts of mangroves to reduce storm surge and flooding risk.

52. To model the flood protection benefits of mangroves, we applied the "avoided damage" valuation approach described in Appendix D (Annex 2). The avoided damage analysis using the Menendez method does not include the value of existing mangrove cover but provides reasonable estimates for the value of mangrove cover if it is removed. This has been used in this preparation of the project to estimate the benefits of increasing mangrove cover.

53. The Menendez model analysis concludes that based on current conditions, 22,400 people have an annual expected risk of coastal flooding caused by storm surge (18,900 in urban areas and 5,500 in rural areas). Under these current climate conditions, but without the protections that mangroves provide, approximately 36,200 more people would be at risk. The Menendez model analysis also concludes that the protection provided by mangroves prevent approximately US$1250 million of property loss from flooding annually. Based upon mangrove area and its location in relation to urban areas, it is estimated that the mangroves provide an average of approximately US$1,600 of property loss from coastal flooding every year in Ecuador. Property loss in this case includes damage to buildings, infrastructure, and other assets as well as the cost associated with evacuating people at flood depth. Based on these figures, approximately one person has reduced annual expected flood risk for each two hectares of mangroves. This means that mangroves are more effective at reducing flood risk for those at risk and therefore concentrating mangrove protection and restoration efforts between the sea and urban areas where they most impact. This is particularly the case in the Gulf of Guayaquil, where most urban inhabitants reside.

Or: more precisely 1 hectare of mangroves provides protection for 0.58 people on average.

## Summary of Climate Change Hazards and Risk/Impacts

### Hazard: 1. Increasing in Floods

Climate change will expose more people to frequent flooding from both riparian floods due to high rainfall and coastal swells associated with storms. The frequency of coastal swells will increase as sea level rises (see Annex B) and extreme ENSO events (see Hazard 3 below) exacerbate the impacts of storms.

### Risk/Impact: In coastal mangrove areas, approximately 198,000 people are currently exposed annually to high flood-related economic losses. This is expected to increase by up to 25% in 2050 and 40% in 2070 (see Annex B).

By ensuring that current mangrove areas are conserved, deforestation due to human pressures (mostly shrimp farming) is reduced by 50% and restoration expanding mangrove coverage by 4.55 ha, the number of people exposed annually to high flood-related economic losses will be reduced from approximately 198,000 in 2017 to around 63,000 in 2050 (see Annex B).

### Hallegatte et al. (2013) estimated the relative risk of vulnerability to flooding (economic average annual losses/sector's GDP) for 138 coastal port cities, by assessing city-level flood risk and a database of urban land use. The number of coastal port cities in Ecuador with high flood risk is 45 out of 138. Of these, mangroves included in the project is ranked number four globally for flood risk.

When future socio-economic hazards are considered (see Hazard 2 below), under the assumption of adaptation measures by 2050, the increase in the economic average losses expected between 2017 and 2050 due to coastal flooding will be reduced from approximately $3.4 billion to around $2.6 billion.

### Ecuador's shrimp production has been highlighted as having the highest vulnerability to climate change of all sectors (see Hazard 2 below). The main factors contributing to this are: temperature, water balance, population density, precipitation changes, flood risk, drought and risk of disease. According to Sánchez (2016), shrimp farming in Ecuador is at high flood risk under current conditions.

### The shrimp aquaculture sector in Ecuador is located on the coast where 95% of shrimp farms are situated in areas classified as high flood risk under current conditions. In addition, the transport infrastructure affecting the shrimp aquaculture sector and around 4,500 ha of ponds were identified as being at high flood risk (see Hazard 2 below). The projected increase in sea level and intensified precipitation due to climate change will have a significant impact on this sector.

### This damage could result in up to a 20% drop in yields per pond (Sánchez, 2016). These damages are expected to be particularly severe for shrimp farms located in the province of Los Ríos (e.g., Joshua's shrimp farm) in shrimp aquaculture farming and processing (Yahya Piedrahita, 2018).

### Assessing the impact that is the effects on natural and human systems of extreme weather and climate change events. Impacts generally refer to changes in the state or condition of a system as a result of exposure to an event. The vulnerability of a system refers to its susceptibility to climate change or hazardous climate change events occurring within a specific time period.

### Click Labs data estimates the presence of 1,571,298 ha of aquaculture ponds in Ecuador in 1998 (source: http://www.clicklabs.org/).

### Other estimates for the shrimp industry reference 100,000 - 220,000 directly or indirectly employed.

# Hazard: El Niño events

El Niño events have already led to observed ocean warming and dramatic rise in sea level due to seawater expansion (Cai et al., 2014; Wang et al., 2019). During the 1976-78 event, sea surface temperatures increased by up to 3.5°C over a large area of the Pacific Ocean and caused sea levels to rise by about 40 cm (Cai, 2000). In the equatorial Pacific region, sea levels can deviate from the global mean level by as much as ±1 m during El Niño events.

ENSO is a natural climatic driver, and it is very likely that variability-related effects of ENSO will be amplified in future climate scenarios (PCC, 2021). There are indications that extreme El Niño events may increase in frequency and intensity under future climate change scenarios (PCC, 2021). These extreme events have become more frequent and more intense (Magañ et al., 2007; Wang et al., 2019), but even an El Niño of moderate strength can cause significant impacts. This will affect larger areas and more people as its flooding impacts are compounded by sea-level rise.

# Risk: Historical El Niño events provide a reference point for the scale of future climate-related damages for coastal communities in Ecuador

The 1982-83 El Niño event was one of the strongest on record, with over 506,000 people displaced (DFID, 2007). Flooding events were particularly severe during this event and caused damage to infrastructure. In total, 400,000 houses were destroyed or damaged in Ecuador, and an estimated $1 billion was lost due to the flooding. The 1982-83 El Niño event also resulted in over 222,000 flood deaths (Olea Pedraza, 2015). Flooding events registered since 1965 on the coast of Ecuador have caused significant damage and loss of life. For example, during the 1983 El Niño event, with 67% of those occurring during El Niño years. During those flooding events, 97,031 people were displaced (DFID, 2007). The 1997-98 El Niño event was one of the most severe in terms of its impacts on coastal communities in Ecuador. Of these flooding events led to over USD $5 billion in damages and losses (Olea Pedraza, 2015). In total, 34,600 people were displaced or died during this event. The 1997-98 El Niño event also occurred in EM-GAT. Furthermore, one quarter of the total coastal population was exposed to increased flooding risk due to sea-level rise and land subsidence (Olea Pedraza et al., 2015). These are associated with floods and damages to sanitary infrastructure caused by the 1997-98 El Niño event (Vio et al., 1999).

B (a). Theory of change narrative and diagram (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagram)

Current Conditions and Trends

- **55.** Current trends in levels of emissions and vulnerability are described in greater detail in section B1 and Annex B2, but key points for the Theory of Change are summarized here for clarity.
    - Sea level rise (≈12 cm by 2040 and 49–64 cm by 2100) will increase impacts of El Niño events, augmented interannual variability in sea levels, and extreme weather events. This will lead to more frequent, intense, and damaging coastal flooding.
    - Flood impacts are concentrated in recurring El Niño events and other extreme sea level events. SLR will magnify these effects.

- **56.** In addition to maintaining biodiversity and many ecosystem services, mangroves play a dual role, both in reducing climate change vulnerability of coastal communities to flooding events, and in increasing carbon storage capacity. The project aims to protect 102,990 ha of primary mangrove forests that are still lost each year. Areas under community management and PAs are gaining momentum as the main drivers for mangrove conservation.

- **57.** Nearly 96% of all mangroves areas are concentrated in a subset of eight municipalities providing important food protection for people and assets. With measures to protect and restore mangroves, the current flood protection benefits will be doubled by 2030 (see Annex B1). The project is also expected to provide more resilient livelihoods for 41,500 climate-smart households in coastal communities, with an additional 98,600 direct beneficiaries and therefore, to avoid double counting, estimates the overall number of direct beneficiaries at 137,200.

## MANGROVE ECOSYSTEM

### Figure 7: Carbon storage in healthy and degraded mangroves showing that carbon is sequestered in both woody biomass and into the soil.

**Barriers:**

1. Accelerating the rate of mangrove recovery at a pace commensurate with growing climate risk, after decades of deforestation, requires a coordinated multi-pronged approach involving diverse instruments and stakeholders to overcome key barriers.
2. Barriers 1 & 3: Lack of economic incentives and economic alternatives for effective mangrove conservation and management, potentially hindering their engagement.
3. Barriers 2 & 4: Gaps in coverage, legal recognition, and organizational capacity to ensure that proven community-based mangrove management models are effectively implemented. Community-based mangrove management and PA-based management have proven effective tools for mangrove protection; however, their coverage is limited. For example, only 10% of the mangroves in Ecuador's coastal zone are under some form of legal protection, with less than half of these areas between 2008 and 2018. Furthermore, many AUSCSEMS are constrained by limited management capacity and insufficient resources to effectively implement mangrove conservation and management.
4. Barriers 5 & 6: Insufficient incentives and investments for practices by communities and businesses that enhance resilience against climate change. The DGF exists because nations recognized this barrier to climate change resilience and mitigation. Other programs that will also address this barrier in Ecuador's mangroves are still quite young (e.g., the Socio Mangrove Program). These programs aim to provide incentives, financing mechanisms, and capacity building for communities and businesses to enhance their ability to adapt and mitigate climate change.
5. Barriers 7 & 8: Although some local financial institutions cater for businesses, high transaction costs and lack of awareness about available funding options are barriers to accessing finance. Financial models are often novel and perceived as higher risk. There are very few opportunities for early-stage community-based mangrove conservation projects to access finance.
6. Barriers 9 & 10: Insufficient long-term stable financing to support community mangrove conservation efforts. Activities in Components 1 and 2 address this barrier.

## Outcomes and the project contribution.

### Outcome 1: The area of mangroves under effective climate-adapted management is increased.
The project will expand the mangrove areas under effective community-based management, support adaptation planning in existing PAs and new PA proposals, and improve application of existing regulations on mangroves. The project targets 158,633 ha of mangroves under effective climate-adapted management.

### Outcome 2: Flood risks associated with climate change are reduced by expanding mangrove areas under effective climate-adapted management.
The project will reduce flood risk for vulnerable people and communities through reduced deforestation and restoration activities. The project targets a reduction in flood risk for over 10,000 households.

### Outcome 3: GHG emissions from deforestation are reduced and carbon sequenced by expanding mangrove areas under effective climate-adapted management.
The project will reduce GHG emissions from deforestation through reduced deforestation and restoration activities. The project targets an estimated net reduction of 732,100 tCO2e over the project implementation period.

### Outcome 4: Institutional framework for mangrove protection and coastal planning is strengthened.
Project activities will result in improved regulatory systems or incentives for climate resilience and their effective implementation. This will be achieved through capacity building with national authorities and local communities, as well as engagement of civil society groups.

#### Co-benefit 1: Biodiversity and ecosystem service benefits are increased.
The increased forest cover from reduced deforestation and restoration will provide additional habitat for biodiversity (5,725 ha) and increase the area available to support fisheries and nursery areas. The project targets an increase in the number of species recorded per hectare of ecosystem co-benefits (due to the complexity and methodological challenges involved), global estimates of the economic value of mangroves as a habitat for biodiversity (see Annex 2 for values for fisheries habitats) according to a record of 250 attempts at valuing the ecosystem services of mangroves (Geltner et al., 2016), and the importance of mangrove habitat. See also D3 and Annex 3 for further discussion of ecosystem service value.

#### Co-benefit 2: Economic resilience of coastal communities is increased.
Project activities to secure mangrove protection will result in economic benefits for local communities, including improved livelihoods through small-scale community businesses that will result in increased economic resilience for 41,500 people from coastal communities.

#### Co-benefit 3: Uptake of sustainable shrimp production practices is increased.
The project will contribute to international and national initiatives to increase sustainability of shrimp production practices and to increase adoption of best management practices (BMPs) by small-scale shrimp farmers. The project targets an increase in the number of commitments to no deforestation covering at least 20,000 ha of shrimp farms.

#### Co-benefit 4: Economic value of fisheries is increased.
The increased forest cover from reduced deforestation and restoration (5,725 ha) will provide additional habitat for fisheries areas and nurseries. The project targets an increase in the number of species recorded per hectare of ecosystem co-benefits (due to the complexity and methodological challenges involved), global estimates of the economic value of mangroves as a habitat for biodiversity (see Annex 2 for values for fisheries habitats) according to a record of 250 attempts at valuing the ecosystem services of mangroves.

Activities will include strengthening community-based management (AUSCENM) and PAI implementing adaptation climate action plans.

The project's theory of change also relies on the following assumptions (all are important but the most important have been highlighted):

* Community communities feel sufficiently incentivized by project activities to engage in mangrove conservation and restoration.
* When local communities have strong rights over mangroves, they are more likely to implement sustainable practices.
* Economic empowerment associations with the necessary knowledge and skills for mangrove management and restoration will enhance their ability to implement effective conservation practices.
* There are sufficient business opportunities for mangrove community associations that can be developed through project activities.
* Exposure of private sector actors to market drivers and climate risks motivates increased investment in sustainable aquaculture practices.
* Commercial bank lending rates remain at levels that would not discourage investment in sustainable aquaculture practices.
* Local communities will make use of improved data availability in their decision making and land-use planning.
* Government agencies will have at least their current resources and capacities to support the project.
* There is political will to strengthen the application of legal and regulatory frameworks during and after project implementation.

B.3.1 Overview

74. Mangroves are among the planet’s most important habitats for carbon sequestration and Ecuador’s mangroves have been identified as important stocks of irreplaceable carbon, which once lost cannot be recovered before 2050 when the world must reach net-zero emissions to avoid the worst impacts of climate change. In addition to their role in mitigating climate change, mangroves provide a wide range of ecosystem services that support livelihoods, particularly due to their importance in natural fisheries, making their conservation and restoration critical for sustainable development.

As described in section B1, loss of Ecuador’s mangroves has slowed in the last decade, but unprotected areas continue to lose mangrove annually. This project will transform the situation by consolidating the recent gains on a country-wide scale through an integrated approach that combines policy reform with implementation by catalyzing partnerships between community, private and public sector actors, and long-term incentives to protect and restore mangroves.

76. Reducing gross deforestation rates of mangroves by 250 ha per year (20% of man-made deforestation) and restoring 4,850 ha of mangroves are the principle direct strategies for generating food-protection benefits for 39,000 people in coastal communities, achieving net emissions reductions and sequestration of 14.4 Mt CO2e per year, and providing livelihoods to 67,000 people through improved shrimp production plans and policy instruments for 3.4 million people.

The project will focus on a combination of measures relating to reducing mangrove deforestation and increasing information sharing and mangrove conservation approaches, increased private-sector engagement and improved government agency and public-sector capacity centered on three main Components and six specific activities. The overall approach is summarized below, with additional detail provided in Section 5.4.3 of Annex 2 and in Section E.6 of this document.

Component 1 focuses on actions to increase both the area of mangrove under protection by local community stakeholders, their economic sustainability and the quality and effectiveness of management for these areas, as well as increasing the resilience of communities dependent upon them.

Component 2 focuses on engaging the private sector, particularly shrimp aquaculture, to become a transformational agent for change, reversing its previous role as an agent of mangrove loss. This component will promote the integration of sustainable shrimp production practices into shrimp farms and other coastal areas affected by deforestation as well as catalyze new sources of financing for the long-term sustainability of community-led mangrove conservation.

Component 3 focuses on creating the enabling conditions, through improved governance and the generation of timely, targeted information, which are the scaffolding supporting mangrove conservation, planning, regulation and business practices.


77. Each component addresses critical barriers; none is sufficient alone to produce the desired climate outcomes of maintaining mangroves to reduce vulnerability, especially in food, and to reduce emissions. Local action by communities and private sector actors will be supported through improved governance and strengthened relationships with the private sector. For the private sector, realizing sustainable growth opportunities and contributing to national goals for reducing deforestation and increasing carbon sequestration requires regulation, its application, as well as supportive government action to position the country’s shrimp sector as a leader in sustainability.

B.3.2 Outcome** contribution to CGI Integrated Results Management Framework (RMF)**

780,000 tCO2e over the 7-year project implementation period

## B

### Implementation of mitigation and adaptation strategies in GCF.919 ha of mangroves located in PkA.

#### Activity 1.1.2. Implementation of mitigation and adaptation strategies in GCF.919 ha of mangroves located in PkA.

**Currently, PkA does not have enough human and financial resources to effectively implement conservation and restoration activities on its own.**

As a result of the findings from the Feasibility Study, well-managed mangrove PAs have lower deforestation and therefore GHG emissions than unmanaged mangroves. The proposed project will focus on implementing mitigation strategies in the areas and increase new carbon sequestration through active restoration activities and natural mangrove recovery.

**The project will also implement adaptation strategies to ensure that they are managing the PAs consistent with promoting ecosystem resilience. For example, one key adaptation strategy is to improve water quality in the estuaries where the mangroves grow. This could require management action in surrounding habitats. PkA usually don't include adequate adaptation practices in their restoration plans, so working to improve the planning and implementation of climate adaptation action is important.**

**There is only one association in Jambat Estuary that has only women as active members; the others are mainly formed by men.**

## GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 15

### B

**99.** Co-financing will work with MAATE staff (paid with MAATE in-kind co-financing) in four PAIs: Manglares Cayapas Matas, Manglarine Churute, Manglares El Salado and Manglares Estuarinos del Rio Muniene – which is the largest mangrove forest in Ecuador. These PAIs are located on the coast of the Pacific Ocean and have the highest potential for further reductions in deforestation. CI, PUCSE and MAATE staff will work to integrate climate change adaptation into management plans and activities.

In Year 1, workshops will be organized by CI to identify the conservation objectives and the specific climate change impacts that need to be addressed within each PAI. These workshops will result in a draft management plan, that will include recommendations for climate resilience and adaptation and identify distinct areas of mangrove forest where climate change is expected to have significant negative effects. This is intended to be integrated into updated PAI management plans or their next update (typically management plans are updated every 5 years). CI will also organize workshops in Year 2, which will focus on the implementation process. During Years 2 and 3, two-yearly training events will be developed in each of the PAs to make PAI managers aware of climate change impacts and how to explicitly incorporate climate change adaptation into PAI management plans and objectives.

**100.** Management of PAIs to reduce deforestation will also be strengthened by improving staff training and providing equipment and resources for monitoring and enforcement activities, including the use of new technologies such as data loggers, gear for underwater surveys, software and computers to ensure monitoring in line with the climate change adaptation plan. These improvements are expected to be implemented and maintained by PAI staff, with resources from the national environmental fund (FIS) covering costs of operation and maintenance.

**101.** Project impacts and learning at the four PAs will be amplified through the national network of Parks (Red de Areas Marinas y Costeras Protegidas del Ecuador). PAIs and PA networks need to be adaptively designed and managed in order to respond effectively to climate change, which is expected to have significant effects on mangroves. These effects may affect boundary locations, sizes. Networks help reduce risk and promote resilience. A network analysis will be conducted by CI to identify the most effective activity, all of which are closely with the University of Natural Heritage and the provincial offices of MAATE.

In subsequent years, knowledge on the design, implementation and monitoring procedures will be presented to the PAI staff in workshops organized by CI. In Year 4 of the project, CI will work with the network to produce and distribute guidelines on how to integrate climate change into management plans and organizational strategies. The national network is critical for building capacity within the network as it maintains climate change resilience and rebuilding ecological and social resilience.

**Output 1.2:** Improved livelihood activities and more economically productive community businesses enable local people to respond effectively to climate change and incentives to participate in, and maintain, mangrove conservation and restoration.

### Activity 1.2.1: Technical and business development support to mangrove community associations, with an emphasis on market linkages

By supporting mangrove-dependent communities through groups formally linked to mangrove conservation, ongoing conservation and stewardship can be made economically viable, with improved incomes reinventing communities as stewards of the environment rather than destroyers. This will also increase the capacity of community enterprises, the value of mangroves to the communities is also increased and this creates incentives for more effective management. CI, PUCSE and MAATE will provide support to develop business plans that are resilient to climate change impacts and shocks and provides more opportunities to adopt adaptation measures. CI and PUCSE will provide support to develop business plans that are resilient to climate change impacts and shocks and provides more opportunities to adopt adaptation measures.

The project will also provide technical assistance in the development of new products, marketing strategies, and strategies including strategies for improving enterprise governance and administration, access to finance and capacity building. The project will also provide training on market linkages and business planning with capacity provided through specialist consultants. The easy-based CI sustainable production special and the use of a value chain approach will be used in the development of new products that are more resilient and adding value to mangrove-dependent fisheries products. Product quality and food safety will be enhanced by monitoring supply chains, classification and selection of product sizes and classes according to market requirements.

**104.** Other community economic activities not directly related to seafood products will also be supported to enhance revenue generation from these communities. CI, PUCSE and MAATE will provide technical support to CI, market analysis and study visits to other initiatives for one-to-one training.

### Activity 1.2.2: Establish and consolidate financial mechanisms in support of mangrove community associations (income and livelihoods)

#### 105. Activity 1.2.2 is designed as a follow-on to support early-stage businesses (other new ones created in Activity 1.2.1 or existing ones).

**Ci will award small cash grants on the competitive basis for projects originating with the associations eligible under Activity 1.2.1, and the progress of the project and supported under Activity 1.2.1. Various organizations with legal status in the provinces of Elmerides, Manabi, Guayas and C. Oro will participate, including both AUCD/FAO and CI.**

**The grants will be awarded to micro-enterprises, small enterprises, and small-scale enterprises by helping them to start their operations with the aim of improving the income of the beneficiaries and increasing the economic activity in the communities where they are located through the sustainable use of mangrove environmental goods and services. To be eligible for the grants, the enterprises will need to have a business plan that includes the proposed activities, the expected benefits for the community, and the business. These enterprises are artisanal fishing production associations or cooperatives that report to the beneficiary association. The micro-enterprises must employ at least 5 people with an annual income of less than US$10,000 and between 1-5 workers.**

**This grant mechanism, totaling US$500,000, will be awarded by CI and will provide an estimated 20-30 cash grants to micro-enterprises (US$20,000 per micro-enterprise) for the purchase of equipment or tools in processing equipment, operating costs and other needs to scale-up enterprises. Selection criteria for the grants have been developed with the support of CI and will be used by the beneficiary associations to select their representatives from MAATE, associations and partner organizations of the project. CI will enter into grant agreements with each selected micro-enterprise.**

**To access credit and investment from existing private and public financial institutions, CI will support associations to develop and market analysis to increase the degree of formality of the mangrove community business enterprises. This information will be used by the associations to request loans or other forms of financing (see conversion index). This information and technical support will be used to provide assistance in the identification of potential investors and financial institutions that can provide credit, investment, and/or guarantees for this project. The associations will also have access to a list of private sector entities (banks, microfinance institutions) and other public financial entities that provide credit and investment to micro- and small enterprises.**

**109. Ci will hire a Biocenterpreneur Specialist with expertise in small enterprise development to provide the technical support described in activity 1.2.2.**

#### Component 2: The private sector becomes a transformational agent for change by reducing GHG emissions and providing financial support to conserve and restore mangroves that increase climate resilience for other coastal populations.

**110. Engagement with the private sector in the project consists of promoting and enabling the adoption of improved shrimp farming practices (not the focus of this project) and advancing mangrove conservation and restoration through partnerships with private companies. The project will support the development of a private sector commitment to a no-deforestation requirement and to promote CDS production methods that include mangrove restoration (20% of the area of land used for shrimp farms). This will be achieved by providing technical assistance to shrimp farms to access finance private.**

**115. In preparation for the project, the AEC and CI will provide seed funding (US$300,000 and US$600,000, respectively) to the 200 Mangrove Manager subunits of Ecuador's Socio Bosque Program. This funding will be available to support the Socio Mangroves program during project implementation. Additionally, the AEC and CI will request that the Government of Ecuador agree to provide an additional US$350,000 in funding for the 200 Mangrove Manager subunits. These funds will be used by the beneficiary association to pay for the cost of CDS contribution to the Socio Bosque Program. The AEC and CI will also request that the Government of Ecuador bear the cost of FGDs to contribute to the Socio Bosque Program. ## Project 2.1: Shrimp aquaculture farms adopt practices and production standards that require elimination of deforestation and active restoration in coastal and mangrove areas.

### Activity 2.1.1: Technical assistance for development and promotion of climate-smart shrimp aquaculture practices (2023-2025)

The project will:

* **Reduce deforestation on current shrimp farms** by building farm resilience through the adoption of improved management and production practices.
* **Improve water quality** using biosecurity protocols, water quality management systems, and improved hatchery practices to reduce disease transmission and improve water quality sensors.
* **Increase profitability per hectare** by producing more shrimp per hectare. This enables farms to increase their profitability and output.

### Activity 2.1.1: Financial capital improvements (2023-2025)

The project will:

* **Develop a business plan for each farm** that includes financial projections, cost estimates, and revenue forecasts.
* **Identify potential sources of financing** such as microfinance institutions, government programs, and private sector investors.

### Activity 2.1.1: Policy and alignment (2023-2025)

The project will:

* **Develop a policy framework for shrimp aquaculture in Ecuador**, including regulations on land use, water quality management, and environmental protection.
* **Identify key stakeholders** such as government agencies, private sector investors, and local communities to ensure alignment with national policies.

### Activity 2.1.1: Technical assistance (2023-2025)

The project will:

* **Develop a technical assistance plan for shrimp aquaculture farms**, including training programs, capacity building workshops, and on-farm demonstrations.
* **Disseminate best management practices** through field visits, workshops, and publications.

### Activity 2.1.1: Ongoing monitoring and evaluation (2023-2025)

The project will:

* **Monitor progress towards the project goals**, including financial performance, environmental impact, and social benefits.
* **Evaluate the effectiveness of technical assistance** through regular assessments and feedback from stakeholders.

### Activity 2.1.1: Dissemination of best management practices (2023-2025)

The project will:

* **Develop a guide for sustainable shrimp aquaculture**, including best management practices, regulatory requirements, and market opportunities.
* **Disseminate the guide through workshops, publications, and online resources** to promote sustainable shrimp aquaculture in Ecuador.

### Activity 2.1.1: Ongoing capacity building (2023-2025)

The project will:

* **Develop a training program for shrimp aquaculture farmers**, including technical skills, business management, and environmental protection.
* **Disseminate the training program through workshops, field visits, and online resources** to promote sustainable shrimp aquaculture in Ecuador.
