B

### Implementation Guidelines and foster adoption of best practices. CI and NCA will utilize a "jurisdictional approach" to coordinate alignment among relevant stakeholders throughout the region. Jurisdictional Approach projects have been shown to be more cost-effective, efficient, and scalable than individual project approaches for coordinated disease management, enhanced climate resilience, and environmental stewardship (Kittner et al., 2018). The CI will lead the development of a "jurisdictional approach" in Ecuador, including:

- Partner selection (led by CI) in partnership with NCA; based on an assessment tool* (see criteria in Appendix B of Annex 2, Feasibility Study); applicable regionally and across geographies, to be finalized.
- Engagement to identify CSS project participants, and a lead partner screening to select shrimp farms that will participate in the CSS project.
- Develop a collaborative, time-bound workflow with all CSS project partners based on the baseline analysis of each farm. The workflow will include: (a) training for CSS project participants; (b) each participating farm to produce shrimp meeting the CSS criteria and each participant entity to support the adoption of CSS practices (e.g., through technical assistance, capacity building, or financial support conservation uses); led by CI, in partnership with NCA.
- Capacity building and technical assistance (led by CI, in partnership with NCA) Conducted and technical sessions for 200–250 farmers from small and medium-sized farms and other stakeholders in the region.

#### 11.7 Technical assistance from CI and NCA will enable farmers to design and implement restoration initiatives on portions of their farms which will confer water quality benefits within the farm and provide broader climate change resilience, including through reduced sedimentation and efficient flood protection. The costs of the mangrove restoration on farms will be financed by the farmer.

A target of 250 ha of mangroves will be restored in the immediate vicinity of shrimp ponds and in areas where providing additional habitat for shrimp is feasible. This will be achieved with technical assistance from consultants hired by CI to establish hydrology and landscape conditions within the restored area while allowing the restoration project to proceed. The restoration will be implemented through a combination of conventional approaches to coastal protection, by integrating ecosystems to improve the overall outcome for biodiversity and ecosystem services. The mangrove restoration project is expected to be completed in 2023; through this project will provide tools to replicate at scale elsewhere in the region and the world; with potential to become a best practice for global shrimp-farming regions and as a model for other agriculture production sectors.

There are many examples of exploitation and mismanagement of mangroves and shrimp farms, and hence the need for better coordination among stakeholders. Effective public policies and programs by national government agencies, local communities and the private sector, all of whom operate in the same space (Rahman et al., 2018). The CSS project will be implemented through a multi-stakeholder approach to implementation of CSS at project sites and project initiatives and is critical to achieving program sustainability. Community-based organizations, non-governmental organizations, local government agencies, national and regional government public institutions, universities, local organizations, national and international financial institutions, and other stakeholders are all expected to participate in the CSS project. The CI will communicate project activities and outcomes will promote climate-smart shrimp amongst other producers in Ecuador.

To disseminate knowledge gathered by the project, collections of key documents and resources will be housed within existing knowledge portals including: (i) the website and training initiatives led by NCA as part of the SSI-related work; (ii) the CSS website hosted on the CI’s website; (iii) the University of California at Davis (UCD), with its course on Marine engineering and Aquaculture engineering (a key stakeholder of the CSS project).

### 12.1 A/C Communications & Knowledge Management Manager, the Manager of Component 2 (Incentives, Financial Mechanisms and Climate Finance) and the Climate Smart Aquaculture specialist will lead activities that will target a range of stakeholders including: (i) government agencies; (ii) private sector companies; (iii) farmers (with an estimated average of 20 people per training); A further 19 workshops are planned for CSS technology demonstrations and trainings.

See <http://css.aquafarm-mapping.net/app/> for the CSS assessment tool developed for the Philippines and Indonesia that will be adapted for Ecuador.

Activity 2.1.3. Facilitate partnerships and access to mechanisms for credit and investment in shrimp farms for expansion and consolidation of climate-smart aquaculture practices.

122. CC will work with different actors of the shrimp supply chain to address finance access barriers and facilitate the flow of credit and investment for farm operators that seek to transition their production modes into more sustainable ones.
123. The project offers two types of products: (i) a financial service, and (ii) other services. A significant demand is being created among farmers wanting to invest in the SSP and CSS models. The project will provide access to green credit lines by key financial institutions such as the Development Bank of Paraguay, the Inter-American Development Bank, and others. This will also imply in promoting a sustainable model of shrimp production, reducing bank staff on impact and risk in investing in a shrimp-based business.
124. As new financial instruments are being developed, business demand for technical support to ensure successful implementation of these models is increasing. The project will provide technical assistance through platforms such as the Sustainability Academy, to small- and medium-sized farms, and other service providers in sustainable shrimp production. These services include training on best practices, access to financing, and information open to the general public. The Sustainability Academy portal combines education materials, workshops, self-assessment tools, and a forum for exchange of experiences among stakeholders.
125. The Sustainability Academy content will be expanded to include CSS, good aquaculture practices, mangrove conservation and restoration component), market opportunities in connection with SSP and/or ASC, and participation in the Socio-Manglar program. This project will bring together stakeholders to make the Sustainability Academy and the Socio-Manglar program more accessible for small- and medium-sized farms that are not currently clients of the banking system to date, expanding the base of beneficiaries of the program.
126. Implementation of demonstration cases, from diagnosis to work plan development, implementation and management, will be carried out in collaboration with the private sector. The project will also include investment cases. It will provide technical support to businesses in the project feasibility and implementation phase (e.g., business planning, market analysis, financial projections, etc.). This will include mangrove conservation or restoration component), market opportunities in connection with SSP and/or ASC, and participation in the Socio-Manglar program. The project will also promote the development of a model for sustainable shrimp production that is based on the principles of sustainability. Support from the project will be conditional on the business’s commitment to invest in mangrove restoration.
127. CC will also promote the implementation of a roundtable created by the National Mangrove Plan with industry actors to include the CSS concept into the agenda and priority discussion topics and the solutions to address financial access barriers for small- and medium-sized farms, as well as other actors that are interested in reducing risks of investments in the shrimp industry, so working in connection with the NCA as well as with the private sector.
128. To address investment needs, the project will seek to identify and support shrimp farmers on the design of sustainability efforts as a tool to mobilize capital towards CSS, ASC and SSP models. CC will assist these needs through technical assistance (e.g., business planning, market analysis, financial projections) provided that they comply with initial requirements: i) A concrete investment opportunity that can improve climate resilient practices in shrimp production; ii) The willingness of the farm operator to invest in sustainability efforts and prioritize opportunities based on a series of eligibility criteria (other details in Section 5.4.3 of Annex Z, Financial Plan).
129. The technical support from the project will be used with the goal of unlocking private finance to specific geographies with a role in mangrove conservation or restoration but not necessarily those farmers being the most vulnerable. CC will seek to identify and prioritize shrimp farms that are owned by small- and medium-sized farm operators in the area, and they are the ones that face more challenges in their capacity to attract investment. The project will consider two types of interventions: i) financial instruments that aim at reducing or limit the capacity of attracting capital to promote sustainable intensification at farm level; or ii) define an intervention model that seeks to improve access to credit for small- and medium-sized shrimp farmers, with efficiencies and improved conditions, and definition of Key Performance Indicators (KPIs) to monitor achievement towards these goals.

## Output 2. Sustainable management of mangroves is improved through agreements with the private sector, including aquaculture companies.

### Activity 2.2.1: Establish agreements with businesses, including aquaculture companies, to contribute to mangrove restoration and financial sustainability of the national Socio Bosque Incentive Program (the Socio Manglar Subaccount)

#### 130. The GCF will work with NCA and ASC to continually strengthen training materials on the CSS concept and to refine the CSS standard. This will include developing a market engagement strategy for CSS in Ecuador. This will include assessing market engagement for CSS with retailers, including market research and analysis of potential buyers. This will also include working closely with ASC and NCA and third-party independent auditors that confirm farm adherence to the CSS standard.

#### 131. Ecuador’s Socio Manglar incentive mechanism provides a tangible, government-supported pathway for shrimp farms and other enterprises operating on Ecuador’s coast to make effective their commitments to mangrove conservation and restoration. The GCF will support this initiative by providing funding through the Subaccount of the Socio Manglar subaccount of Ecuador’s Social Bond Fund, with an initial donation of US$100,000 (USD 75,000 from the Global Environment Facility (GEF) Trust Fund and USD 25,000 from the Forests, Agriculture and Environmental Sustainable Investment Fund (FAIS)*). And the new Socio Manglar subaccount is managed using a similar structure to the existing FAIS. The GCF will provide funding for the management of the fund making and FAIS has a board made up of representatives from the public and private sectors. All funds are subject to the same rules as FAIS, including the requirement that 50% of the board be comprised of an investment committee that oversees the policies and strategies implemented with the funds based in Ecuador and works on behalf of investors internationally. Appendix D of Annex A provides further background on the Socio Manglar subaccount.

#### 132. FAIS is a private, non-profit entity that has a track record of the management, mobilization, investment and implementation of projects to support sustainable development in line with GEF priorities. It will also provide climate change, complementing the efforts of the Government of Ecuador. FAIS currently has more than USD 10 million in assets under management and is supported by a Board of Directors that includes international donors, including CI, who has a permanent voting seat on the “Technical Committee” responsible for the oversight of projects. The Technical Committee will be responsible for project selection, monitoring governance and ongoing financial and technical monitoring. GCF will ensure compliance with GCF requirements such as the requirement to have 50% of the board comprised of an investment committee that oversees the 20-year project lifecycle. CI will provide an annual “testament” to GCF confirming the continued existence of the Technical Committee, which will be reviewed by GCF. FAIS has a track record in supporting community management of mangrove areas and it also obtain from MAATE and FAIS written confirmation that they will continue to support the project through 2035. CI will provide an annual “testament” to GCF confirming for the objectives of supporting community management of mangrove areas. CI will monitor financial and technical performance, including the implementation of the project milestones. The first testament will be held (financial reports) and broader Socio Manglar program (technical reports). Further details on CI’s role in relation to the project and its relationship with GCF regarding after the project implementation period are included in the Term Sheet, Annex 14.

#### 133. The Government of Ecuador (through MAATE) currently finances the Socio Manglar project directly from its annual budget. The GCF will support this initiative by providing funding through the Subaccount of the Socio Manglar subaccount to provide a more stable and long-term sustainable source of financing for the Socio Manglar program. This will be done in line with the GEF’s priorities, which include supporting small and medium enterprises looking to expand their environmental commitments.*2* The Ecuadorian businesses seeking to comply with these commitments are expected to make a voluntary contribution towards the project, with a minimum contribution from GCF (see below). CI will also work with ASC, the corporate partners of their value chain and their respective supply chains to ensure that they are aware of the benefits of the CSS standard and the Socio Manglar program. CI will also work closely with MAATE to promote support for mangrove conservation through the FCEC.

The interest income will be used to pay annual incentives to the AUJUGA and CIPM communities, as well as to cover operational costs of the Socio Mangroves subaccount. In addition, the GCF funds will be used to capitalize the Socio Mangrove subaccount for providing long-term sustainable finance in support of the project’s activities (see Section 2\.1). The interest income generated from this subaccount and other contributions (see next section), will generate enough income to provide incentive payments for the communities, operational costs, and to cover any shortfall. In addition, the GCF funds will be used to capitalize GCF resources in a separate interest-bearing bank account alongside other contributions to the Socio Mangrove subaccount. The interest earned from this account will be used to pay annual incentives to the communities compliant with GCF requirements (See Appendix 1 of Annex 2 for details of the Socio Mangrove subaccount and its financial structure). The GCF funds will be managed by FIAS, who will also monitor the use of GCF funds within the subaccount managed by FIAS.

These funds are intended to support the subaccount’s capital early in the project (Year 2) is expected to provide a level of confidence from potential private sector donors because the subaccount will have sufficient equity capital to attract additional investment. Private-sector private-donor are more likely to contribute alongside public funding, such as from GCF; since it will be clear that there is a strong commitment by the project to ensure that the funds are used for their intended purpose. The interest income generated from this subaccount and other contributions (see next section) will provide incentive payments and attract sufficient additional capital to allow the Socio Mangrove program to expand to cover more mangroves, including those in the buffer zone of the protected area. Output 3.1 Decision making for mangrove management by national government agencies and local governments is based on generation and provision of accurate and up-to-date data on mangrove condition and socio-economic information.

Activity 3.1.1 Monitoring of mangrove condition and socio-economic impacts in mangrove-dependent communities.
- 140. The reduction of emissions and the environmental and social benefits associated with the implementation of project activities will be monitored integrated remote sensing and community participatory. This activity will provide data on changes in mangrove cover, carbon stocks, and other components.

Monitoring, Reporting and Verification (MRV) of Mangrove Cover and Carbon Stocks
- 141. CII will enter into collab with two local universities or NGOs (chosen/selected) to collaboratively generate data and capacity at all levels to improve MRV systems for mangroves. To be integrated both into project M&E, REDD+ and NDCs/REDD+ reporting systems and the national MRV system.
- Mangroves along with other tropical ecosystems are being key priority in the NDC (2019). The information generated will be used to improve the national MRV system for mangroves. This activity is also fundamental to the implementation of the PECI with the private sector (linked to Activity 2.3).
- As described in Annex 2, natural changes in mangrove cover due to the complex hydrological dynamics of mangrove ecosystems are expected and will be monitored. The monitoring of these changes will provide a better understanding of the structure of the mangroves and the biodiversity they contain. The areas that will be monitored include: i) areas where mangroves have been lost or degraded, ii) areas where mangroves have been restored, iii) areas of infrastructure development and deforestation not controlled by project activities.
- A subgrant will be awarded to a local university or NGO to provide technical assistance in the collection and analysis of data on changes in the structure of mangrove cover. This activity is also fundamental to the implementation of the PECI with the private sector (linked to Activity 2.3).
- Updated pre-project baseline data on mangrove coverage and change will be gathered and integrated into national forest inventory and FREL. CII will enter into a collab with a local university or NGO to analyze mangrove cover and project results for the next five years.

CII will disseminate information to decision makers at the local and national level, through policy reports for local governments, creating maps for local communities and extended reports to the MAATE and other national decision makers.

Socio-economic impact evaluation (SEE)
- The SEE’s main objective is to assess the effects from project proposed activities on environmental and socioeconomic outcomes (i.e., climate mitigation and emissions reductions, resilience, enhanced livelihoods) and the level of benefit between treatment and control groups within the project implementation period for the project activities.
- CII will conduct the SEE in Year 2 following project start-up to the baseline and in Year 6 for the final assessment. CII will enter into a collab with local universities or NGOs (chosen/selected) to provide technical assistance in the design, analysis of the data, and reporting. The collab will also be responsible for training. Eligible criteria for selection of the local university is provided as Appendix 7 of Annex 2.

Output 3.2 Legal and regulatory frameworks at local and sectoral level are harmonized and include climate change adaptation and mangrove management.
Activity 3.2.1 Support local governments (2 provincial governments, 2 municipalities and 5 parishes) to improve and/or implement PSDTs and other participatory planning instruments that incorporate climate change adaptation and mangrove management, applying a gender approach.

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 43

#### Activity 1.2.2 Strengthen regulatory frameworks and law enforcement by agencies and institutions responsible for mangrove conservation, with a focus on human rights.

- CI will work with local governments to enforce their capacity to manage their territories with a clear perspective of mangrove ecosystem management and marine spatial planning that is critically important in the context of food production benefits from mangroves as well as other ecosystem services from coastal ecosystems. CI will work with local governments to develop a framework for integrated coastal management (ICM) plans, including mangrove conservation and restoration. For this, CI will work following PDOT's guidelines and will provide technical support to local governments in the development of ICM plans.
- CI will work with five parish administrations (Paroquias) to develop climate change risk-management plans that integrate mangroves as a key component for coastal resilience. These plans will be developed through participatory planning that will help them increase the economic, social and cultural resilience of individuals and communities in the face of climate change impacts.
- CI will work with two municipal governments (Guayaquil and Machala), responsible for land-use planning and zoning through their PDOTs to provide technical input and facilitation of participatory planning processes to develop strategies for climate-change adaptation, including strategies for conservation and restoration of mangroves.
- CI will support two provincial governments (Guayas and El Oro) with technical support to integrate climate change considerations into the development of land-use plans. This will include the identification of climate change investment. These activities will support management of priority conservancy areas, local fisherman, mangrove-dependent communities, and other stakeholders in the development of integrated coastal management plans as well as integrated coastal management plans.
- CI will enter into service agreements with consorcios building for 19 to provide territorial planning support through their PDOTs. The Ministry of Environment (MMAE), the Local Government Administration Management Specialist will provide support to these government entities across the four projects of the strategy.
- CI will provide ongoing support, monitoring and public communication for the implementation of plans, commitments and agreements with municipal ordinances and other legal instruments determined by PDOTs.

#### Activity 3.2.2 Strengthen regulatory frameworks and law enforcement in the context of the Roundtable for Sustainable Shrimp, to be convened by the MMAE and VAMC.
- CI will provide staff time and cover workshop costs as needed.

### In Year 10:
To 5 to 6: CI's Legal Specialist and Local Government & Integrated Coastal Management Specialist will work with partners to re-establish the integrity of the UOCV for law enforcement and sanctions for illegal activities in mangrove forests. The legal framework will be developed through a joint effort between MMAE, VAMC, NGO and the IUCN; the UOCV's main goal is to avoid and control the clear-cutting of mangrove forest areas through the cooperation of public, private and NGO entities. ## B. Implementation arrangements (max. 1500 words; approximately 3 pages plus diagrams)

### NDA and Government Partners

#### Ecuador's MAATE, as the GCF National Designated Authority (NDA), will ensure that activities implemented by the project align with strategic national objectives, priorities, and standards, including the National Climate Change Strategy, the National Forest Policy, and other relevant policies, strategies, plans, and needs. The Executive Entity (EE) will engage with the NDA throughout project implementation. The NDA will act as a focal point for coordination between the EE and Ecuador's government on matters related to the status of project activities and impacts. MAATE, through its Undersecretariat of Natural Heritage and Underground Water Resources, is responsible for providing technical support to project activities and provides grant and investment financing as described elsewhere in this proposal.

#### Accredited Entity

- The CLO-GCF Agency will serve as the Accredited Entity (AE) for the project. The CLO-GCF Agency will be responsible for the overall oversight of this project as defined in the Accredited Master Agreement (AMA) with the GCF. This includes, but is not limited to, the following: a) Project planning and monitoring; b) Reporting, audits, and annual site visits); and review and approval of the EE's annual work plans and budgets.
- CLO-GCF will be responsible for ensuring that the project achieves its intended outcomes and the achievement of project results and Outputs, reporting to the GCF, and project closure and evaluation. CLO-GCF will also ensure that the project is aligned with the GCF’s Strategic Plan 2018–2022, the GCF’s Strategy for Africa, and the GCF’s Strategy for Asia and the Pacific.
- The CLO-GCF Agency will be responsible for providing technical support to project activities and financing as described elsewhere in this proposal.

#### Project Governance

The project's governance structure includes the Project Steering Committee and the Project Management Committee. The Project Steering Committee will be composed of the following individuals: i) the Undersecretary of Climate Change of MAATE or his/her delegate; ii) the Undersecretary of Natural Heritage of MAATE or his/her delegate; iii) the Director General or Vice President of CLO-GCF or his/her delegate (who shall also be the Undersecretary of Climate Change).

The principal functions of the Project Steering Committee are to: a) provide strategic guidance and support regarding project implementation, including program progress and performance monitoring, development of strategic issues that might arise during implementation, and provide support for the necessary inter-institutional cooperation; b) approve the annual work plans of the EE. The Project Steering Committee will also participate in the selection of the Project Director, through a competitive process.


---
**Page 52**
---

163\. The Project Steering Committee will meet at least twice per year to review the progress of the ongoing semester project and to advise the Project Director on strategic and policy matters. The Project Steering Committee will be convened by the Project Director in advance to give the members sufficient time to schedule their participation.

The Project Steering Committee may also call special meetings as needed, including when a majority of meetings of the Project Steering Committee will be convened when deemed necessary and at the request of one or 2 of its members. The Project Steering Committee may also invite key stakeholders to support specific themes.

164\. The Project Management Advisory Committee will comprise the following individuals: (a) the Director of the Marine Environment Protection Program of the National Environment Agency; (b) the representative from WAMF; (c) the Director of the Coastal and Marine Program of CI-Ecuador; and (d) the Project Director. The Project Management Advisory Committee may also invite key partners to provide advice on specific themes.

165\. The Project Management Advisory Committee will meet at least four times per year to advise the Project Director and the National Project Director (NPD) on technical matters, and to discuss challenges and opportunities for project implementation. The Project Management Committee may invite key partners to provide advice on specific themes.

166\. For both government committees, members who are government employees will not be remunerated by GCF funds.

167\. The NPD will be appointed by MAATE's Undersecretary of Natural Heritage, who will be employed and funded by MAATE. The NPD will advise the Project Director on government policies and priorities; review coherence of the project with national development plans; and provide advice to the Project Steering Committee in preparation for the annual Procurement Plan for project services and goods; and review the technical and financial quarterly project reports.

168\. Technical Advisory Committees will be created for each of the Components and each of the estuaries of the project. In the case of Component 1, the Technical Advisory Committee will include MAATE, the Risk Management Unit of CI-Ecuador, the National Project Director (NPD), the Planning National Secretariat, the Viceroy of AUSCEN; The Component 2 committee will include MAATE, the VMAP, the NICA, and the Fisheries and Aquaculture Department of CI-Ecuador. In the case of Component 3, the Technical Advisory Committee for Component 3 will include MAATE, the VMAP, the Planning National Secretariat, Provincial, municipal, and Parish governments, and representatives from AUSCEN. For each estuary included in Components 1, 2 or 3 of each estuary, a committee will be created to integrate the Zonal Directors of MAATE with the planning institutions, local food producers and AUSCEN representatives.

Exiting Entity

169\. CI-Ecuador will execute this project. CI acting through its country office in Ecuador (referred to in this proposal as "CI" or "CI-Ecuador"), will be the FBO for all activities of this project. CI-Ecuador will be responsible for project implementation, including the management and implementation of policies, plans, programs, projects, measures, resources and actions to achieve resident communities and other stakeholders' objectives.

170\. CI-Ecuador will work in close collaboration with the Government of Ecuador and policies and priorities in coordination with MAATE to achieve project outcomes and deliverables. CI-Ecuador will also be responsible for the implementation of the Project Management Plan (including sub-grant agreements, service agreements, and MOUs) for this project. As a branch of CI, CI-Ecuador is subject to the same terms and conditions as other branches of CI. For more information on CI's Operations Manual (see Annex 21). The CI-GF Agency has assessed the capacity of CI-Ecuador and has determined that it will be able to effectively manage this project in accordance with the Project Management Plan.

Throughout Project implementation, CI-Ecuador will be supported by various CI divisions which will lend specific expertise, including CI's Amazon Division, Conservation Finance Division (supporting private sector funding).

## and for Oceans (supporting blue carbon and mangrove restoration), and Project Delivery and Monitoring (supporting safeguards and gender).

170. For more than 30 years, CI has been protecting nature for the benefit of all. CI employs more than 1,000 people and partners with thousands of local communities to conserve biodiversity in over 25 countries around the world and undertaken interventions across 77 countries, protecting more than 601 million hectares of land, marine, and coastal areas. CI has a long history of working closely with fishers and other coastal communities on the coasts and coastal areas of continental Ecuador, Andes Choco and the Amazon to implement conservation solutions within priority landscapes and seascapes. These efforts have been supported by the USAID-funded project “Ecuador’s Fishery,” with close collaboration with artisanal fisher associations to promote capacity building and sustainable management measures for small-scale fisheries in the spongy lobster, tuna, and prawn fisheries and in marine Ecuador (with black shell and red crab fisheries).

171. CI-Ecuador will establish the main Project Management Unit (PMU) at CI’s office in Guayaquil. The PMU will be headed by a Project Director who is supported by a Project Manager, an Evaluation Manager, a Safeguards Manager, a Gender Manager, Communications & Knowledge Manager, Procurement and Contracts Officer, and Administrative Finance Officer. The PMU will also have staff based in Guayaquil and Esmeraldas.

172. In addition, CI, in coordination with MAITE, will establish an Enlace Field Unit (EFU) in each of the Project’s execution offices for the northern region (Esmeraldas, Manabi, Carchi, Los Ríos, Guayas, and El Oro); and regional execution offices. These EFUs will be led by the MAITE Provincial Directors in Esmeraldas, Guayas, and Manabi or an equivalent position in the other provinces. The EFU staff will include a Project Director, a Monitoring and Evaluation Technician (CI staff for the southern estuaries and PUSEEIC for the northern estuaries). Establishing EFUs is to help ensure that project activities are implemented effectively and efficiently with local communities and stakeholders for achieving the goals of the project. Evaluations on mangrove areas have shown that frequent technical support from CI has been critical in ensuring that project staff adopt best practices, including conservation practices. The Enlace Coordinator will ensure effective liaison and coordination with local stakeholders and focal points at the provincial level to provide guidance and oversight over project implementation. The EFU and the supporting staff will support implementation of Conservation and Stewardship Agreements with fishing communities and other coastal users in their respective regions.

## Justification for GCF funding required (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

181. Public and private funds for the activities proposed in this project are limited and insufficient to tackle the ongoing challenge of mangrove deforestation and restoration at the scale required.
182. Grant funding is warranted to create enabling conditions for sustained capacity and investment.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 49

### Ecosystem-based food risks due to climate change and they are among the world's most variable problems in mitigating GHG emissions, especially through the accumulation of carbon in soil as organic matter.

#### 183. Gaps in the generation and dissemination of data regarding climate change scenarios and mangrove ecosystem services (e.g., coastal protection, fisheries, tourism) at national levels (provincial, municipal, and parishes), not adequately incorporating climate change risks and the potential for adaptation to these risks. These gaps are particularly evident in the case of mangroves.

#### 184. Gaps in the understanding of the impacts of climate change on mangrove ecosystems, especially the impacts of sea-level rise and increased frequency and intensity of storms and hurricanes. These gaps are particularly evident in the case of mangroves, particularly in soils, are well documented globally. Better Ecosystem-specific data and monitoring programs (e.g., soil moisture content) would help to better understand these impacts.

#### 185. Gaps in the generation and dissemination of data regarding climate change scenarios and mangrove ecosystem services (e.g., coastal protection, fisheries, tourism) at national levels (provincial, municipal, and parishes), not adequately incorporating climate change risks and the potential for adaptation to these risks. These gaps are particularly evident in the case of mangroves.

#### 186. Private sector investment in mangroves has also been constrained. Private sector investment would be made possible by removing barriers to private sector investment, such as lack of information on market demand for sustainable shrimp (and other products), and lack of access to financing.

#### 187. Private sector return from mangrove conservation and restoration have been undervalued, leading the in aquaculture sector to extensive production models that have driven approximately half of mangrove deforestation globally over the past three decades. The private sector has also been constrained by high costs per hectare, which reduced costs and increased revenues. Shrimp aquaculture using CCS models (see below B 3 and B4) can be a win-win for both shrimp producers and mangroves. However, these models require increases in on-farm mangrove areas, requiring a shift away from Ecuador's traditional, extensive methods of shrimp farming that are not compatible with mangrove conservation.

#### 188. Community associations linked to protection of mangroves, reinforcing and expanding enterprises and investments in the sector, through private or public sector or ecosystem also provide a way to internalize the returns of their mangrove conservation commitments under stewardship agreements (AUCMEN, see section B3 and Annex A). However, these community-based mangrove generating activities are not optimized. Limiting the scale and effectiveness of these conservation strategies.

#### 189. Regulatory requirements have contributed to reversing Ecuador's mangrove deforestation trends, but gross deforestation rates remain high (see Figure 2). The main drivers of this deforestation, especially from international demand for more sustainable products in shrimp markets, are rapidly increasing.

#### 190. Motivating private sector investment, which has to date been marginal for mangrove conservation/restoration requires better alignment of the potential for private benefits and regulatory frameworks and incentives that recognize these benefits. The project will support the development of a national strategy for promoting technical support and building the capacity of both the public and private sectors, the project will support long-term financing mechanisms (e.g., carbon credits) through government mechanisms as well as establishing financial mechanisms for stable investment flows.

#### 191. The project objectives align with GCF's function as demonstrated by its contribution to three of GCF's priority areas: climate change mitigation (reducing GHG emissions and adaptation benefits), ecosystem services, and the significant GHG emissions reductions and adaptation benefits it will provide. Given the high vulnerability of mangroves to climate change impacts, including sea-level rise and increased frequency and intensity of storms and hurricanes, the project will provide a direct response to these impacts. Of course, significant national public funding for this project is not realistic. Nevertheless, the project will provide an important demonstration that private sector investment in mangrove conservation can be successful.

#### 192. The project will also contribute to GCF's priority area of promoting climate change adaptation by catalyzing climate interest in mangroves and other coastal ecosystems. It will do this through a number of mechanisms, including the development of farming industry such that agriculture is sustainable, zero deforestation and mangrove restoration.

#### 193. The project will also contribute to GCF's priority area of promoting ecosystem services by providing substantial unrealized but demonstrated opportunity to protect mangroves through community conservation, which will provide a range of ecosystem services including coastal protection, fisheries, tourism and new economic opportunities for some of Ecuador's poorest and most vulnerable coastal communities.

#### 194. However, to capitalize on these opportunities, grant funding is critical to overcome the barriers to mangrove conservation and restoration. GCF has funded successfully pilot projects on community conservation and promoting

B.6. Exit strategy (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

[See Annex 2, Feasibility Study for more details.]

Project investments will drive a paradigm shift in the institutional, organizational and financial frameworks governing mangrove conservation and management in Ecuador. The project includes various activities that build sustainability, outlined below by component:

Project Component I: Mangrove areas under effective management coverage increased, including through the establishment of Area-based Sustainable Use Management Entities (AUSCMEs) and FPA implementing climate adaptation plans.

191. Project investments in strengthening the management of AUSCMEs and FPsa, expanding their coverages, and explicitly incorporating climate risks and benefits into management will consolidate a foundation that will be maintained beyond project completion.
192. AUSCME and Socio Mangar agreements create legally binding terms and agreements for community-based mangrove conservation and incentives for up to two decades. Project investments will support training, planning and capacity building to ensure income establishment costs for agreements and thus scale up long-lasting coverage of these programs.

AUSCME establish (renewable) ten-year commitments from communities (with contracts utilizing a 10-year renewable model) to manage their mangroves in perpetuity. These commitments will sustainably manage mangroves, recognized and endorsed by the environmental authority. Areas restored to mangrove cover will be managed under these agreements for up to two decades. The project assumes that areas of these areas beyond project implementation. The project design assumes that the Government of Ecuador will continue to provide funding for managing existing mangrove areas in perpetuity through sustainable management.

The project will expand the width of the scope of Socio Mangar agreements to cover an additional 20,000 ha. These Social Mangrove Agreements embody a 15-year renewable model on the part of the communities to sustain these areas and benefits; while including additional legal provisions in their contracts beyond the terms of the agreement. The project assumes that the Government of Ecuador will continue to provide funding for managing existing mangroves through sustainable management by the environmental authority and its members. As noted in the project Theory of Change, an important assumption of the project is that the communities will continue to manage these areas.

The project also includes incorporating climate adaptation into the strategies of government-run mangrove-dominated Marine Protected Areas (MPAs). By integrating these strategies into the management plans of these MPAs, the project assumes that the Government of Ecuador will provide funding for managing these MPAs already have consistent funding annually from the government but there are also additional ongoing annual costs associated with climate adaptation. The project assumes that the Government of Ecuador will continue to provide funding for managing existing mangroves through sustainable management by the environmental authority and its members.

Legal commitments and financial commitments will contribute to reinforcing a solid foundation of grassroots capacity built by the project. Social capital and local livelihoods require legal and financial instruments, and vice versa. The project assumes that the Government of Ecuador will continue to provide funding for mangrove conservation, driving the signing of AUSCME agreements covering 60,000 ha to date. By investing in building capacity at all levels, the project assumes that the Government of Ecuador will continue to provide funding for managing these areas through sustainable management by local enterprises.

Legal commitments and financial commitments will contribute to reinforcing a solid foundation of grassroots capacity built by the project. Social capital and local livelihoods require legal and financial instruments, and vice versa. The project assumes that the Government of Ecuador will continue to provide funding for mangrove conservation, driving the signing of AUSCME agreements covering 60,000 ha to date. By investing in building capacity at all levels, the project assumes that the Government of Ecuador will continue to provide funding for managing these areas through sustainable management by local enterprises.

Project Component B: The private sector becomes a transformational agent for change by reducing GHG emissions and providing financing support to conserve and restore mangroves that increase climate resilience for other coastal populations.

197. Long-term sustainability is the underlying rationale for this project outcome. By recognizing and internalizing long-term benefits, the private sector will be more likely to make investments in private-sector practices and investment decisions; the project will lay the foundation for ongoing action.
    * In shrimp aquaculture sector, shifting a significant proportion of production area to climate-smart practices will help reduce GHG emissions. This can be achieved by adopting ASC/SCS certification criteria and best management practices (BMPs) in shrimp farming. By linking these actions by early adopters to public-private dialogue and market engagement, the project will support the development of a market for certified shrimp.
    * The project will also contribute to the sustainability of the work being supported by GCF. Should the CSS Fund be fully funded, it would provide an additional $49 million per year in incentives as co-financing.

200. Certification schemes are designed to reward responsible farming practices. Globally, buyers in major markets are making strong commitments to purchase farmed seafood products from certified sources and ASC is a recognized standard for shrimp aquaculture certification. The project will support the CSS Fund in its efforts to invest in more sustainable practices and to undergo the assessments necessary to obtain certification. The project will also provide technical assistance to help small- and medium-sized farms achieve ASC certification and by the end of the project there will be more examples of small- and medium-sized farms that have achieved ASC certification, which will increase their ability to access markets where ASC is a common incentive for firms to attain certification®. The project will support farmers to access these markets.

201. The project will expand a dedicated long-term finance mechanism to assure continuity of resources for the Socio Mangroves Fund (SMF) and its associated projects. The SMF is an initiative of the Government of Ecuador, which will provide US$49 million per year for these incentives as co-finance. The project will ensure that this funding is available in perpetuity by establishing a trust fund with the World Bank. Four million dollars ($US4 million) of GCF funding is requested to increase the capital of the Socio Mangroves Fund (SMF) and to support its operations, including the SMF Trust Fund, which will be used for shrimp aquaculture and Ecuador’s PESC. The GCF contribution of $4 million will be held in an interest-bearing bank account with the World Bank. The SMF Trust Fund will be established as a separate entity in accordance with the investment policy of GCF. Projections for the growth of the capitalization and use of the subsequent resources are provided in Table 10. In addition to providing financial incentives for climate change mitigation benefits, the project will also contribute to creating the necessary conditions for results-based payments (RBP) for mangrove conservation in the long-term.

GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 52

B

Project Component 3: Create the enabling conditions for sustaining reductions in mangrove deforestation and increasing mangrove restoration by strengthening governance, climate change adaptation strategies, coastal management policies, and legal enforcement.

202. Sustainability and long-term improvements of policies and public institutions will be ensured by a combination of regulatory instruments and institutional capacity.

203. A key part of our overall strategy is to ensure the development and implementation of policy, planning and legal instruments that will govern mangrove conservation and climate considerations in local development plans, regional development plans, national development plans, and sectoral policies (e.g., coastal zone management development plans), ensuring the basis for long-term application of these regulatory instruments guiding development.

204. Harmonized public policy instruments for the management of the mangrove ecosystem (e.g., procedures for assuring transparency in regulation, decision making and enforcement action, inter-agency cooperation, etc.) will be developed to ensure that all stakeholders are aware of their responsibilities and obligations under the law.

In addition, capacity building for national and regional institutions will help ensure that institutions can continue to monitor the effectiveness and sustainability of their actions after project completion and improve their effectiveness in implementing policies and strategies. This will include a review of institutional design and execution to ensure that they can sustain these activities after the GCF funding concludes. For example, the project will develop a monitoring system for mangrove restoration and conservation that will be generated by the project with the aim of including a robust set of data and methodologies for inclusion by Ecuador in its national reporting framework. The project will also support the development of a significant carbon pool and sequestration into national monitoring; Ecuador will be able to provide a more comprehensive report on the impact of mangrove restoration activities, including the role of climate finance. Also, institutionalization of training programs, curricula and materials developed by the project will provide the basis for ongoing replication by MAATE and the Judicial Branch after the project concludes.

# EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

The assessment is of the performance of the project/programme against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF's Initial Investment Framework.

## D1. Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

205. The project will provide both adaptation and mitigation benefits. The direct adaptation benefits come from reduced flooding risk of vulnerable people and project activities that are expected to increase people’s resilience in the face of climate change. Mitigation benefits come from a combination of reduced emissions by reducing mangrove deforestation and from carbon sequestration.

206. Eight priority municipalities, with a population of 3.4 million, were selected based on mangrove cover and population at risk of flooding related to climate change (see para.18). Flood modeling including the effect of mangroves shows that these areas are highly vulnerable to increased flood risks due to losses of ecosystem services (See Annex 25). Based on the modelling, the flood risk for 60,000 people can be reduced by protecting and restoring 34 ha of mangrove forest. This is expected to increase the rate of mangrove recovery as compared to baseline rates. The population at risk is likely to increase as populations grow in these areas. The project will also provide benefits from increased food security and population growth and migration introduces great uncertainty into the estimations (See Annex 25 for details of Adaptation beneficiaries).

### Adaptation Impacts

| --- | --- |
| Change in expected losses of economic assets due to the impact of extreme weather disasters in the geographic area of the GCF project | USD 280 million per year in avoided losses of economic assets from flood protection benefits of mangroves |
| Direct and Indirect beneficiaries reached (ARA I Most vulnerable people and communities) | 89,650 direct beneficiaries of flood protection benefits. |
| Beneficiaries (female/male) adopting improved and/or new climate-resilient agricultural practices - ARA I: Most vulnerable people and communities | 41,500 people (50% female, 50% male) with diversified livelihoods and increased food security, and/or reduced flood risk |
| Hectares of terrestrial non-forest freshwater and coastal-marine areas brought under restoration and/or improved ecosystems (ha) | 156,633 ha of mangroves protected and strengthened |

### Data and assumptions

* During project implementation period.
* Based on USD 600 in avoided property damage per hectare of added mangrove by comparison to the baseline.
* People at high annual risk of flooding benefit from enhanced mangrove cover and management. See Annex 25
* People living in municipalities with high mangrove coverage to benefit from reduced flood risks.

Includes all of Ecuador’s mangroves, including those in the Amazon region focused on four estuaries (including the Guayas Estuary) and includes the enabling conditions activities

# Mitigation Impacts

- GHG emissions from gross deforestation under the baseline scenario are projected to be **2,428,985 tCO₂e** over the period including the project implementation period and **11,062,248 tCO₂e** over 20 years.
- Reductions in GHG emissions from gross deforestation relative to this baseline scenario are projected to be **567,404 tCO₂e** over the period and **3,566,685 tCO₂e** over 20 years.
- In addition, removals of GHG emissions from project reforestation activities are projected to be **164,707 tCO₂e** over the period and **988,242 tCO₂e** over 20 years.

In addition to the project's contributions towards advancing national climate change adaptation and mitigation plans, the project will also contribute to the aims of the GMA², which brings together a global community of practitioners working on mangroves in order to share knowledge and best practices. The project is a member along with other international organizations and the project will contribute in two ways to the GMA's work.

The first relates to ongoing knowledge sharing of GMA, especially related to mangrove restoration and "blue carbon" information. One of the functions of GMA as a knowledge hub to share information between practitioners working on mangroves is to provide technical guidance and best practices for project design informed by GMA toolskits and best practice guides. Data collected under activity 3.1.1 will also be shared with the GMA as a matter of course (through the Mangrove Restoration Tracker Tool and Global Mangrove Watch).

The second, the project itself makes an important contribution to the Mangrove Breathewatch² initiative launched by the GMA in June 2014. The aim is to establish a global network of high-level indicator sites to secure the future of 15 million hectares of mangroves globally (through conservation and restoration) through long-term financing of mangrove protection. This project alone represents about 1% of this goal.

<sub>² http://www.mangrovealliance.org</sub>

D.2 Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

This project will drive a paradigm shift in the way that Ecosystem values and benefits from critical natural infrastructure that protects coastal people and assets from flooding. By fully valuing the adaptation and mitigation services provided by mangroves, this project will help to transform existing policy frameworks across Ecuador's coastal region, the project presents an innovative approach to the goals of reducing GHG emissions and increasing resilience in a cost-effective manner.

The current paradigm undervalues both climate risks and the ecosystem services provided by mangroves. production of mangrove forests is currently valued at US$0.01 per hectare annually, while the benefits from climate risk and GHG emissions are both augmented by lack of sufficient government, private sector and civil society investment in mangrove restoration and conservation. The project will address this gap through empowerment of local stakeholders, and mobilization of private sector investment.

The combination of bolstering public sector frameworks and capacity, catalyzing public-private partnerships and engaging with the private sector to create a market for ecosystem services is unique. This focus on the green infrastructure that mangroves provide is an innovative and cost-effective approach with strong potential for replication in other countries. The project will work closely with government counterparts at all levels; government entities over time and the project will create lessons, guidance and tools for application in other countries.

Across all activities, the project will engage in comprehensive monitoring and reporting with the aim of strengthening learning and knowledge sharing. The project aims to carry out two comprehensive evaluations of its progress against its objectives and outputs. Each project evaluation will include the identification of lessons learned and their dissemination.

Complementing the strong focus on local action, community participation, gender equity and private sector engagement is a strong emphasis on building capacity for long-term institutional sustainability in policy framework. This enabling environment includes the strengthening of regulatory and policy frameworks with clear roles and responsibilities as relating to mangrove conservation and restoration dialogue and coordination across sectors.

The project includes a series of capacity building packages for local entities to strengthen their ability to plan, implement and monitor land use plans that will engage more closely in the land use planning process with local government. Building consensus among key stakeholders, including government, communities, private sector actors and civil society organizations is critical to achieving success with mangrove restoration costs. However, this project aims to demonstrate how mangroves can be a very cost-effective way of addressing climate change, through which to achieve social objectives.

The project approaches the development of mangroves in a participatory manner. The project has selected several planning tools to be formulated through a transparent and multi-stakeholder process. These include land use plans, community action plans, and restoration plans for the project. Technical assistance, training, logistical support, and coordination are the responsibilities of the project team. The project will also work with local communities to identify their own priorities, contributions, and to reach agreements for the benefit of all. It is expected that after several similar participatory processes, a replicable model can be developed. This approach is innovative in that it shows an approach is valid and worthwhile, despite the additional initial transaction costs, and that reaching agreements is an effective means of managing relationships with other stakeholders.

The project involves the scaling up and consolidation of activities that have successfully been used to conserve and restore mangroves in Ecuador. The area where the project is located lacks the most basic and essential infrastructure for mangrove restoration and conservation. A new community-based center will be built jointly between the local communities and the local government as a first step to ensure the long-term sustainability of the project, and to provide a focal point for capacity building, training and transparent planning processes. In addition, the participatory process for land use planning will be closely linked with the development of these centers.

Legal and regulatory adjustments, as needed for effective planning, will be recommended through technical documents

D.3. Sustainable development (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### Environmental benefits

217. The project will reduce deforestation, increase restoration and enhance management across Ecuador’s entire mangrove area of 156,633 ha.

218. In addition to the mitigation and flood-risk abatement benefits highlighted under GCF’s reporting framework (see Table D.4), there are additional ecosystem services including biodiversity conservation and habitat for fishery groundings. Ecuadorian mangroves are home to 179 plant species (Comrie, 2014) and contain a rich diversity of animals such as the black mangrove crab (Gecarcinus quadratus) that is habitat for spawning, breeding and nurseries of commercial and non-commercial fish species. The abundance of these species in Ecuadorian mangroves has been estimated to be US$352 million per year (Mera et al., 2014). Mangrove ecosystems provide other ecosystem services such as the high primary productivity of this ecosystem (Muthar et al. et al., 2004; Mera et al., 2014) and carbon sequestration (Bouwman, 2005). The total value of mangroves in Ecuador is estimated to be US$378-1,000/ha/year (Spalding et al., 2010). According to UNEP (2014), the full range of ecosystem services provided by mangroves in Ecuador is estimated at US$596 million per year. A more recent review of 230 studies around the world demonstrated that the average value of ecosystem services provided by mangroves was US$8,700/ha/year (Gelczer et al., 2010). The average value of US$1,161/ha/year in 2018 prices (Gelczer et al., 2020). In spite of valuation methods used, the importance of mangrove fisheries as a food source for local communities is widely recognized.

### Social co-benefits including health impacts

219. In addition to environmental benefits, the project will result in additional co-benefits, especially for mangrove-dependent communities and associations.
- Improved management capacity for mangroves managed by 60 associations will lead to enhanced ability to engage with government stakeholders, resolve internal conflicts and develop new partnerships.
- Improved mangrove management and plans are likely to integrate other social and environmental considerations such as the protection of biodiversity, conservation of cultural heritage sites and waste disposal.
- Improvements in control of illegal activities in mangroves area, in partnership between community-based associations and government authorities will also generate co-benefits enhancing local security.

### Economic co-benefits

220. Mangrove conservation and restoration is a relatively low-cost solution for climate change adaptation that has significant economic benefits for the government, the coastal sector, and the communities through avoided costs of the impacts of extreme climate events. The shrimp sector will be able to access new markets in Asia and Europe as well as other countries with high demand for Ecuadorian shrimp, accordingly, resulting in increased export revenue and employment.

221. Communities will see lower flooding and relocation costs, reduced impacts on infrastructure, round crop yields and improved food security through the use of agroforestry techniques will increase their resilience, economic stability, and food security of marginalized coastal fishing communities. Mangrove conservation and restoration also provides a habitat for fish species such as the black mangrove ecosystem dedicated to artisanal fishing, obtaining corvina (Cynoscion altivelis), mullet (Mugil crenalis), red snapper (Lutjanus campechanus), white snapper (Lutjanus griseus), yellowtail snapper (Ocyurus chrysurus) and black clam (Anadara spp.). Several of these species are migratory to the sea and are an important contribution to the composition of rural fisheries, frequently exploited by the marine fishing industry.

### Gender-sensitive development impacts

222. The project will contribute directly to increasing the empowerment of women and other vulnerable groups in mangrove-dependent communities through their participation and leadership in organizations and enterprises. See G.2 and Annex B.

D.4. Needs of recipient (max. 900 words, approximately 1 page)

223. Economic growth in Ecuador has directly reduced poverty and inequality levels and increased the size of the middle class. Between 2007 and 2016, poverty decreased from 36.7% to 23.2%, while extreme poverty decreased from 9.5% to 4.8%. Inequality, measured by Gini coefficient, improved from 0.55 in 2007 to 0.47 in 2018.

224. However, the economy is still heavily dependent on the oil sector—a vulnerability demonstrated in recent years with the fall of global crude prices and the appreciation of the US dollar. The reduction of this dependency has been part of the national strategies implemented by the central government.

With more than 6,500 km of coastline, approximately 54% of Ecuador's population of 17 million people live in coastal areas. These populations are highly vulnerable to SDR due to the associated risks of saltwater intrusion and storm surges, flooding and storms.

These trends put coastal areas in Ecuador at particular risk. With 45% of its population below the extreme poverty line, these communities are particularly vulnerable to climate change impacts, with increasing numbers of predominantly poor and vulnerable people in the coastal areas living in unsafe housing, in part due to lack of access to land tenure security.

227. Beyond the basic challenges of coastal development, the risks of climate change are placing additional stresses on the Government of Ecuador's efforts to achieve sustainable development. Despite the high level of commitment by the government and its agencies, there is a need for greater coordination among mangroves and wetlands to address the combined future impact of sea-level rise and intensified storm surges.

The project addresses the needs of the government of Ecuador, coastal communities, and important environmental resources in the context of climate change as described in the National Climate Change Strategy and the National Climate Change Plan.

D.S. Country ownership (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

230. The project is fully aligned with national plans to combat climate change and with a set of national policies and strategies related to climate policies, the project is well-aligned with major new initiatives announced by the Government of Ecuador in March 2023 to protect its entire coastal and marine resources. These include the National Climate Change Strategy (2012-2025), the National Heritage and Natural Resources Act, and the National Forest Law.

The MAATE is the national environmental authority in charge of designing environmental policies and coordinating strategies, programs, and projects for the conservation of ecosystems and the sustainable use of natural resources. The project will be implemented by the MAATE with technical support based on the conservation and adequate use of biodiversity and natural resources. The GCF Focal Point (GCF FP) is the lead executing agency.

231. MAATE has been involved in the whole process of the development of the proposal, including development of the PFP application, public consultations and review of drafts of the funding proposal. Several different bodies have been consulted on this project: Ministry of Environment, National Heritage and Natural Resources (now included within Natural Heritage), and former Coastal and Marine Resources.

The protection of mangroves in the context of climate risk management in coastal areas is included as a priority under the following national strategies:

- National Climate Change Strategy 2012-2025: The proposed project directly addresses seven of the nine objectives outlined.
- MAATE prioritizes addressing the multi-billion-dollar cost of extreme climate events and recognizes mangroves as an important component in coastal risk management.

Mangroves are declared as forest and protective vegetation under Ecuadorian law and are an integral part of the country's REDD+ efforts.

A cost of US$18 per ICCa, which is in the typical range for avoided deforestation type mitigation projects.

In addition, this project will be bringing multiple adaptation benefits to a wide range of people, including direct beneficiaries (e.g., coastal communities) and indirect beneficiaries (e.g., flood protection). ## Cost Per Person

The cost per person is relatively low because its primary goals are to secure public goods in the form of the ecosystem services that mangroves provide to achieve mitigation and adaptation benefits. Most of these activities to improve livelihoods are directed at poor communities that can play an essential role in improving mangrove conservation.

## Economic Analysis

An Economic Analysis for the Project is provided as Annex 3a (model in Excel) and 3b (narrative) and considers three scenarios: a baseline scenario, a project scenario with only proposed mangrove conservation activities, and a project scenario that includes all proposed mangrove conservation activities. The economic benefits of the project outweigh the costs by a factor of 4.7. Further, the "project" scenario as presented in Annex 3b is robust to changes in key assumptions (e.g., discount rate) and other scenarios considered (based on the highest NPV, benefits to cost ratio and economic internal rate of return). The three scenarios are described below.

In the baseline scenario, a scenario that only included the project's proposed mangrove conservation activities to reduce deforestation and improve livelihoods, the economic benefits were US$10.5 million over 20 years for these activities. The high economic value of the proposed project reflects the very high economic value of mangroves in terms of their role as a critical habitat for fish and other marine species that are key to food production, which underpins the livelihoods of the coastal farmer families that make up much of the vulnerable population.

The model (Annex 3a) can be adjusted to consider the impacts of larger changes.

E.7 Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

240. The Monitoring and Evaluation (M&E) Plan is provided as Annex 11. The plan includes GCF-level Impact and Outcome indicators based on the RIMF and Project-level Outcome and Activity indicators, metrics for each sub-indicator, and a description of how these will be measured. The plan also outlines the quantitative resources needed to collect this data. The project monitoring and evaluation will be coordinated by a dedicated M&E unit at CCi-Ecofunds, in close collaboration with the GCF Project Officer, C40 partners and other CI units contributing to the project. The M&E unit will ensure that all required data is collected and implemented in accordance with the GCF Evaluation Policy, including technical results and financial data as necessary and appropriate. These requirements may include additional monitoring activities beyond those outlined in Annex 11. Specific monitoring requirements will be established based on their roles. The monitoring data collected by these partners will be aggregated for project level reporting.

241. The Project will monitor the achievement of GHD emissions reductions and vulnerability reduction as outlined in sections E2 to E6 will be monitored by the Project’s M&E specialist (in coordination with staff at partner organizations) using the tools described above. Monitoring activities may include more frequent or less frequent as outlined in the Monitoring and Evaluation Plan (Annex 11). The M&E specialist will be assisted by project staff working within CCi and other CI units on gender and safeguards issues, blue carbon and project impact evaluation.

242. The M&E Plan is a living document, follows the templates specified by GCF, and focuses on measuring the progress towards achieving the outcomes of the project. The M&E plan will be reviewed and updated during the inception stage of the project (the first six months of implementation) by the M&E specialist and in collaboration with the Project Officer and other relevant partners. The M&E Plan will be reviewed again when the M&E requirements are fully assimilated and that the plan is owned by the project staff. The full M&E plan will take into account the GCF Evaluation Policy, including the use of a three-point scale rating for contributions to a paradigm shift in the enabling conditions using a three-point scale rating as specified in the GCF Evaluation Policy. Recommendations from the Interim Evaluation will be incorporated into the design of the Project to secure maximum impact.

243. The Project Steering Committee will meet annually to review and approve annual budget and workplans, discuss progress towards achieving project outcomes, and make decisions on resource allocation with key partner networks. The NDA will participate in these meetings as a member of the project steering committee.

244. CCi-GCF Agency staff at AE, will continuously monitor the work of the EE through quarterly EE reports and monitoring visits. These reports will be submitted to the GCF Project Officer for review and submission of an Annual Report to monitor project progress and report to the GCF Agency. The report will summarize the annual progress towards achieving the outcomes of the project.

245. The Project will undergo an Independent Interim Evaluation in Year 4, consistent with GCF’s Evaluation Policy. The evaluation will determine progress being made towards the achievement of outcomes and outputs and will show how well the project is performing against its objectives. The evaluation will also assess contributions to a paradigm shift in the enabling conditions using a three-point scale rating as specified in the GCF Evaluation Policy. Recommendations from the Interim Evaluation will be incorporated into the design of the Project to secure maximum impact.

246. An independent Final Evaluation will be conducted at the end of the Project and will be undertaken in accordance with the AE’s guidance and consistent with GCF’s Evaluation Policy. The Final evaluation will focus on the extent to which the project has achieved its outcomes, outputs and objectives (and any other if any such correction took place). Once again, it will include an assessment of the project's contributions to a paradigm shift in the enabling conditions using a three-point scale rating.
