# RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## F.1 Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages)

### Selected Risk Factor 1: A changing climate may impact the feasibility, sustainability or success of the project.

#### Category | Probability | Impact
------------|-------------|-----------------
Technical and operational | High | Loss

**Description**

The base line study confirms that Ecuador's coastal ecosystems are susceptible to climate change impacts. However, the impact on the project activities themselves is considered low. It is one of the main aims of the project to increase the resilience of the mangrove ecosystem to the impacts of climate change. The main climate change impacts expected in the project area include sea level rise (SLR), increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events such as hurricanes, and changes in temperature and precipitation patterns.

### Selected Risk Factor 2: A changing climate may impact the adaptation opportunities provided by the project.

#### Category | Probability | Impact
------------|-------------|-----------------
Technical and operational | Medium | Low

**Description**

One climate change impact that could influence the adaptation opportunities provided by the project is SLR, which could influence the area available for reforestation. The base line study included an assessment of the vulnerability of the mangrove ecosystem to SLR. It was expected in all parts of the project area, but the Guayas Estuary was identified as having the highest risk.

The vulnerability assessment included in the baseline study included the history of SLR, particularly development of mangroves in response to SLR, and the current status of mangroves in Ecuador (in 2014). It was found that mangrove reforestation projects have shown strong resilience against SLR. Mangroves are considered a protective barrier against future climate change impacts such as coastal erosion, and even protecting themselves against future SLR (Spalding et al., 2014; Vargas et al., 2015).

### Selected Risk Factor 3: There are risks to the project area from potential third party action.

**Mitigation Measure(s)**

The project addresses this risk through Activity 1.1.1 under which stakeholder meetings will be conducted as part of the baseline study and during the implementation phase, in order to identify and mitigate future impacts on mangrove reforestation, considering the views of men and women, for use in the final confirmation of sites for reforestation, especially in the Guayas River Estuary.


---
**Page 90**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 83

## Category: Technical and operational
### Probability: Medium
### Impact: Loss

**Description:** The project area suffers from pollution from cities and their productive activities, agricultural runoff, the proximity of large ports, and the presence of a battery in the coastal zone. These factors could significantly impact a part of the project area although they are likely to be very localized and involve small areas (with respect to the scale of the overall project site). The impacts on water quality and other environmental resources lie outside of the control of the project at the same time, they further emphasize the need to increase the resilience of the mangrove ecosystem.

## Mitigation Measure(s)
The project already addresses these issues as follows:
- **Activity 1.2.1** and **2.1.3**: To introduce climate-smart approaches to existing economic activities or bio-enterprises based on climate-smart approaches, which would lead both to less contamination from industrial activities and to the introduction of new productive activities.
- **Activity 3.2.1**: Includes work with local governments, municipalities, and prefectures that are responsible for monitoring water quality in the project area.

## Selected Risk Factor 4: Project staff could be at risk due to tensions between certain actors within the project area and their inability to resolve these as a result of project implementation.

### Category: Technical and operational
### Probability: Medium
### Impact: Medium

**Description:** In the project area, there are tensions between stakeholders and the government, because some do not accept the existing rules. The project aims to work especially with local associations, which may lead to a further divide between members and non-members. Tensions between stakeholders within the project could disrupt activities if some stakeholders do not wish to participate.

Furthermore, there is the risk of criminal gangs operating in remote mangrove areas. Ecuador's remote coastal areas are also subject to illegal fishing, poaching, and other forms of environmental crime. The risks are particularly high along the coast and to Europe. The risk exists throughout the project area but is more acute in the northern estuaries and some areas of the Guayas estuary. Violence between criminal gangs and with law enforcement is common, making it important that the project works closely with local authorities and other stakeholders to ensure compliance with laws and regulations as well as law enforcement agencies.

Despite these risks, the project team believes that the risk to project staff is a result of existing tensions is considered to be low and in case of actual security issues, CI's Security and Protection Plan applies to CI staff as well as site project partners. CI already has a presence in this region, and there have been no gaps in operations, even with additional security personnel. The project team will work closely with local authorities and other stakeholders to ensure compliance with laws and regulations, with a close relationship with the government and also with local partners, including communities where the project is implemented.

The project team has established a grievance redress mechanism that allows them to identify and address risks quickly, efficiently, with the safety of CI partners and beneficiaries as the first priority. CI and partners also have security plans in place, which are regularly reviewed by CI's Security Team to ensure they remain up-to-date and provide CI with early warnings when conditions begin to deteriorate.

In addition, the projects likely to reduce existing tensions through its planned activities. The established project-specific grievance redress mechanism will allow for complaints to be made at any time and will be able to track where tensions are aggravated, and responses will be identified on a case-by-case basis to address these tensions.

## Mitigation Measure(s)
For the potential risk of tensions between stakeholders within the project area, the project-specific Grievance Redress Mechanism to track grievances related to tensions between stakeholders and between stakeholders and the project team. The team will initiate spaces and procedures for stakeholder dialogue where tensions between stakeholders and the project team can be addressed in a timely manner.

The project ESMP also includes the requirement to screen all the community grant support that will be provided.

**Page 91**
---

and consider how the grant could create is to be assessed in the screening and mitigation measures developed if the risks are medium or high level.

For the work with AUSECM organizations, CUSUCE will also be encouraging AUSECM organizations to include more members to maximize participation of fishers within the associations and reduce the potential for conflict between members and non-members.

To address safety risks posed by criminal activity, CUSUCE's Director for Safety and Security will conduct a thorough security assessment and mitigation plan during project start-up. Example mitigation measures include pre-activity community engagement meetings with local authorities to identify high-risk areas and develop risk reduction strategies; efforts to combat criminal gangs; Hostile Environment Awareness training and guidance on avoiding and reporting criminal activity; and the development of a database to identify CUSUCE staff and partners as contributing to project objectives (other than criminal or law-enforcement activity).

An additional mitigation measure will be an emphasis on selection of consultants and subgrantees who have a demonstrated track record in working with communities in areas prone to natural disasters. The majority of the two northern sites, is well known in the northern areas and familiar with working in those regions and with local communities.

A Security Plan has been developed for this project and is included as an Appendix 11 of the ESFP (Annex E).

## Selected Risk Factor 5 - The project area is susceptible to earthquakes, subsidence, landslides, erosion, flooding or extreme climatic conditions.

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | High | Low |

**Description**

The project area is sensitive to erosion, flooding or extreme weather conditions, for example in El Niño or La Niña years. These natural extreme events have the potential to disrupt project operations; however, the impacts on project operations are expected to be low since the issues are likely to be relatively short-term by comparison with the length of the project.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

The project includes several activities that mitigate these measures themselves, i.e., they can jointly reduce the overall impact of such extreme weather events and minimize the potential for their impacts.
- Local people's livelihoods will be improved through the development of new bio-enterprises (sub-activity 1.2); and
- The resilience and extent of the mangrove ecosystem will be increased, thus reducing the risk of impacts from erosion, flooding or extreme climatic conditions; and
- Improve local planning, including identification of areas at risk of flooding and identification of priority natural infrastructure to protect and restore.

Linked to the restriction activities (and related to risk factor 2 above), CUSUCE and CUSUCE will consult with local stakeholders on the potential impacts of the influence of extreme weather events is taken into consideration in the establishment of bio-enterprises. If relevant (e.g., regarding the selection of sites to establish mangrove nurseries).

## Selected Risk Factor 6 - Political instability and changes in government agencies

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Medium | Low |

**Description**

The political stability index has shown a steady improvement over the last decade and was at -0.2 to 2014. The index measures perceptions of the likelihood of Ecuador's government becoming destabilized.


---
**Page 92**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 85

**E**

Political risk in Ecuador is characterized by possible changes in government personnel in key positions or changing mandates of different ministries because of elections. One example is that the administration of mangrove concessions has been transferred from the Ministry of Environment to the Ministry of Agriculture and Livestock.

Since some of the Outcome 3 activities relate to working at the subnational governments, changes at those levels could also impact the project operations. The main risk is that changes in personnel and/or government functions could cause delays or disruptions in project implementation. Another example is the change from a FAS-Ecuador funding structure or rules, which may impact its implementation of Outcome 3. FAS has taken several steps to enhance good governance practices within the organization and has developed a set of internal procedures and best practice standards for conservation trust funds. A risk remains that some of these practices could be changed under a new administration.

A snap general election for both the executive and legislative branches took place in August 2023. All the leading political parties and candidates embraced environmental issues as key pillars of their platform so it seems unlikely that there will be any significant changes to the government’s priorities or policies on this issue, which is one of the key focus areas of this proposal. The regulatory frameworks and programs around which the proposed project is built are also likely to remain unchanged in the short term because they have been developed within existing constitutional and regulatory frameworks and are unlikely to be impacted. Although ministries may change following the election, there will still be continuity at the level of the FAS-Ecuador team as well as at the local government levels throughout numerous previous administrations and through many different ministers. Local and provincial officials who have worked with FAS for a number of years also remain in place.

The new administration is expected to remain in place until 2027, which will be sufficient time for the project implementation period. However, there remains some uncertainty as to whether the new administration will continue to prioritize the sector. Local and provincial elections will happen during the project implementation period but CI Ecuador’s strong relations with those governments are expected to provide continuity for the project. A new administration has also been elected in the United States which may impact the level of funding available from USAID and other sector partners.

### Mitigation Measures

CI has been able to maintain its activities during the last 20 years in the political context of Ecuador and has been able to collaborate consistently with successive administrations and with subnational governments. Elections were held in August 2023, but there is no certainty that this will result in a change in government priorities or policies.

All relevant ministries have been involved in the development of this project, including participation during Project Preparation funding activities of Funding Proposal development. The project has been designed to address governance issues and includes a capacity building component for FAS-Ecuador. CI Ecuador continues to work with the ministries involved. MATE is even providing co-financing for the project. In the case of any changes in ministry personnel, CI will continue its extensive engagement with the government to ensure a smooth transition and to adduce possible changes as appropriate.

The risk that a new administration would alter FAS’s governing structure or rules is considered low risk and in due diligence there are some measures highlighted to design and implement before the granting period. There are internal procedures for managing change of personnel, including the possibility of use of GCF funds. The project budget also includes support for capacity building and enforcement support to FAS.

The risk presented by changing priorities and approaches from new administrations warrants monitoring, although it has been reduced since FAS’s inception due to concerted outreach by FAS and engagement with the donor community. It is expected that any changes in government priorities would be met with additional reforms to FAS so soon after the Government of Ecuador helped restructure it in 2014.

Of note, the vast majority of Fund decisions are made by an Administrative Board (Junta Administrativa) or “JA” for short. The JA is a group of representatives from various donor agencies and organizations that have signed agreements with donors; these agreements are largely determined according to the requirements in such agreements, and largely operate independent of the FAS Board of Directors. The governance structure of each one of these JAs is defined in their governing agreement. The JA is responsible for approving all projects under its mandate. FAS-Ecuador is primarily responsible for supporting the execution of projects, making decisions on fund allocations and disbursements, consistent with the terms of any donor agreement. The JAs are responsible for the evaluation and oversight of donor

## Selected Risk Factor 7: Prohibited Practices, Including ML/FT

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Prohibited practices | Low | Medium |

### Description

The Project involves a wide array of actors, including government, sub-grantees, vendors and service providers, and beneficiaries. These actors are subject to various national laws and regulations that prohibit certain activities in their country for GCF prohibited practices, including terrorist financing and money laundering. CI Ecuador follows and is subject to the same rules as other entities operating within its jurisdiction. The Project also involves sub-grantees which have been assessed as effective by the GCF. In addition, CI Ecuador is subject to national laws related to AML/CFT, such as the Law 104 of 2005 on Money Laundering and Financial Terrorism, the Anti-Money Laundering Regulation to the Prevention and Detection of Laundering Money and Financing Terrorism, CI-Ecuador's own internal policies (e.g., GCF Policy on Prohibited Practices), and the United Nations Office on Drugs and Crime [UNODC], which conducts a thorough review of financial procedures related to AML/CFT and to which CI-Ecuador must submit annual reports. All sub-grantees and service providers, including sub-grantees of CI Ecuador, sub-grantees PIESA and FIAS, were screened and cleared against international sanctions lists.

Concerning impact, serious breaches in prohibited practices, including ML/FT, would impact the reputation of CI and the project.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

CI has several procedures and controls in place to prevent, mitigate, and identify risk. In order to comply with GCF AML/CFT requirements, CI Ecuador must ensure that all sub-grantees are subject to the same rules as other entities operating within its jurisdiction. CI also conducts an assessment of Prohibited Practices (PP) risks of grant recipients and includes any required mitigation measures in its legally binding grant agreements. All EE staff and subgrantees must also undergo training on AML/CFT requirements, including GCF policies and procedures. The Project will ensure that all sub-grantees (and their service providers/sub-grantees) must include language on compliance with the GCF Policy on Prohibited Practices, including AMICLFT Sub-Grantee Agreement, in their respective grant agreements. CI Ecuador will also monitor compliance of EE staff and sub-grantees with GCF policies and mitigation measures as part of project monitoring, including site visits and regular reporting.

To report and remedy risks, any CI employee; employee of an executing entity, grantee, partner, or vendor; or beneficiary may report a grievance or violation of these policies anonymously online by phone through CI's Ethics Hotline at 01-800-025-4367 or in person to the CI Ecuador office. The matter will be referred to Investigations for Investigations.

Full details can be found on CI’s website at https://www.conservation.org/about-us/privacy-policy#privacy

## Selected Risk Factor 8: Unrealized or delayed co-financing

### Description

Co-financing plays an important part in achieving the full impact of the proposed project. There is a medium risk of delayed or unrealized co-financing for the project, because MAATE’s grant co-financing depends on the approval process and funding availability from the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources (MENR). If this does not happen, the negative impact to the project would be limited to this activity. In addition, a portion of the co-financing committed by MAATE is in-kind and must be staff time. There is also some risk of non-mobilization.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

The project team will jointly assess the degree of risk (both likelihood and impact) that co-financing will be funded and mobilized on time at project inception. The Project Team will maintain regular engagement with co-financing sources to understand and manage this risk. In the event that co-financing is not realized, CI will try to identify alternative sources. If that is not possible, CI would work with GCF within the framework of the project’s PFA to explore adaptive

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 87

## Selected Risk Factor 9: Risk of inflation

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Low |

### Description

Although currently decreasing, Ecuador's inflation rate for 2023 was 3.7% according to the International Monetary Fund’s Data Mapper (April 2023). Ecuador's average inflation over the period 2015 to 2022 was 1.8%, with a median of 1.4%. The IMF forecast for inflation in Ecuador is 3.6% in 2023, which is above its historical range but below the global price of oil, on which Ecuador's economy is highly reliant. The IMF’s forecast for inflation in Ecuador is 1.34% in 2025 and 1.78% in 2026. Given these forecasts, the risk of inflation is considered a medium risk to this project as it could impact the budget, co-financing, and activity implementation. High inflation rates would be accompanied by high interest rates at financial institutions, which may make it difficult for private sector shrimp farm companies taking loans to invest in climate smart shrimp practices (Activity 2.1.2).

### Mitigation Measure(s)

To mitigate for the impacts of inflation, CII has made adjustments into its budget figures. The budget has been prepared assuming an inflation rate of 3%. The 3% figure is above the IMF’s forecast but given the challenges of inflation in Ecuador and the global price of oil, it is considered a prudent assumption to make. The project team will regularly monitor inflation pressures on the project budget and continually seek to identify cost savings where possible.

## 243. The Environmental and Social Risk Screening for the project indicated that a limited-and-adequate Environmental and Social Impact Assessment (ESIA) should be conducted to identify potential project risks. This assessment resulted in a category B risk rating.

### 249. The project is risk category B (moderate-risk).

## 250. An Environmental and Social Impact Assessment and Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP), included as Annex E, includes a summary of the project and the applicable legal and institutional framework; the proposed project activities; potential environmental and social impacts; mitigation measures and implementation modalities. Also included are a Grievance Redress Mechanism as well as:

- Process Framework for Restriction of Access to Natural Resources:

  - Choza Pescadores
  - Stakeholder Engagement Plan (Annex T); and

## 251. The project does not intend to cause negative environmental or social impacts; on the contrary, it aims to increase the resilience of the mangrove ecosystem and benefit local communities. It is based on rights-based and community approaches, including measures to ensure the participation of vulnerable groups, such as women and youth.

## 252. Some risks are, by their nature, beyond the control of the project. For example, climate change may impact the primary forest areas in the project area and increase mangrove ecosystems from pollution in some places. The project includes a variety of activities to address these issues.

- Activity 1.2.1: Community-based mangrove restoration (CBMR) and reforestation.
- Activity 1.2.2: Community-based mangrove restoration (CBMR) and reforestation.

The resulting map (see Annex D) defines a potential of 10,000 ha while the project includes ±4,850 ha as a target to be achieved by 2030. The CBMR and reforestation activities will be developed in close collaboration with communities and will be implemented through community-managed projects. A Community Management Plan for Alto-Descentdant Communities and Choza Pescadores will be developed as part of the reforestation activities including assessment of risks and measures to avoid identified risks.

#### G2: Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

255. During Project preparation, a gender assessment (see Annex B) is undertaken to understand the differential impacts of the proposed project on women and men, including those with disabilities and other vulnerable groups. The gender assessment and an analysis of the proposed project design, a Gender Action Plan (GAP) was developed. The development of this GAP will be informed by a review of relevant national legislation, policy and programs in the country, as well as key informant interviews, household questionnaires, focus group discussions and consultation workshops with project stakeholders and partners.

256. The CAP (see Annex B) is underpinned by a gender-sensitive baseline survey (GBS) and the included actions are largely concentrated in the support of marginal associations where direct collaboration will be established. The GBS will also inform the development of the GAP, which will include specific agreements that integrate gender considerations, tailored and specific support to women in marginalized communities, and the inclusion of women in decision-making processes. This will be achieved through the adoption of diversified and climate resilient livelihoods options linked to mangroves, and ensuring that project activities are designed with a focus on women’s needs, capacities and the importance of mitigating gender inequalities in the project, including gender-based violence.

257. To support implementation of the GAP, the CAP will employ a full-time Gender Manager to ensure robust gender mainstreaming across all aspects of the project. The Gender Manager will be responsible for providing appropriate capacity building for colleagues and delivery partners that is well-designed, clear, tailored to the context and sustainable in nature. The Gender Manager will also provide technical support on issues related to gender outcomes; meet with women/men’s groups, collect qualitative information, help project teams to prioritize gender considerations, and ensure that gender equality is integrated into all aspects of the project, including estuaries and, in particular, the social coordinators in the implementation of the GAP. The manager will also collaborate with the Gender focal point at the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources (MENR) and will be supported by Ctr’s Global Gender Advisor. The Gender Manager position will be based in Guayaquil City.

258. A complex set of historical, social, cultural, and economic factors influence the project site: current gender norms reinforce traditional roles that limit women’s participation in decision-making processes; lack of access to reproductive labor impinging on their ability to engage in community, organizational, and project activities, and finally high rates of gender-based violence that further highlights inequalities and is an important barrier that

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 90

## keep women from exercising their voice and agency in mangrove conservation.

This situation is compounded by a number of structural problems associated with education, health, the provision of utilities and limited employment opportunities.

### 259. In these mangrove-dependent communities, women are more vulnerable and experience higher rates of poverty than men. They tend to control the main economic activity in the communities: the harvesting and sale of seafood. Often it is men who hold formal positions of leadership within communities and fishing associations.

#### 260. Women generally have less access to resources and information than those in the north. In the north, women often walk (often collectively) to the mangroves and are therefore more actively engaged: they collect seeds, cut branches for firewood, and harvest fruit. In contrast, in the south, women's access and use of the mangrove areas is constrained by distance as the mangroves are far from the communities where they live. Women also have less access to resources than men because they are largely absent from the associations; mangrove associations in the south have ~15% women members.

#### 261. Women living in mangroves work long days, which include family caregiving responsibilities, harvesting or fishing activities and other domestic chores. In the north, women spend an average of two to three hours per day on these tasks; little time is spent on their caregiving or reproductive tasks. By contrast, men have much more flexible working hours (typically four and five hours). This means that once their day's fishing or harvesting is over, they are relieved from work.

#### 262. Rates of GGR in very high (~85%, in project sites) and could be perpetuated/increased by climate change impacts which may exacerbate economic disruption.

### G.3 Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximatively | page)

#### 264. The project will be executed by C-Governance; and therefore, financial management and procurement for this project and the associated sub-grants will be carried out in accordance with relevant provisions in the Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA) signed between C-Governance and the CGI.

##### Financial management:

#### 265. CGI uses Unit4 Business World software suite as its enterprise resource planning (ERP) system for financial accounting, reporting, budgeting and all its global operations. This system supports financial monitoring, reporting, human resources, procurement, and record-keeping in line with the requirements of CGI's AMA. It supports a system of ledger accounts and sub-ledger accounts to ensure that all transactions are recorded accurately. CGI also uses a coding system for monitoring and reporting of projects. In addition, all EAS and sub-grantees will be required to use CGI's ERP system as part of their financial management.

#### 266. An annual audit of the project will be conducted by an independent external audit firm in accordance with audit standards under U.S. GAAP and furnished to the CGI in accordance with Cis' AMA.

#### 267. Disbursements made by CGI will be recorded to the ledger account for this GCP Fund, which can be backed up using the CGI ERP system. The CGI ERP system is also used to make CGI's report of acceptable progress and financial reports any other requirements as included in the applicable grant agreement.

#### 268. Cis has procedures and controls in place to prevent risk of prohibited practices, including ML/FT (collectively: "PP"). CGI applies rigorous CFTAML screening to all CGI funding recipients. CGI also assesses PP risks of grant recipients through due diligence and ongoing monitoring. CGI will provide training on the identification and reporting of PP (including red flags and how to report violations), and it grant and services agreements (used by both CGI and its sub-grantees) include a clause requiring compliance with Cis' policies and procedures regarding PP. Compliance with CGI policies and mitigation measures is part of project monitoring, including site visits and audits.

# Secretariat's assessment of FP235

## Proposal name: Mangroves for Climate: Public, Private and Community Partnerships for Mitigation and Adaptation in Ecuador

### Accredited entity:
Conservation International Foundation (CI)

### Country:
Ecuador

### Project/programme size:
Small

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| **Mangroves model:** The project involves government, local communities and the private sector in supporting community-based mangrove management teams undertaking income generation while restoring and sustainably managing mangroves through a results-based payment scheme. In addition to mitigating climate change impacts, they provide ecosystem services that protect coastal communities and upstream communities from flooding caused by floods and sea level rise.<br><br>**Community grants:** The project is working with a community grant programme that aims to empower local communities to sustainably manage large areas of mangroves via community-based management agreements that grant reward payments for their management activities. |  |
| **Transformation in the private sector:** The project will accelerate the transformation of the shrimp industry from being a driver of mangrove destruction and degradation or preservation, while shrimp farmers will be able to earn a premium price for certified shrimp cultivation. |  |


---
**Page 107**
---

# Financial sustainability model

The project will help to capitalize the trust fund subaccount on results-based payments for community mangrove management, with additional funding expected internally from private and government sources. The trust fund is expected to run in perpetuity, hence ensuring long-term project implementation and even beyond the 20-year project lifespan.

## Scalability and replicability

The project may allow Ecuador to become a demonstration site for climate-smart shrimp farming practices that can be replicated across different regions, especially due to the project's link with the Global Mangrove Alliance.

### Summary of the Secretary’s assessment

#### Project background

1. The project background, as set out in the funding proposal, underscores the pressing need to address the impacts of climate change on Ecuador’s coastal regions. Climate change projections indicate an increase in sea level rise and more frequent and intense coastal flooding, that threaten local communities and economic activities. Mangroves play a crucial role as natural buffers against these effects; however, they are under threat from various human risks. Despite recent conservation efforts, mangrove losses continue, with adverse consequences for carbon sequestration and biodiversity.

2. The project’s components aim to address these challenges by engaging local communities, the private sector and government entities in the protection and restoration of the mangroves. These actions will contribute to reducing coastal flooding, enhancing efforts to reduce greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions, to enhance flood protection for vulnerable coastal populations, and to increase resilience against climate change impacts. The project aligns with climate change strategies and contributes to global initiatives such as the Global Mangrove Alliance.

3. The project’s components focus on increasing the area of mangroves under community-managed restoration activities in the province of Manabí. This will be achieved through improved governance frameworks. By integrating these efforts, the project aims to achieve multiple climate change mitigation and adaptation benefits including reducing GHG emissions, protecting ecosystems and enhancing infrastructure resilience.

4. Moreover, the project’s alignment with the objectives of the Global Mangrove Alliance underscores its potential for knowledge-sharing and global impact by leveraging international partnerships and best practices; the project aims to contribute significantly to global efforts

## Component-by-component analysis

### Component 1: Mangrove areas under affective and climate-adapted management increased, including through community-based management (ASCEM), and with protected areas implementing climate adaptation plans (total cost: USD 26 million; co-fund: USD 15.5 million)

#### Output 1.1 Focus on reducing flood risk for vulnerable populations by increasing mangrove cover and associated benefits

Activities include providing training, technical assistance and equipment to ASCEM associations, as well as the development of a national application process for incentives under the country's Socio Manglar (SM) programme.

#### Output 1.2 Aim to improve livelihood activities and economically productive community-based management practices through mangrove conservation and restoration

This involves providing technical and business development support to mangrove community associations, including access to microfinance services. Financial mechanisms will be established and consolidated to support micro- and small-scale


---
**Page 109**
---

### Component 2: The private sector becomes a transformational agent for change by reducing GHG emissions and providing financial support to conserve and restore mangroves that increase climate resilience (USD 50 million)

#### The project aims to engage the private sector in promoting improved shrimp farming methods to reduce mangrove deforestation and advance mangrove conservation and restoration in Ecuador. In the accredited entity (AE), it will collaborate with Ecuador's National Fund for Climate Change Adaptation (FONACICA) to promote sustainable shrimp farming, including mangrove restoration on farms. The project will also engage private sector entities across private finance.

#### Seed funding will be provided by the Aquaculture Stewardship Council and CITES established in 2018 through Ecuador's Socio-Bosque Fund to support the SM incentive payment. The project aims to catalyze investment in CSS farming methods, leading to reduced deforestation and increased mangrove restoration.

#### Technical assistance will be provided to shrimp farmers for adopting SSP and CSS standards, with a focus on sustainable intensification and mangrove restoration. The project will facilitate access to financing from the Ecuadorian government's Sustainable Practices Fund, including through the Eco Business Fund. It will also establish agreements with businesses in the private sector to provide technical support and financial incentives.

#### In addition, the project will disseminate knowledge through various platforms and workshops, develop a marketing strategy for CSS farming, and seek to mobilize private investment from the private sector. The project aims to strengthen private sector engagement in mangrove conservation and restoration while promoting sustainable shrimp farming practices that reduce deforestation. It is expected to create a long-term financial mechanism that attracts private sector investment and provides incentives for mangrove conservation.

### Component 3: Create enabling conditions for accelerating reductions in mangrove deforestation and increase mangrove restoration by strengthening governance, climate change adaptation strategies, and the legal framework (USD 54 million, GEF cost USD 5.5 million)

#### Actions by local communities and the private sector to protect mangroves and maintain forest productivity as a nature-based climate change adaptation and mitigation strategy are contingent upon the Government of Ecuador establishing the necessary governance, regulatory frameworks, and enforcement mechanisms for controlling illegal activities and ensuring that climate considerations are integrated into planning and investments.

#### By providing better life climate and mangrove forest information to decision-makers and stakeholders, particularly focusing on public entities, the project will directly contribute to increasing mangrove conservation and restoration. This will also provide a framework for achieving project outcomes related to mangrove management and climate resilience.

#### Improved legal and regulatory capacity, focusing on planning, preparedness, and enforcement, will support sustaining mangroves and enhancing awareness and resilience

among beneficiaries, primarily in the eight priority municipalities of the project area. This will directly contribute to strengthening institutional and regulatory systems for climate-resilience planning and development.

To achieve these objectives, the project includes several activities under output 3.1, such as monitoring weather conditions and socioeconomic impacts, integrating remote sensing and community participation for emissions reduction monitoring, and conducting socioeconomic impact assessments. These activities are designed to address adaptation needs at both local and sectoral level, incorporating climate resilience and mitigation strategies, and enforcing regulations.

b. Activities under output 1.2 include supporting local governments to improve planning instruments that integrate climate change adaptation and mangrove management, strengthening the capacity of communities for climate-resilient livelihoods, and generating proposals for regulatory changes in various sectors. This will involve analyzing legal frameworks and developing a policy brief on climate resilience and mangroves at both national and sectoral level.

In addition, the project will develop an online and mobile communications application (app) for reporting deforestation and other impacts on mangroves, thereby enhancing transparency and enforcement efforts.


## Assessment against investment criteria

### 3.1 Impact potential

The project aims to directly benefit 99,600 individuals, with an additional 3.4 million indirect beneficiaries. Furthermore, it targets an estimated 450 GWh emission reduction of 4,678 tons CO2e.

The AISEM project seeks to address both adaptation and mitigation challenges posed by climate change, particularly in vulnerable coastal regions. By leveraging the protective capacities of mangroves, this project will provide multiple benefits for both local communities and the global environment. This project represents a multifaceted approach to addressing these challenges, recognizing that mangroves play a crucial role in fostering resilience and mitigating environmental degradation.

Adaptation impacts: Direct adaptation benefits include reduced flooding risk and increased food security through improved livelihoods. These benefits are particularly significant for select municipalities with high mangrove cover and populations at risk of flooding.

Potential benefits for 89,600 people in mitigating flood risks, according to flood risk modeling estimates:

- A USD 250 million per year reduction in economic asset losses caused by extreme climate-related events.
- Approximately 3.4 million indirect beneficiaries from improved planning processes in mangrove management.
- 41,500 individuals (50 per cent female, 50 per cent male) expected to adopt climate-resilient livelihoods or experience reduced flood risk;
- 156,613 hectares of mangroves protected and strengthened, covering all of Ecuador's mangroves.


---
**Page 111**
---

### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential

This project aims to transform Ecuador’s approach to valuing and utilizing critical natural infrastructure, particularly mangrove ecosystems, to protect coastal communities from flooding and erosion. By integrating mangroves into national development strategies and economic frameworks, the project offers an innovative solution to reducing GHG emissions and enhancing the livelihoods of vulnerable populations living in areas most at risk due to underlying ecosystem risks and ecosystem services provided by mangroves, which is the result of inadequate information, institutional coordination and private sector investment. The project will create a conducive environment for policy dialogue across sectors, fostering public-private partnerships and empowering local communities, with a focus on leveraging the “green infrastructure” of mangroves. This approach is cost-effective and scalable, providing an opportunity to protect coastal communities threatened by climate change. Comprehensive monitoring and reporting mechanisms will be established to track progress against project milestones, with mid-term and final evaluations to identify lessons learned and disseminate findings.

The project aims to create an enabling environment and policy framework by strengthening the capacity of local communities and institutions across relevant sectors at the national and subnational level. Capacity-building initiatives will empower local communities to participate in decision-making processes related to mangrove conservation, with support from local government. The project emphasizes consensus-building among stakeholders through participatory approaches that ensure the inclusion of marginalized groups in progress. Private sector participation will be enhanced through support for land-use planning and the adoption of sustainable land management practices.

The project focuses on scaling up successful mangrove conservation and restoration activities in Ecuador, addressing the lack of basic land-use planning instruments in the project area. Partnerships with local communities and institutions will be strengthened at both national government, with recommendations for legal and regulatory adjustments as needed.


### 3.3 Sustainable development potential

#### Environmental co-benefits: The project aims to reduce deforestation, enhance restoration and improve management practices across Ecuador’s entire mangrove area, which spans 156,633 hectares. In addition, mangroves provide basic services such as reducing flood risks, managing sedimentation in water systems, for example, by acting as buffers against erosion; conserving habitats for numerous plant and bird species. Mangroves also serve as vital nurseries for fish and other marine organisms, providing a critical habitat for the survival of various fish species. Thus mangroves play a significant role in fish production and provide substantial ecosystem services such as carbon sequestration, water purification, and biodiversity service value of mangroves, including biodiversity and other services, is estimated to be substantial, providing essential benefits to local economies.


---
**Page 112**
---

### Social co-benefits: In addition to mitigating flood risks, the project will yield various social co-benefits particularly for mangrove-dependent communities and associations, as follows:

- Improved social cohesion and organizational capacity among 60 associations with AUSCENM, which will enhance the ability of communities to engage with government stakeholders, resolve internal conflicts and develop additional community activities;
- Enhanced mangrove management and planning are likely to integrate social and environmental considerations into decision-making processes, thereby ensuring that actions benefitting human health and well-being; and
- Collaborative efforts in mangrove restoration in mangrove areas between community-based associations and government authorities will enhance local security, generating collateral benefits.

### Economic Benefits: Mangrove conservation and restoration offer a cost-effective solution for climate change adaptation, generating economic benefits for the national and local governments by preventing damage from extreme weather events. The shrimp production sector stands to benefit by accessing new markets through sustainable SCD production leading to increased export revenue and employment opportunities.

- Communities will experience reduced flooding, along with minimized impacts on infrastructure and crops due to improved drainage systems.
- Improved adaptive fishing techniques will enhance the resilience, economic stability and food security of marginalized coastal fishing communities. Mangrove ecosystems form the foundation for diversified livelihoods in coastal areas by providing a significant source of income through livelihoods and contributing to marine fisheries.

### Gender-sensitive development impact: The project will directly contribute to empowering women and girls through their participation in community activities and advancing their participation and leadership in organizations and enterprises, ensuring a gender-sensitive approach to development initiatives.


#### 3.4 Needs of the recipient

Scale: N/A

- Ecuador has experienced notable economic growth from 2007 and 2018 resulting in a reduction in poverty levels, inequality and the ongoing expansion of the middle class.
However, the economy's heavy reliance on oil sector presents a significant vulnerability, particularly given the recent drop in global crude oil prices and the devaluation of the dollar.

- With over 3,619 km of coastline, Ecuador’s five coastal provinces are home to approximately 54 per cent of the population. These areas face significant risks from sea level rise, adverse weather events and other climate-related hazards. Coastal communities are disproportionately affected by poverty, with 45 per cent of the population in the project area living below the national poverty line. Unsafe housing conditions persist due to rapid urbanization and persistent poverty.

- The challenges of coastal development are compounded by the threats of climate change. Projected sea level rise and more frequent and intense storms will exacerbate the combined impacts of sea level rise and intensified storms. The project addresses these pressing needs by supporting the national government in developing a comprehensive strategy for supporting the national and local government, coastal communities and crucial economic sectors such as shrimp farming and fisheries.


---
**Page 113**
---

3.5 Country ownership

The project is in alignment with the NDC in terms of contributing to both mitigation and adaptation goals, as well as in terms of protecting mangrove ecosystems. The project will also be good to other national policies, including the national climate change strategy; the conservation of the national forest estate; and REDD+ efforts. Moreover, the project is linked to the national macroeconomic development plan and the national poverty reduction strategies for NDC implementation; thus the results will be aggregated into the national biennial transparency report (NBR) under the UNFCCC’s Biennial Update Report (BUR) reporting framework.

In addition, country ownership is firmly established through active government participation in the project governance structure. Both the Ministry of Environment, Water and Ecological Transition and the Vice-ministry of Aquaculture and Fisheries will be part of the project management committee. The project also has a strong institutional link with the project component. This investment enhances ownership and promotes awareness of the technology being proposed by the project, setting the foundation for replication and scalability in the future.

### 3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

The project provides the most value of the scenarios considered over both the project implementation period (5-6 years) and the project lifespan (20 years), and it gives a high benefit-to-cost ratio (B/C). The project is expected to generate USD 299 million of economic value over its lifetime. The project also has a high social return on investment, in such a way that the project as designed provides excellent value for money because it will generate significant benefits for society over both the implementation period (5-6 years) and the expected project lifespan (20 years).

### IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

The project aims to achieve GHG emission reductions while providing flood protection and enhancing biodiversity in the mangrove ecosystem through coordinated efforts to protect and restore the mangrove ecosystem in Esaúre. The project interventions are intended to have positive environmental impacts, which will be achieved by reducing emissions from deforestation and forest degradation (REDD+). These impacts are expected to be minor and site-specific, and can be managed through careful design and standard mitigation measures. The environmental co-benefits due to reduced deforestation, restoration and mangrove protection include improved water quality, increased biodiversity for fish and species, enhanced biodiversity and ecosystem services. The social co-benefits include improved livelihoods, improved food security, and better health outcomes for human health and well-being.

#### Environmental and social risk category and safeguard instrument

The project has been categorized as environmental and social (E&S) risk category B. The Secretariat confirms the categorization and that it is within the AEF’s E&S risk assessment level. Since the interventions are very specific to the mangrove ecosystem, they have not been identified with any potential partners, and are not yet identified, the AE has prepared an environmental and social impact assessment (ESIA) for each of the subprojects. The ESIA will identify and analyze the potential risks and impacts and possible mitigation measures and set out the procedures for assessing and managing E&S risks and impacts of the individual interventions (subprojects).


---
**Page 114**
---

### Compliance with GCF’s Environmental and Social Safeguards (ESS) Standards

#### ESS1: Assessment and management of environmental and social risks and impacts

The project will conduct an assessment to identify potential adverse effects on the environment and communities (refer to Annexes 6 to the funding proposal). This includes summarizing the project activities, outcomes, and applicable legal and institutional framework. The biophysical and social environment, identified potential adverse effects, and measures for avoiding, mitigating, and/or compensating for these effects will be described.

#### ESS2: Labour and working conditions

The project will involve mostly community members in its activities. Workers may be exposed to drawing hazards, poisonous animals and diseases while collecting resources. The project will ensure the health and safety of workers by developing prevention and emergency response planning for these activities. The project’s activities are expected to be conducted within national labour laws and regulations. However, the project has a risk of potentially involving or leading to working conditions that do not meet national or international labour laws and regulations. In this regard, the project will ensure the health and safety of workers by developing prevention and emergency response planning for these activities.

#### ESS4: Community health, safety, and security

The mangrove restoration/reforestation activity of the project may expose community members to physical hazards, such as drawing hazards or poisonous animals and diseases. Mangrove ecosystems are home to some potentially dangerous animals and vectors that can result in diseases such as malaria, dengue, leprosy, etc., which could be transmitted by mosquitoes. The project’s activities will be conducted, and community health, safety, and security plans will be implemented on each of the subprojects. These plans will include measures for avoiding, mitigating, and/or compensating for these effects.

#### ESS5: Land acquisition and involuntary resettlement

The All has determined that the project may result in involuntary resettlement due to loss of access for some groups or individuals to mangrove resources. A process framework for restricting access to mangrove resources will be developed with the assistance of relevant community associations put in place restrictions on access to the mangrove resources for both members and non-members, as well as measures for avoiding, mitigating, and/or compensating for these effects.

#### ESS6: Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural resources

The project will not result in significant adverse impacts on biodiversity. The project may support various forest use and conservation activities that may take place in and/or around protected areas and other conservation sites. The project is not expected to result in the extinction or severe decline of any species of flora and fauna, nor will it have a significant impact on any species and enhance the ecosystem services that mangrove forests provide. The project includes mangrove restoration

### Cultural heritage

The project is expected to implement activities that might affect cultural heritage sites and areas of cultural significance. These will involve the removal, alteration, or disturbance of any non-replicable or critical cultural heritage elements, including sacred places, burial grounds, and other culturally significant sites, without free, prior, and informed consent of the communities where it belongs.

#### Sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment (SEAH)

The revised GEF Environmental and Social Safeguards Policy (ESMP) 2012/13 requires safeguarding from SEAH in GEF-financed activities. The AE provided information on SEAM safeguards during the proposal development process. The project will identify potential SEAM risks, especially community engagement that might involve sexual exploitation or abuse of women and children. The AE is committed to ensuring a safe, trusted, and respectful environment for all people involved with the project, including staff, contractors, and communities in programmes and projects. This is in alignment with the GEF SEAM policy which is applicable to its projects. The project recognizes potential SEAM risks, especially community engagement that might involve sexual exploitation or abuse of women and children. The AE will ensure that, in cases where SEAH incidents occur (or have a high probability of occurring), they address these issues through appropriate measures as per the GEF SEAM policy.

The AE has therefore identified potential SEAM risks and mitigation measures in the ESMP.

The project will have dedicated full-time staff including gender focal points who will be responsible for safeguarding and gender-related matters. An entry unit field will be established in each of the project's four target entities to be directly involved in the daily activities of the host communities.

The consultation process involved key project stakeholders such as the men and women of local mangrove associations and other disadvantaged or vulnerable stakeholders. ## Gender policy

The AE provided a gender assessment and action plan with the funding proposal, and therefore complies with the requirements of the GCF Gender Policy. The assessment report presents an overview of the current situation regarding women's participation in decision-making for women and men in the targeted project area. The gender assessment is based on secondary data, as there are no baseline studies available to provide a comprehensive picture.

The majority of the secondary data is from outdated sources (i.e., 2010-2012). The Secretariat therefore recommends that the AE should conduct an initial baseline study in order to enable some revisions to the documents in annex B of the funding proposal if there are any major changes in demographics and/or socioeconomic statuses that may have a bearing on this specific project.

The gender action plan for the project outlines various interventions in order to ensure that actions taken under the project will contribute to increasing women's participation at all levels, including decision-making positions. The AE also aims at increasing women's participation in ensuring diversified and climate-resilient livelihood options among women and men, as well as improving access of women and men to fisheries associations and gender quotas, among other things. The Secretariat recommends and suggests that the AE include the following provisions: (i) engage local women and fisherwomen in participatory activities related to the project; (ii) provide training for women on sustainable marine and coastal resources in assessments, and their traditional practices in natural resource management; (iii) ensure that women have access to credit facilities, which will improve their access and profitability, and address barriers within families and households by involving men; (iv) build capacity of women's groups and associations through the project; and (v) engage local women and fisherfolk, and (iv) define common goals and develop strategic alliances between women's groups and associations.

The Secretariat recommends that the AE should consider including a gender balance when forming stakeholder groups; this may involve including female minority leaders and managers to encourage participation and capacity-building. The AE should also ensure that women have access to credit facilities, so that financial resources actually reach the needy, poor, vulnerable and disadvantaged communities including women and girls.

It will also accelerate mangrove recovery to enhance flood protection for 19,000 people and

## Accredited entity/executing entity capacity to execute the current programme (low risk)

### Cl, through its GCF agency, will serve as the AE with GfC overseeing project execution.
With a track record in conservation spanning three decades and covering Galapagos, marine protected areas, and coastal ecosystems, GfC has extensive experience in the creation and management of protected areas and collaborating with artisanal fisher associations to manage resource management. GfC has collaborated with Cl through previous projects (i.e., SPS and FPGS) in addressing mitigation, adaptation, and sustainable livelihoods in Africa.

## Programme-specific execution risks (medium risk)

### Security risk: The Secretariat notes that there are reports of threats from criminal gangs operating in the Galapagos archipelago. Some islands, particularly Guayquil, are reportedly entangled in the cocaine trade, with drug traffickers exploiting the archipelago's remote location for transit and storage. This is raising security concerns in the country, potentially disrupting project implementation.
The AE also acknowledges that there have been reports of illegal fishing, overfishing, and the exploitation of Ecuador’s coastal areas for cocaine trafficking, underscoring potential impacts on project activities. In its mitigation plan, the AE has developed a security plan (annex 6.5MP) to address these risks. The AE will work with the Secretariat to develop and implement a mitigation plan during project start-up.
The mitigation measures will include pre-activity assessments, border controls, and ensuring coy identification of Cl’s staff.

### Political instability: The political landscape in Ecuador has been characterized by instability since the 2017 presidential election, which was only resolved with the October 2023 election. Newly elected President Noboa was voted into office without a majority in the National Assembly.
To secure political stability and ensure project implementation, Cl will work closely with the political left and conservatives. Nonetheless, Cl’s 20 years of experience operating in Ecuador's political context provides valuable insights for navigating complex relationships with local and provincial government officials, and it is expected that this will help to deliver projects on time and within budget.
Cl will also engage with relevant social communities, project partners and relevant authorities, to help address political challenges.

### Project viability [and concessionality]: The use of grants (i.e., 100 per cent concessional funding) for the project is considered sustainable. However, the direct expenditure for mangrove conservation and restoration efforts is limited and insufficient.
Vulnerable populations in Ecuador are directly exposed to threats due to poverty and climate change, and they lack adaptive capacity due to poverty. Granting funding is therefore warranted to create the enabling conditions for sustaining capacity and incentivizing private sector investment in mangrove conservation and restoration activities while fostering long-term sustainability.


---
**Page 118**
---

### Compliance risk (medium risk)

#### 4.3.1

Ecuador is not currently subject to United Nations Security Council resolutions, nor is it currently under the Financial Action Task Force list of high-risk jurisdictions or jurisdictions under increased monitoring relating to anti-money laundering (AML) and countering the financing of terrorism (CFT).

The project area is located in a region of criminal gangs operating in project areas and rated the probability as "medium." The mitigating measures described in the security plan include staff training, regular visits by GCF staff, close collaboration with local authorities, as well as cooperation with local partners and authorities. The funding proposal also noted that project costs will be increased to cover additional security costs. The Board has placed emphasis on its AML/CFT controls in annexes 20 to the funding proposal and confirmed that its AML/CFT policies include sanction screening of subgrantees and all CI/CC/Ecuador employees.

Following the submission of the funding proposal, the GCF Secretariat noted that since the proposal was first submitted, the AE confirmed that the security plan has been updated accordingly, with additional security costs included in the budget. Given the low probability of AML/CFT risk within Ecuador, this project is deemed to be medium risk from a compliance perspective.

#### 4.3.2 GCF portfolio concentration risk (low risk)

In case of approval, the impact of this proposal on the GCF portfolio concentration in terms of result area and single proposal is immaterial.

### 4.5 Results monitoring and reporting

As a cross-cutting project, the funding proposal aims to achieve a combination of mitigation outcomes (reduction in GHG emissions from deforestation and land use for mitigation), most vulnerable people and communities, and ecosystem and ecosystems services for adaptation. The expected mitigation outcome is an estimated 6 tGHG emission reduction of 4,609 tCO2e per year over the project’s implementation period.

The expected adaptation beneficiaries amount to 89,600 and 3.4 million direct and indirect beneficiaries respectively. This document provides the necessary annexes to demonstrate the calculation methods used for these estimates.

The theory of change has been improved and refined to clearly articulate the results chain that links the project activities to the expected climate outcomes and co-benefits. It also identifies pathways that will lead to the expected climate outcomes and co-benefits.

The logical framework is consistent with the requirements of the Logical Results Management Framework (LRMF) and represents a shift from the old paradigm shift perspective through a combination of building community knowledge and resources for mangrove management and livelihoods, as well as strengthening collaboration between the private sector and government on mangrove protection and restoration. The project will be measured against the following indicators: indicator 2.1 (adoption of climate-resilient livelihood options), supplementary indicator 3.1 (change in livelihood practices), supplementary indicator 4.1 (natural resources restored) and core indicator 1 (GHG emission reductions). These have all been incorporated into the logical framework and relevant means of verification, targets and indicators are provided for each.

The M&L plan, which includes indicator-specific approaches for data collection and data sources, including baseline data, are well provided for in the project budget and AL fees respectively. The budget allocation for the M&L activities is consistent with the requirements of the GCF Evaluation Policy.
