# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP235

## Proposal name:
Mangroves for climate: Public, Private and Community Partnerships for Mitigation and Adaptation in Ecuador

## Accredited entity:
Conservation International Foundation

## Country:
Ecuador

## Project/programme size:
Small

### Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

#### 1. Overview
The Mangroves for climate: Public, Private and Community Partnerships for Mitigation and Adaptation in Ecuador project is a cross-cutting project aimed at strengthening the resilience of coastal communities to climate change impacts while also significantly reducing greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions. Over an implementation period of six-and-a-half years, this small-sized project will bring together the public, private, and community sectors to develop and implement a comprehensive strategy for mangrove conservation and restoration as well as coastal protection, biodiversity, and local livelihoods. This project meets the urgent need for climate-smart agriculture and sustainable development in Ecuador at multiple levels, providing direct benefits to Ecuador's most vulnerable coastal communities.

The project interventions are centered around three main components:

- **Community-based mangrove management:** Strengthening community-led initiatives that improve local livelihoods and ensure sustainable use of mangroves.
- **Private-sector engagement:** Mobilizing the shrimp farming sector to adopt and finance climate-smart practices that align with mangrove conservation efforts.
- **Create and enabling environment:** Develop and enforce policies that integrate mangrove conservation into national climate change strategies, including improved governance and legal frameworks.

The execution of the project proposal is expected to (i) protect and manage 156,631 ha of mangroves in Ecuador, which will sequester approximately 4.6 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (MtCO2 eq) over its 20-year lifespan; and (ii) enhance the climate resilience of 98,000 people living in vulnerable coastal communities.

#### 3. Budget
The total budget for the proposed project is USD 45.9 million. A substantial portion of this budget, USD 36.4 million, is requested as a grant from GCF. Co-financing of USD 9.5 million will be provided by the Ministry of Environment, Water and Transfers of Ecuador (MMAETE), Conservation International Foundation, and other partners.

#### 4. Conclusions
This project is vital not only for its environmental, economic, and social benefits but also as a model for integrated, multistakeholder climate action. It aligns with the national climate strategy and the country's commitments under the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change and its Paris Agreement.


---
**Page 122**
---

### Impact potential

#### Scale: High

The project aims to significantly reduce vulnerability for the $90 billion direct and 3.4 million indirect jobs in Ecuador’s coastal economy, which is heavily dependent on mangroves in part of Ecuador's coastal and total population, respectively. This wide reach focuses on the most vulnerable groups, such as small-scale fishers and other coastal communities. By implementing strategies to ensure that the project benefits reach the most marginalized people within these groups, including tailored measures for varying vulnerability levels, it could enhance the project’s impact.

From a climate change mitigation perspective, the proposed project can reduce 4.6 Mt CO2eq through mangrove restoration and conservation of mangrove forests, which are significant carbon sinks.

By prioritizing natural mangrove barriers, the proposed project avoids the long-term liabilities associated with artificial structures. This approach enhances resilience and flood mitigation capabilities, boosting the adaptive capacity of exposed communities. The continuation of activities initiated by the proposed project could integrate hybrid approaches, combining both natural and engineered solutions to enhance coastal protection.

The proposed project directly causes GHG emission reductions from mangrove deforestation and degradation in the short term (2015-2030). This reduction is aimed at resulting in a mitigation impact of 79.22 t CO2eq during the project’s site and six-year implementation phases, with an additional 4.6 Mt CO2eq over its 20-year lifespan. The proposal includes a clear project timeline for each phase, including milestones, activities, outputs, outcomes, measurement, reporting and verification (MRV) at the project scale as well as how it links to the national MRV system.

Furthermore, the proposed project indirectly supports low-emission practices by promoting sustainable shrimp farming techniques within the private sector.

The project also contributes to building climate-resilient local regulatory systems by embedding climate resilience in local and national governance. This involves improving the community management of mangroves and supporting legal frameworks that encourage private-sector conservation efforts.

The proposed project promotes the generation and use of climate information in decision-making processes, particularly through the Ecuadorian National Climate Centre on El Niño Southern Oscillation. However, establishing robust feedback mechanisms to adapt and update practices based on the latest climate data could further solidify this aspect.

By engaging local communities, the project aims to increase awareness and adaptive capacity among local populations. Educating communities on climate threats and adaptation strategies is a key component of the project’s strategy, empowering stakeholders to actively engage in conservation and adaptation efforts.

Overall, the Independent Technical Advisory Panel (ITAP) considers the impact potential for both adaptation and mitigation of the proposed project to be high.

### Paradigm shift potential

#### Scale: Medium to High


---
**Page 123**
---

10\. The proposed project advocates a collaborative approach, engaging public, private, and community partnerships in mangrove management and restoration. By integrating the adaptation and mitigation benefits of mangrove ecosystems into economic and governance frameworks, it aims to create new opportunities for sustainable livelihoods in the shrimp aquaculture sector, to promote sustainable practices.

15\. The project's theory of change is coherent and well-structured, showing an increasing mangrove area that will lead to increased carbon sequestration, directly supporting global low-carbon development pathways.

18\. Furthermore, the TAP welcomes the inclusion in activity 3.1.1 of an explicit system for MRV of mangroves, which includes a monitoring plan, involving community participation and making subgrants to competitively selected universities or non-governmental organizations to establish a national MRV system. This will be integrated into the project monitoring and evaluation (M&E) plan and the national REDD+ MRV systems. This enhanced MRV system will provide more accurate data on mangrove extent and/or private sector efforts to mobilize resources based on mangrove carbon sequestration.

19\. The M&E plan targets the ecological, social, and economic impacts of the proposed project. It includes a monitoring framework for each activity, with specific indicators and extensive details on this plan. Improving this aspect, notably establishing a clear and fair fit-for-purpose feedback loop between the project and its stakeholders will ensure that lessons learned are effectively disseminated, promoting adaptive management and replication in other contexts.

20\. The proposed project involves creating a USD 4 million endowment in the Socio Manglar subcomponent of the National Fund for Sustainable Development (FONAD) to provide long-term financial support for mangrove conservation. This strategic financial planning ensures the continuation of mangrove restoration activities beyond the project period; however, ongoing contributions and financial sustainability will require continuous reinforcement.

21\. The proposed project promotes new business activities and markets, particularly in climate-smart shrimp farming. By further expanding by exploring additional ecosystem services provided by mangroves that could be monetized through market mechanisms, such as carbon credits or biodiversity offsets, promoting mangrove conservation into shrimp-farming projects, the project alters incentives for market participants to invest in mangrove restoration and protection, which can lead to a shift away from mangrove loss. Increasing awareness and highlighting the benefits of these changes among the broader industry could further shift market incentives. In addition, greater diversification of economic activities within the shrimp farming sector will help reduce their dependence on climate-sensitive sectors, but would also increase their resilience to adverse impacts associated with climate change.

22\. The proposed project will address barriers such as the lack of coordinated management and inadequate enforcement of environmental regulations. However, barriers such as those relating to land tenure and access to resources for restoration activities are more complex; long-term financing depends to some extent on private sector participation, and the project's strategy for achieving this is lacking in detail.

23\. Overall, the TAP rates the paradigm shift potential of the proposed project as medium to high.


### 1.4 Sustainable development potential

24\. The proposed project demonstrates significant potential for improving environmental quality and conservation. It emphasizes the restoration and preservation of mangrove ecosystems, which are vital for carbon sequestration, biodiversity conservation, and acting as


---
**Page 124**
---

natural drivers against coastal erosion and flooding. This aligns with Sustainable Development Goal (SDG) 13 (Climate action) and 14 (Life on land). The project aims to reduce 64 Mt CO₂ eq over its duration, thereby enhancing air quality and soil stability through increased mangrove coverage.

The proposed project focuses on areas that encompass a significant portion of Ecuador's coastal province. These provinces are home to approximately 3.4 million people in coastal provinces who are indirectly affected by these climate risks, with substantial assets at stake, including the shrimp aquaculture industry, a major economic driver.

### 1.5 Needs of the Recipient
Scale: High

Ecuador’s coastlines, especially its extensive mangrove ecosystems, faces significant climate change impacts such as sea-level rise, extreme El Niño events, and increased precipitation variability. These factors amplify coastal flooding risks, endangering local communities and economic activities.

The vulnerability of Ecuador's coastal provinces is intensified by the ongoing deforestation of mangroves, which are crucial for flood control and erosion prevention. The project’s focus on restoring and conserving mangrove areas directly addresses these climate change impacts.


---
**Page 125**
---

The project identifies and aims to support groups particularly vulnerable to climate impacts, integrating strategies that address their needs into the broader national climate and development agenda. The proposed project promotes sustainable livelihoods and resilience in these communities; those strategies should be closely evaluated to ensure that they result in tangible benefits.

### 16 Country ownership

**Scale:** High

The proposed project directly supports Ecuador's objectives for low-emission and climate-resilient development, including the achievement of nationally determined contributions. It aims to help achieve Ecuador’s nationally determined contribution targets by reducing GHG emissions and promoting community-managed mangrove conservation and restoration in the Manabi Province Mangrove Ecosystem.

The stringent Environmental Code of Ecuador and local regulations offer a solid policy foundation for the proposed project. However, these policies require strengthening to ensure effective mangrove conservation and restoration. The project aims to foster a supportive environment for mangrove conservation and restoration through improved governance and climate adaptation strategies.

The project leverages experience from existing climate-change initiatives. Alongside local partners, they have participated in previous mangrove conservation efforts, further enhancing their credibility and capability. The proposed project offers a new opportunity to enhance local capacities and contribute to national-level mangrove conservation and restoration initiatives in the country.

The project development process included consultations with civil society, local communities, government sectors, and other stakeholders, with a focus on gender equality and sustainable livelihoods. It intends to maintain engagement with these groups throughout the project. Local ownership is essential for long-term success; therefore, institutional mechanisms for continuous stakeholder input and feedback during implementation could improve project outcomes.

The project engages in its country institutions such as MAATE along with local governments and community organizations, fostering local ownership and accountability. Strengthening these relationships will ensure effective implementation, and their contributions will be crucial for successful project outcomes.

The proposed project is designed to complement and synergize with other national and international initiatives that support the project's activities are integrated and contribute to broader conservation and climate-resilience efforts.


---
**Page 126**
---

1.7 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: Medium to High

The proposed financial structure, with a USD 34.6 million GCF grant and total funding of USD 45.9 million (co-financing ratio is 1.21), aims to tackle major obstacles like insufficient financing for mangrove restoration and shrimp farming. The heavy dependence on grant funding indicates a need for more diversified financial approaches to ensure sustainability beyond the initial funding period.

The project's reliance on grants reflects high concessionality, which is essential due to its focus on vulnerable communities and ecosystem services that offer public benefits. However, the proposed project has already outlined several steps taken to minimize the level of concessionality needed to ensure viability.

The proposed project is designed to leverage additional private and public investment rather than relying solely on grant funding for private sector investments in sustainable shrimp farming and mangrove conservation. However, it would benefit from clearer metrics on the expected financial returns and sources beyond initial funding.

The project expects to reduce 4.6 Mt CO2 eq over its lifetime, with an investment cost of roughly USD 10 per t CO2 eq based on total funding. To fully evaluate its relative efficiency, this cost-effectiveness should be compared against other GCF projects involving mangrove restoration and shrimp farming. Furthermore, the M&E methodology on which MALE, universities, and community monitors will be trained goes beyond measuring the gross regional impact of mangrove conservation by also incorporating a more comprehensive hydrological dynamics of mangrove ecosystems as a contributor to mangrove losses and gains. This offers an opportunity for synergies with other GCF projects involving mangrove restoration.

Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The proposed project is well-structured with a solid theory of change to ensure long-term results and resilience. In order to improve it, the ITAP suggests that the AIE takes into account the following aspects:

(a) Diversification of financial strategies for long-term sustainability: While the proposed project has already outlined several steps taken to minimize concessionality, it is crucial to develop a more diversified financial approach. This could involve establishing partnerships with private sector investors and exploring innovative financing mechanisms. Providing clearer metrics on expected investment amounts and sources will strengthen financial planning and ensure the project's viability beyond its initial funding period.


---
**Page 127**
---

**(b)**

* **Promotion of economic diversification to enhance long-term resilience and mitigation impact:** To further enhance the project’s impact and financial viability, it is essential to promote economic diversification within the targeted regions. This can be accomplished by supporting small-scale agriculture, livestock production, aquaculture, fisheries and shrimp farming. By fostering a more diversified local economy, the proposed project will help reduce dependency on single crops or livestock products and increase their resilience to adverse climatic conditions. Additionally, providing training and resources for these activities in conjunction with climate change adaptation can help create more stable and sustainable income sources for local populations; and

**(c)**

* **Integration of comprehensive monitoring, evaluation and learning plans:** The proposed project will include a Monitoring, Evaluation and Learning (MEL) Framework to ensure that the project is on track. This MEL Framework will help capture lessons learned and disseminate them effectively. This will promote adaptation and resilience, enabling the project to adjust strategies as needed and enhance replicability in other regions.

* **Based on the analysis presented in the above paragraphs,** the iTAP recommends that the Board approve this funding proposal.

# Executive summary

The mangroves along Ecuador’s coast provide important economic services to local communities including food, income sources, and protection from flooding and storms. Across all project sites, women and men depend on the natural resources they obtain from the mangroves.

A complex set of historical, social, cultural, and economic factors influence the project sites’ current gender dynamics in relation to mangrove use and management. Women are often excluded by men in decision-making fora. Secondly, women are overburdened with work, especially reproductive labor, and this limits their ability to engage in community, organizational, and project activities. This reinforces existing inequalities between men and women and perpetuates the inequalities at the household level and an important additional barrier that keep women from using their voice and agency. This situation is compounded by a number of structural problems associated with gender inequality, such as lack of access to land and limited employment opportunities.

In these mangrove-dependent communities, women are often more vulnerable and have higher rates of poverty than men. Men tend to control the main economic activities in the communities: the harvesting and sale of seafood. Often it is men who hold formal positions of leadership within communities and fishing associations. As Treveno & Murillo-Sandoval (2021) highlight in a study on gender equality in Ecuadorian coastal communities, women’s access to resources and social gender norms influence the strategies that men and women use to respond to mangrove degradation and loss due to growing shrimp aquaculture, with women being disproportionately affected by the loss of mangrove forage.

There are distinct gender differences with respect to mangrove use and management in the northern project sites versus those in the south. In the north, women are able to walk (often collecting firewood) through the mangroves without fear of harassment or violence from men. Women have a greater say over how they use and manage Mangrove associations in the north have, on average, 60% women members. Men have a dual role; they work in crab and black cockle extraction as well as in fishing. In contrast, women are more involved with the collection of mangrove resources than men. Women engage in activities such as collecting firewood, harvesting or fishing activities, cooking for the family and looking after the children. The average working day for women is around 14 hours and little social value is placed on their caregiving or reproductive labor. In the south, women are more isolated from the mangroves due to fear of harassment or violence from men. Women’s access to resources in the south is limited as they are not recognized by the communities or organizations that manage them. This can be attributed to their access and use of the mangrove resources being perceived as a threat to male livelihoods. In addition, because women’s activities are more individual (each person arranges their own access to the area), Mangrove associations in the south have, on average, 15% women members. Women engage in activities such as collecting firewood, harvesting or fishing activities, cooking for the family and looking after the children. The average working day for women is around 14 hours and little social value is placed on their caregiving or reproductive labor. In contrast to men who can work up to five days a week at harvest time (for between four and five hours), this means that once their day’s work is over, they are relieved from work.

Rates of gender-based violence are high (57% in project sites) and could be perpetuated or exacerbated by climate change impacts that result in economic disruption.


---
**Page 137**
---

As a result of gender norms, men and women are impacted differently by climate-related events. Rising sea levels and flooding – the two major effects cited in justifying climate action in these project sites – can also have consequences like an increase in water salinity and water-borne diseases, which are likely to affect primarily women who tend to be responsible for collecting water for household use.

Women (who may also be employed as fishers or farmers) experience climate impacts such as more unpredictable weather and changing rainfall patterns. Men, on the other hand, are more likely to be affected by changes in sea levels and flooding. These gender inequalities may be exacerbated by the dynamics of climate change in the project area.


Our analysis included extensive review of relevant secondary information related to gendered uses of mangroves, the policy environment that supports gender equality in Ecuador, and statistical data on women’s participation in decision-making processes.

The analysis also drew on primary information from project sites gathered through surveys (involving 80 men/19 women), community workshops (involving 223 men/40 women) and semi-structured key informant interviews.


The analysis points to several key areas of intervention for the project, as laid out in the project’s gender action plan. These include mangrove use agreements that integrate gender considerations; ensuring equal representation of women and men on the project steering committee (the two women's associations); setting targets (50/50) for women's and men's benefit from the adoption of diversified and climate resilient livelihoods options linked to mangroves, and ensuring that women’s voices are heard in decision-making processes. The analysis also highlights the importance of mitigating gender inequalities in the project, including gender-based violence.

# Introduction

## Project background

Climate change will result in significant impacts on local environmental conditions along the coast of Ecuador, including a rise in sea levels, El Niño events, and changes in the intensity and variability of rainfall, flooding, and atmospheric temperatures. Based on models generated by NASA's Goddard Institute for Space Studies (Hansen et al., 2013), it is expected that sea level will rise by about 4 cm over the next century. Considering that the 1997-1998 El Niño event led to a 42 cm increase in sea levels, periods of heavier rainfall are expected to worsen the rise in sea levels.

The rise in sea levels is a major concern for coastal areas of Ecuador. The Dartmouth Flood Observatory has recorded more floods since 1987, displacing over 506,000 people (Dartmouth Flood Observatory, n.d.). Based on current assessments, 39% of areas within 5 km of the coast are already at high risk of annual flooding (Secretaría Nacional de Gestión del Riesgo, 2018).

Ninety-nine percent (99%) of mangroves are concentrated along the shores of the country's 4 main estuaries: Cayapas-Mataje, Muñoz, Guayas, and Jambelí. These areas are home to 2.9 million people, most of whom live in high poverty rates. Those people rely heavily on fishing and harvesting crabs and black clams.

There are 4,536 registered members of mangrove management associations devoted almost exclusively to the conservation and sustainable use of mangroves. These management associations cover approximately 43% of Ecuador's mangrove forests, meaning that the true number of people who fish and gather seafood from the mangroves may be at least double the number of registered members (INEC, 2018). The total population directly dependent on fishing in mangroves is likely to be around 45,000.

Despite their importance for coastal communities, mangrove forests have been deforested since 1969 (CURSEN, 1999, 2007; Ministry of Environment of Ecuador, 2020). In 2018, there were 156,633 ha of mangrove remaining in mainland Ecuador. While the introduction of new regulations requiring the protection and sustainable use of mangroves has been a positive step, it has contributed to an increase in net reforestation; there continues to be significant gross deforestation.

The ongoing mangrove deforestation in these areas is the result of a combination of (i) an increase in illegal logging, (ii) poor enforcement of environmental regulations that penalize causing damage to mangroves, (iii) pollution from liquid and solid waste, and (iv) poor spatial planning. The loss of mangroves has significant impacts on coastal communities.

Given their importance, and in the face of future impacts from climate change, the government of Ecuador has made it a priority to reduce the risks faced by coastal communities and promote the ecosystem services provided by mangroves in its National Climate Change Strategy 2012 – 2035. The strategy aims at reducing vulnerability and increasing resilience against climate change.

In this context, this project seeks to build resilience and a coordinated adaptive capacity along the entire coast and in the sectors most vulnerable to climate change, in particular coastal communities and the fishing sector. The project has 3 components:


---
**Page 139**
---

Component 1: Mangrove areas under effective and climate-adapted management increased, including through community-based management (AUSCEMA) and protected areas implementing climate adaptation plans.

Component 2: The private sector becomes a transformational agent for change by reducing GHG emissions, increasing mangrove restoration in coastal zones, and restoring mangroves that increase climate resilience for other coastal populations.

Component 3: Create the enabling conditions for sustaining reductions in mangrove deforestation and increased mangrove restoration by strengthening governance, climate change adaptation policies, and coastal zone management policies, and law enforcement.

Ecuadorian mangroves provide important economic benefits to local communities who harvest white fish, black clams, crabs, fish, shrimp, oysters, mussels, blue crabs and use snails for self-consumption and sale. The forest is also used for beekeeping and logging/charcoal production (CFEN/ADEA [2019]).

Figure 1: Map showing the principal mangrove areas in Ecuador and the four estuaries/areas that make up the project area.


| | |
|---|---|
| | |
| | |
| | |

Mangrove dwellers are a heterogeneous group in terms of race. In the northern estuaries, the mangrove-dependent population is chiefly Afro-Ecuadorian, while in the southern provinces, mangrove populations are made up of mestizos (mixed-race population) and cholo fishermen (cholos are descendants of African slaves).

Occupational identities as crab or seafood harvesters, combined with situations of social vulnerability and poverty, play a more important role in self-identification than ethnicity. In general, mangrove dwellers experience low wages, informal employment and exploitation by intermediaries, lack of access to credit, poor infrastructure, limited access to education and public services (Latorre, 2012).


---
**Page 140**
---

# Purpose and methodology

The purpose of this gender assessment and action plan is to identify key information on the roles, activities, needs, opportunities, interests and rights of men, women, boys and girls that live in the project area in order to understand how the project can support their resilience to climate change. In doing so, the gender assessment exposes the gaps and inequities that exist between men and women, boys and girls, and among different social groups.

Finally, we also took an international lens, recognizing that many other social characteristics (e.g., wealth, education, marriage status, age, etc.) also contribute to women's and men's climate change vulnerability. The gender assessment is therefore conducted using a phased approach to data collection and analysis:

## Collection of secondary information

This process involved reviewing existing documentation from government agencies and non-governmental organizations that work in this area. This includes statistical information, background data, information on the area, and documents and bibliographic references to support the gender analysis. A list of the key documents can be found at the end of this document.

## Collection of primary information

Primary information was collected through various interviews determined based on the stakeholder group and as described in this image.


| Province | Name of estuary |
| --- | --- |
| Northern project areas | Esmeraldas |
|  | (1) Muñone |
|  | (3) Cayapas-Mataje |
| Southern project areas | Guayas |
|  | (4) Guayas River |
|  | (5) Oyo |

From a gender perspective, the two northern estuaries are generally similar to each other, with both men and women actively engaged in mangrove use and management, while the southern two estuaries are more male dominated with women engaging much less than in the north. With this in mind, we have organized this gender assessment and action plan to focus on these two specific areas (Guayas and Oyo).


---
**Page 141**
---

# 12.1 Workshops

In August and September 2013, we conducted 4 workshops in 3 of the estuaries (Muloes, Guayos and Jambel) with men and women from local associations that focus on crab and cockle harvest. The workshops included Afroecuadorian communities and Cholos Pescadores in Guayas and Jambel. Following the observation above that women are more engaged in mangrove use in the north compared to the south, we found that women were much more present in the workshop in the north compared to those in the south.

The main objectives were:
- Obtain participants' general information related to time of residence in this area, economic activity, organization and activities of men and men in mangrove areas.
- Obtain information on potential reforestation sites, types of mangroves to plant and their benefits.
- Identify perceptions that men and women have of the impacts of current climate conditions (wind, rain, presence of water holes, temperature, etc.) and marine conditions, on their communities and mangrove area concessions.
- Identify which products are currently used, who sets them, and how they could be used in the future.

### 1.2.2 Semi structured interviews

To supplement the workshops and desk review, targeted interviews were conducted with 7 representatives from Muline, Guayas and Jambelí estuaries. The aim of the interviews was to increase stakeholder input into the identification of social and environmental risks of the planned project, as well as to gather information on specific risk-related topics:

- Discrimination against women on the basis of their gender, with regard to participation in the design and implementation and access to opportunities and benefits.
- Limitations in women's ability to use, develop and protect natural resources, taking into account the different roles and positions of men and women in accessing environmental goods and services.
- Threats to women’s health and occupational health and safety.
- Other gender equality concerns related to the project.
- Indirect impacts on biodiversity or the ecosystem services on which the livelihoods of affected populations depend.
- Adverse effects in terms of inequality or discrimination for affected populations.
- Possible restrictions in the availability, quality and access to basic resources or services.
- Adverse impacts on "indigenous people" (i.e., here Afro-Ecuadorians and Cholos Proletarios).
- Negative effects on the development priorities of “indigenous peoples” as defined by themselves.
- Adverse impacts on the enjoyment of human rights by the affected population and particularly by marginalized groups.
- Aggravation of conflict or generation of violence between affected communities and indigenous people.

The interviews confirmed that the risks had been identified during the workshops and provided further detail on the situation in different places and the unique perspectives of women and men. The following table provides an overview of the interviewees:

The gender assessment and subsequent gender action plan follow the Guidelines outlined in the Green Climate Fund (GCF) Mainstreaming Gender in Green Climate Fund Projects and the Updated Gender Policy and Action Plan 2018–2020, as well as Conservation International-GCI's Gender Policy in their latest (November 2020) Environmental and Social Management Framework. The project is designed to be gender responsive, meaning it will substantially help to overcome historical gender biases. Measures have been put in place by this project to ensure that both women and men benefit and that the burden of care work, particularly as a result of their gender roles, is not increased but reduced by the project activities.

2. Situation of men and women in coastal Ecuador

2.1 Legal status of women across Ecuador

This section describes the national legal framework in terms of gender and the international legal framework for gender and the environment that the country has been following and with which it has been bringing itself into line.

### 2.1.1 National legal framework

Ecuador has made significant progress in terms of national regulations to support women's rights. For over 10 years, in the Constitution of the Republic of Ecuador, the principle of non-discrimination contained in Paragraph 6 of Article 58 has been further underpinned by Paragraph 3 of Article 11.

Article 11 of the Constitution includes the concepts of direct and indirect discrimination. It makes reference to direct discrimination with the phrase "that has the purpose of impairing or nullifying the recognition, enjoyment or exercise of rights" and indirect discrimination with the phrase "that has the effect of impairing or nullifying the recognition, enjoyment or exercise of rights."

Also included are a series of obligations incumbent upon the state to achieve effective gender equality. These are a series of affirmative actions promoting true equality for rights holders in a situation where there is inequality between men and women; including a gender perspective in plans and programs; promoting equal representation of men and women in leadership decision-making positions; bringing about greater participation by women in public life, particularly in the areas of politics, in particular equality in diversity and non-discrimination, with an obligation to prioritize actions for groups that are most vulnerable or marginalized due to their sex, age, race, ethnicity, or violence; and adopting the necessary measures to guarantee equality for women in access to employment, professional and career training and advancement, fair pay, and self-employment initiatives.

Also enshrined in the Constitution of the Republic are the principles of parity, equity and rotation of power as cornerstones of the electoral system. Therefore, consideration must be given to affirmative action for women in electoral lists. Similarly, the Constitution establishes the principle of parity between men and women in appointing judicial officers.

### 2.1.2 Agenda for Women

As of 2018, the National Council for Gender Equality, a national authority with powers aimed at mainstreaming gender equality in the country, has key instruments to implement policy: the National Plan for the Promotion of Women and LGBTI+ Persons 2018-2023, which includes lines of action, policies, and actions to be undertaken by institutions and civil society based on the principle of fair treatment.


---
**Page 147**
---

The guide is divided into several lines of action that include autonomy and a culture of peace, and address issues relating to a life free of violence, actions in education and public policies in health, sexual and reproductive rights, or in fields such as sport. Another line of action is centered on sustainability of life and combines issues such as reproduction and sustainability, while another focuses on the conservation of nature and its resources. The Ministry also promotes conservation and reforestation programs and initiatives with the goal of improving ecosystem services and water sources.

The manual also includes a section on leadership and the transformation of socioecological patterns and discusses the monitoring and assessment of actions undertaken to promote equality.


### 2.1.3 Gender policy of the Ministry of Environment, Water and Ecological Transition of Ecuador (MAATE)

The Ministry of Environment, Water and Ecological Transition of Ecuador (MAATE) is responsible for complying with international instruments signed by Ecuador in matters relating to environmental protection and sustainable development. However, the country has made some important advances in gender.

The MAATE must therefore actively define policies, mechanisms, and strategies conducive to introducing a gender perspective into gender perspectives in programs, projects, and actions that come under its area of authority.


In this context, the General Coordinating Office for Environmental Planning defined the "Strategy for the Institutionalization of Intersectorality and Gender Perspectives" as a methodology for the institutionalization of an adequate gender perspective in environmental policy (PAN). This initiative aims to comply with Constituent Assembly legislative decrees and urgently seeks to promote the participation of women in decision-making processes, creating spaces to exercise citizen rights and consolidating democratic processes, strengthening the institutional framework of the National Councils for Ecology (Peoples and Nationals), Women; Afro-descendants; Indigenous Peoples; and People with Disabilities. It also aims at promoting a leading role of the state with a decentralized and territorial approach, and the application of the cross-cutting themes of gender equality, interculturality and human rights.

The MAATE has proposed legislation as a priority area to include social perspectives in environmental policies. It is also aligned with major lines proposed by central government and reflected in various public management instruments.


Furthermore, the constitution clearly shows the state's obligation to ensure fair treatment of men and women in all areas of life, recognizing their equal rights and universal human rights inherent to every human being, including the right to a safe environment.

On January 30th, 2015, the environment minister, Tarsicio Granato, and the deputy resident representative of the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP), Noemi Querino, signed the Project for the National Action Plan on Gender Equality (PANEG). This project is part of a broad process to include a gender approach in developing Ecuador's nationally-determined contributions (NDCs). This project was undertaken within the framework of the NDC-SP (NDC Support Programme).


---
**Page 148**
---

Also in 2019, the MAIE and the National Council for Gender Equality signed an international cooperation framework agreement, through which the two institutions committed to leading and ensuring the inclusion of gender equality policies to create and strengthen effective, efficient interventions in climate change-related projects. This agreement is the result of a joint effort by both institutions to develop a National Development Plan (NDC) which will serve to define national priorities and goals that respond to the specific needs of, and differences between, women and men, as well as other vulnerable groups, in the face of climate change, taking into consideration age, ethnicity, disability and/or human mobility.

### 2.1.4 An NDC with a gender perspective

Ecuador is defining its NDC through a participatory process led by the MAIE, with support from a number of aid agencies: the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP), the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO), the French Development Agency (AFD), the NEC-Partnership, the German Corporation for International Cooperation (GIZ), Eurolima Pro, the Inter-American Foundation for Sustainable Development (Fondo Andino Ecológico), and Cooperación Española (FEIAPP).

The NDC Support Program is being implemented through a Technical Roundtable on Gender and Climate Change, which is led by the MAIE with support from the UNDP. This group also includes representatives from Fundación Futuro Latinoamericano (FFLA), which provides facilitation support; the National Council for Gender Equality; the German Corporation for International Cooperation (GIZ); the Universidad Técnica Particular de Loja (UTPL); Universidad San Francisco de Quito (USFQ); the Rosa Luxemburg Foundation; and Cooperación Española (FEIAPP). The Technical Roundtable on Gender and Climate Change was founded in response to a need to establish links between academia, non-governmental organizations and international cooperation agencies. Its main objective is to provide support for the study, incorporation and strengthening of a gender approach in climate action in Ecuador.

In addition, with the support of Cooperación Grupo Randi (CGR), a participatory methodology was developed to include a gender approach.


#### 4) National Climate Change Adaptation Plan (PNCMAE) and gender mainstreaming

The PNCMAE establishes actions to be taken by public institutions and activities based on areas of work or sectors that evidence has shown, in theory and practice, to be most vulnerable to climate change and to cause the most economic, social and environmental harm to the country as a result. The main vulnerability factors identified for Ecuador are those mentioned above: the relationship with the social, economic or environmental system. Equally, the sectors prioritized for climate change adaptation in Ecuador can be grouped according to their relationship with the social, economic, and environmental systems (MAIE, 2012, 87).

In this sense, and taking into account the scope of adaptation initiatives, there are more opportunities to include women and sections considered at risk or vulnerable.


---
**Page 149**

2.2 Political participation at national, provincial, and local levels

Female voting in Ecuador increased from 76% to 84% between 2009 and 2018. However, since the National Assembly was established in 2009, it has not included an equal number of men and women: in 2009, only 40 (32.3%) of the 124 elected assembly members were women. (National Electoral Council, 2019)

In the project areas there is low female representation at the parish and mayoral level. It is interesting to note the differences of gender parity in parish candidates versus election: it is clear that women candidates are not as successful in being elected. It is also interesting to note that in the project areas, women are underrepresented at the mayoral level in both candidates and elected positions, with particular starkness in the northern sites where there are no female mayors.

Looking at the gender gap in politics can be a useful indicator of women's perceived standing in society, as well as highlight some of the historical inequalities that continue to persist. In this instance, it is clear that the project areas have larger gender gaps than the rest of the country, especially when it comes to actually being elected to a position of power and influence. The reasons for these differences are complex but may include factors such as lower levels of education among women in some regions, fewer women who have achieved the level of education needed to run for elected positions, fewer women who have the level of education needed to be elected.

# Socio-economic information

At a national level, the statistics and gender diagnosis presented in the Agenda for Women 2018-2021 (National Council for Gender Equality, CNIG) have shown that relations between men and women are not equal. The gender gap is reflected in the fact that women bear the brunt of inequalities. At a macro level, and especially in rural settings, women have found themselves increasingly burdened with household chores and childcare responsibilities, while men have greater opportunities to engage in the public sphere, in making decisions that affect the community and their families, and in business dealing with external stakeholders.

Overburdening caused by the roles played by women in the home is another issue identified by communities. Women are responsible for managing family income, providing food and other basic needs to their children and other family members, both in terms of providing general care and financial resources. Women have the most important role in society because they play a part in all the different spheres of activity: the domestic sphere (70% female participants), the productive sphere (65% female participants) and the political sphere (one female participant). Likewise, men (in their roles as fathers) also face increasing socio-economic challenges. The most important challenge is that women are increasingly involved in fishing to travel even further out to sea where open waters are dangerous. Men's fishery-based livelihoods are changing as Berli & Gabor (2018) explain, the lack of employment in recent years has driven men into other activities.

The national unemployment levels have shown a clear decline over the last five years and stood at 4.6% in March 2017. The unemployment rate was higher for women than for men — in 2019 5.7% for women compared to 3.8% for men. In the artisanal fishing sector, the conditions of poverty and extreme poverty are high in all the cases analysed.

The national unemployment levels have shown a clear decline over the last five years and stood at 4.6% in March 2017. The unemployment rate was higher for women than for men — in 2019 5.7% for women compared to 3.8% for men. In the artisanal fishing sector, the conditions of poverty and extreme poverty are high in all the cases analysed.

Inequality in education and literacy can be a product of gender inequality in society and can directly influence women’s ability to participate, engage, and influence climate activities. In this situation, however, it is encouraging to see that literacy and schooling are relatively equal between males and females in the project sites.

Education is limited to the primary level. Secondary education can only be pursued in the larger towns where there are sufficient resources for students to travel back and forth. This is a hindrance for women because it limits their access to education – “leaving” their communities requires money for transport. In addition, as victims of violence and sexual harassment, they feel vulnerable at having to travel long distances on public transportation in the evenings or early mornings when there are few people around.


---
**Page 153**
---

transport. Lastly, women’s responsibility toward caregiving makes this their priority within their communities, leading them to give up the chance to pursue education.

The rural population does not have access and full coverage to attend primary, secondary and higher education. Formal education presents limitations, especially at high school, whose percentages of attendance are lower in comparison to elementary school, in most rural parishes (Pérez et al., 2018). In the case of García de la Garza municipality, parents or grandparents who are parents prefer not to send their kids to the school on another island because of security issues with privacy/personal communication.


## Time use/division of labor

In all project areas, caregiving dynamics are similar and, for the most part, are the responsibility of women. Described below is an example of a daily routine encompassing the different expressions of this division of labor.

Women living in mangrove swamps work long days, which begin with family caregiving responsibilities and then harvesting or fishing activities. They return to cook for the family and look after the children, and then cook dinner and finally rest. The average working day for women is around 14 hours a day, although this varies depending on the season. This time is rarely seen as a social investment.

By contrast, men have much fewer days, fishing or harvesting for between four and five hours. The interval after their day’s fishing or harvesting is over, they are relieved from work. This situation changes when they work externally and find themselves compelled to work as day laborers in shrimp farms or on a country estate or ranch (finca), in which case they leave home at around 5:00 am and return between 6:30 pm and 7:00 pm for dinner, lunch and rest after lunch. Work with alcohol were mentioned, but not ambiguous. Some interviewees mention the fact that when decisions on how use financial resources are left entirely to a men, a good share is spent in alcohol.

In this context, spare time is virtually unheard of for women. They rarely have free time when get together with friends or family members and they do not rest after lunch. Work with alcohol were mentioned, but not ambiguous. Some interviewees mention the fact that when decisions on how use financial resources are left entirely to a men, a good share is spent in alcohol.

These observations and surveys are validated by a gender-time study survey conducted earlier (NEC 2010) in the northern province of Esmeraldas. The study highlights a similar unequal division of labor between men and women in this region of Ecuador.


Together, this data reveals the excessive burden faced by women in work both inside and outside the home. In sum, they spend their time tending to household members, obtaining resources to support their families, and engaging in community activities. The situation becomes very much more difficult for a woman who is head of a household where resources need to be obtained on a daily basis in order to subsist.

**Page 154**

2.6 Health and gender-based violence

Life expectancy in Ecuador is approximately 76.4 years (73.7 for men and 79.1 for women). The maternal mortality rate across Ecuador is 59/100,000 live births; infant mortality is approximately 17 deaths/1,000 births. Healthcare is available in the main towns of each canton, but access to healthcare can be difficult because many people are not able to afford it or do not have transportation. For instance, leave the communities one or two months before they are due and stay with relatives so they can receive care in nearby hospitals in the cities of Machala, Guayaquil or Esmeraldas.

Rates of gender-based violence are very high and are linked to the socioeconomic dynamics of exclusion and poverty. The table above presents the magnitude of the issue and manifestations of violence most common. Further, it reveals that incidents are relatively similar in both urban and rural areas. In terms of geography, the project sites are similar to those at the national rate, which is nearly twice as high as the global average.

The importance of gender-based violence was underscored during our field data gathering; one female leader reported that almost all women had endured this kind of violence at some point in their lives. The leaders were able to identify the signs and symptoms themselves, their body language confirming that they had experienced or witnessed this type of violence.

The increase in gender-based violence in disaster situations and places experiencing economic tension has been well documented. Both of these factors are closely linked to climate change on the one hand and poverty on the other. The combination of these two factors, together with the effects of climate change and climate-related disasters, gives rise to heightened social and family tensions. In addition, gender-based violence is often used as a tool for maintaining gender inequality and given that Ecuador already has a high rate of gender violence at over 60%, the phenomenon is likely to worsen as the effects of climate change become more severe. At


---
**Page 155**
---

the same time, as Ecuador remains stuck in the grip of the COVID-19 pandemic, the effects of which will be felt for many years to come, it is also important to stress that gender-based violence has increased drastically throughout the country as a result of the shutdown and economic tension. A 2017 report by the Red Cross on gender violence on the Ecuadorian coast (the Manabi province) found that women in shelters were more likely than men to experience sexual violence, and that they had lower sensitivity in the disaster response mechanisms and called for better prevention and an improved response to gender-based violence in shelters.

At gender-based violence is exacerbated by economic stress; thus relieving this stress can help mitigate and reduce the incidents of GBV. Proactively working with men and women to adapt and buffer their livelihoods to climate change impacts, which can then hopefully reduce economic stress, can be an important mitigation tactic.


### Assets and infrastructure

Local dynamics in regard to economic and financial matters and other assets are similar across the project areas. Women have some opportunities for income generation because they are usually the ones who manage household consumption. Women have no capital to post as collateral to access credit (information from interviews with women). In addition, there is a lack of information on savings and credit systems; there are many other options possible. Sadly, there is no way to put them into effect because we cannot access credit. (data collection workshop, comment by one female participant)

Men, on the other hand, have higher earning potential and greater opportunities to access formal loans. Additionally, benefits were recorded from the rural social service program for small-scale agricultural workers, Seguro Social Campesino, in one area in the north (Manabi).

As far as infrastructure is concerned, there are no paved roads or bridges in Muisne, while Muisne and Machala had running, non-potable water, and in Puente Río Guayas Estuary water has to be managed on the islands, generally by collecting rainwater. Some women sell fresh fish from their boats at the market, but this is not a sustainable income stream.

In the areas close to cities, they do have access to cellular phone and Internet services, which has helped connectivity, especially in Muisne and Guayas Estuaries. The service is weak and at times non-existent, but it does at least enable communication with neighboring areas.

The roads to the main towns of the cantons are usually paved, with gravel road serving to connect them to the coast or other cantons. This creates a sense of vulnerability for women, as they have to walk long days, often without public lighting.

Speedboats are available to travel by river between Jambelí and Machala, and between Guayas Estuary and Muisne. In the case of Guayas Estuary, transport is available from the back road to the town center. River transport is safer because bopping takes place in the town center and the place of arrival has better infrastructure.

3. Access, use, and management of mangroves

### 3.1 Importance of mangroves to local populations

The mangroves provide a livelihood for fishing communities, mestizos, settlers, and Afro-Ecuadorians, who all live in the project areas. The information on ecosystem services that was generated in participatory workshops with the community showed that it was the provisioning service of food from mangroves that was most valued by the local population. The community reported that the effects of climate change are impacting the provision of these ecosystem services. The regulating service was identified in coastal protection in both cases, but women were more involved than men in this activity. In addition, with reforestation efforts undertaken by both men and women, in the case of southern Guayas. The cultural service was also reported as important to both men and women, especially for the use of ancestral knowledge to cure diseases with the bark and roots of mangroves. These activities are performed both by men (collection) and women (preparation), while in the case of ecotourism, it is women who are more involved.

While outdated, an excellent account of gendered use of natural resources comes from the northern Emeraldas providence (Veach 1996). In this example, ocean fishing is done exclusively by men and provides a major source of income. However, inland fishing is performed mainly by women and is their main source of income. Women generally do the bulk of the fish processing, with men usually marketing the fish. Other livelihood activities performed primarily by men include capture agriculture, while those performed primarily by women includes collecting concha (mollusks) which, while the income potential is less than fishing, is considered to be more reliable.

More recent research with mangrove communities in Emeraldas highlighted the impacts that expansion of shrimp aquaculture has on gendered use of mangrove resources, with collapsing fishing livelihoods leading more men into mangrove exploitation and diminishing women’s traditional roles as collectors (Veatch 2013). This overview of the mangrove resources is leading to depletion of mangrove cockle populations and putting the community in a vulnerable positions as everyone is increasingly dependent on this fishery to sustain themselves.

### 3.2 Participation and decision-making in mangrove management

Women are largely inactive in discussions and decision-making in their communities, but men are usually more vocal in meetings where women represented in the booth. In all these meetings that determine rules like monthly payments of the community fund, defining the rules for crab extraction (amounts extracted by each member, defining the extraction areas, number of hours to be spent fishing), or the rules for concha collection (areas and times for mollusk collection), include female members, and this can be an important place to build their capacities. Certain roles within mixed organizations are more feminized, such as secretary and treasurer. However, in terms of decision-making power, women’s specific interests are rendered invisible, which can deepen divides in terms of access, use, and control. Women’s limited participation can be attributed to several factors.


---
**Page 157**
---

1. One recurring problem in women’s participation is their lack of leadership, in the words of one community leader, “women know everything, the only thing they lack is leadership; they already know the rest.” The fact that women do not feel they have that capacity makes them uneasy. Certain restrictions faced by women have to do with the issue of time and space. Women are often unable to attend meetings or representation in assemblies and standing up for their rights as women.

2. Women also face challenges related to childcare, housework, and the household responsibilities they largely shoulder. “You can’t be because you have to care for me.”

Transportation and accessibility to meeting spaces is also limited. Interviews revealed that women are often unable to attend meetings due to lack of transportation or time. This knowledge allows them to move around the area with ease and facilitates their participation in various decision-making fora. Women do not own the canoes or motorized boats used for transportation.

It is important to mention that women engage to a greater extent when it comes to attending meetings involving securing one or more basic services for coastal communities. In other words, women are more active in activities related to community services and works on the mangrove ecosystem.

The mangrove management organizations in project sites were established with the aim of promoting the use of mangroves and accessing communal benefits. The mangrove use agreements are drawn up by the associations, which have a specific objective. While there are no formal rules or regulations regarding the use of mangroves for women’s activities, women have limited decision-making power even though they may be association members. In one community, access to decisions and decision-making regarding the mangrove is particularly difficult because women do not participate in meetings where these issues are discussed. There is little interest or receptiveness toward identifying spaces to provide or promote activities associated with women’s use or exploitation of mangrove resources.

One other organizational aspect that should be mentioned is that there are community boards (organizational structures) which have a board of directors. In the north, the boards of directors in associations generally made up of 5 people, who were selected by all members of the Association. Leadership positions on these boards are usually held by men and women tend to participate as so or secretaries or board members with no additional specific decision-making tasks.

There are also organizations of fishermen, crabs and black clam harvesters. They also have boards of directors that handle and address separate needs to achieve and improve on their objectives. Women’s participation in these boards plays a minimal role on the board, but this depends on the particular nature of each organization.


Below is a table that describes the number and gender make-up of mangrove associations in the four project sites. The data shows that there are more women than men involved in the governance set up between the northern and southern estuaries. In the south, women’s difficulty in participating in decision-making derives from the fact they do not familiar with the mangrove or legally recognized as part of their rights to use it. Women have limited access to information on the conservation and use of the mangroves area. In the north, women can walk to the mangroves in groups, but in the south the mangroves area are far from the community, so they have to take a boat. Usually, women’s participation is minimal or non-existent.

## 3.2.1 Northern estuaries (Cayapas-Mataje + Muleme)

**Cayapas-Mataje**

The primary use of the northern Cayapas-Mataje estuary is chiefly Afro-Ecuadorian and in sociocultural and local development terms, it is dependent on exploiting natural resources from the mangroves, particularly black clams and blue crabs, and fishing in general.

In Cayapas-Mataje, men and women work harvesting black clams and other resources. In terms of natural resource management, women are especially remarkable as they have great knowledge about the life cycle of the black clam and the best times to harvest them for the sale of black clams and use the earnings to purchase certain products to feed their families.

Typically, each community in the northern Cayapas-Mataje estuary has specific areas for black clam harvesting. In some communities, women are responsible for managing these areas while in others, men harvesters use small wooden canoes with oars, which limits the distances they travel to harvest. In addition, women prefer to harvest in areas close to their communities. Fishermen tend to alternate between fishing and harvesting clams, as this is a more flexible activity that allows them to recover. Traditionally, harvesters return the "mother" (with eggs) so she may reproduce, and they also return small clams (under 4.5 cm long) to allow them to grow.

Black clam harvesting has traditionally been performed by women. One reason for this is the fact that it requires little capital investment; however, a certain amount of capital is required. The harvesting areas are close to communities, which allows women to take their children with them while they work and also makes it possible for them to combine this activity with fishing.

Black clam harvesting is the most popular form of work undertaken by women in the area. In the 99 households that reported black clam harvesting as their main livelihood, 82.4% of harvesters were women. The average age was 35 years old (range: 17–60). Women who harvest at further 2-4% of women purchase black clams to sell them on to external intermediaries. One of


---
**Page 159**
---

The most significant findings is that 20% of female-headed households depend almost exclusively on black clam harvesting, and in 10% of mixed households, both spouses harvest black clams as their main, and in some cases only livelihood.

Black clam harvesting is the livelihood of 16% households. Other shellfish are also gathered at the same time as black clams, but they have little market value and are used to provide protein for household consumption.

Interestingly, today 17.6% of black clam harvesters are men. The interviews revealed that men have taken over black clam harvesting as they have no other sources of income, having been driven out of farming due to the lack of land to till (Dungaree et al., 2005).

In Cayapas-Mataje, day laborers and fishers revolve around seasonal labor and livelihoods. They derive their income primarily from black clam harvesting (known as "conchero"), crab harvesting, and fishing. To a lesser extent, they perform agricultural work like sowing and picking coconuts or other fruits on the land of others for a fee. For shellfish, but these activities are more for self-consumption, subsistence, or barter for other foods.

The work carried out by Cayapas-Mataje associations is traditional in nature. For example, no processing facilities exist to handle the catch from the black clam harvesting season. Some debt is incurred by men for the purposes of aquaculture, but women face gender discrimination due to the scant value placed on their work, which is evident in the restrictions they face in accessing credit and loans. Women are also more likely than men to be denied access to credit by lending agencies and the financial system itself. For example, only 9% of the loans for female heads of household did not require any type of guarantee, compared to 29% of male heads of household. Women have, on average, a labor income of $10 per day (USD), while men earn an average daily wage; the average labor income of a man is at a job with a L27 times higher than the income earned by a woman in the same sector (ICID 2018). 35% of female heads of household access credit from informal sources, which only happens to 25% of men. This situation occurs at different levels and in urban and rural areas.

**Muine**

Muine Cojines is notable for the high level of participation by women as members and leaders of these associations.

| Black clam harvesting (conchero) | Cooking, fishing, and selling | Fishing and selling |

| --- | --- |

Most land in the mangrove area is communally owned. However, women are only minimally included in decision-making because they do not feature explicitly in the regulatory framework. Furthermore, cultural practices maintain the sexual division of labor and continue to exclude women from leadership positions.

Often, it is men who hold formal positions of leadership within communities and fishermen's associations. Women, on the other hand, are responsible for homemaking, caring for family members, and children. Very few are able to generate their own income through sales in small businesses like retail or crafts.

In Muine, women engage in housework and caregiving activities but are also involved in collecting black clams. Crab and black clam harvesting is the main economic activity, performed on a daily basis by both men and women. Women are responsible for marketing the produce in small-scale fishing. Women as young as 13 get involved in harvesting the black clams. In this area it is also common to find women who are the heads of their households and who, for various reasons, have become economically independent.

Those being the two main economic activities associated with the mangrove swamp, men and women have developed adequate know-how to improve harvesting in tough conditions: mud, breaking the shells, etc. However, this is not always the case.

It was reported that a hundred clams fetch $2.50 USD and a string of 20 crabs $3.00 USD. The women do not get involved in selling what they harvest – that is the responsibility of the men. However, situations arise when intermediaries come into the communities to purchase the seafood gathered.


---
**Page 161**
---

In these communities, men have a greater chance of engaging in productive labor in the surrounding areas. Both black crabs and crabs are sold on the local market to intermediaries who transport the products to large cities in the north of the country.

There is a month-long closed season between February and March, with a permanent ban on harvesting black crabs below a certain size. The aim of these bans is to ensure the reproduction, growth, and survival of the crab population. During this time, women are allowed to collect small crabs as part of the closed season as the Ministry of Environment (the controlling agency) works to disseminate information about the importance of the ban.

The women are well aware that they must only collect black crabs greater than 4.5 cm, as provided by law, and the rest must be returned to the mangrove. Similarly, they know the dates of the closed seasons for crabs by heart (even though they may not know why it is forbidden to harvest them). They are also aware that they cannot use any kind of chemical or fishing chemicals, and they use traditional methods of fishing, with a casting net or "wide-meshed" fishing net. Other than this, they know little or nothing about environmental conservation, resource management, or the importance of protecting "endangered and forest species."

(interview with female community leader)

During the closed seasons, men and women from the communities have access to access economic opportunities in other sectors and activities, especially for women, who have less training or formal education and have caregiving responsibilities. It was reported in the interviews that very few women are able to leave the home to engage in paid work, as women bear a greater responsibility for household duties. Furthermore, the existing work opportunities can be summarized as follows:

- Shrimp farms, where shrimp are bred and harvested. This work is carried out in the water, so due to the nature of this job, this is also due to the use of potentially disease-causing chemicals.
- Farm work, which includes sowing, reaping, cleaning, and harvesting crops. Working hours are from 7 a.m. to 4 p.m., which a short lunch break. These tasks are considered a man's job and very few women access this type of work on private land.

- State institutions generally look to employ people with at least a high school diploma. Given the nature of these jobs, they require a certain level of education. For many formal education, a very low percentage of women are able to access this kind of work.

Meanwhile, men work as day laborers on the crops or nearly farms and in stocking and tending shrimp. Men are able to travel to nearby towns to offer their labor as construction workers or any other type of service. However, this is more difficult for women as it is not culturally accepted.

This analysis clearly shows gender inequalities in the use of access to control of mangrove resources in the project's three regions. With the goal of guaranteeing gender equity and sustainability in managing conservation, substantial efforts will be deployed to ensure that both men and women have equal opportunities to participate in the management of these resources. A detailed gender plan will also be developed and will contain specific recommendations, activities, staffing details, and the budget.

3.3 Southern estuaries (Guayas + Jambeil)
Guayaquil

The mangrove forest in Guayas is notable for being very near the city of Guayaquil. The mangroves can be reached by land and water, and this has shaped the socio-productive landscape among many people. The Guayas municipality contains 20% of the country's shrimp farms.

In the Guayas River archipelago, the local population possesses ancestral community property under the Constitution of Ecuador, with recognition of customary law and the reception, allocation, and titling of the lands and territories of indigenous communities, Afro-Ecuadorian peoples, and nationalities. The Afro-Ecuadorian people and Mumbuca people, under the Constitution and international agreements, have rights to land and natural resources (Law 109 on Rural Lands and Ancestral Territories). However, the land is located in protected areas over which the Ministry of Environment has influence, and therefore they are required to draw up an agreement on the Sustainable Use and Stewardship of the Mangrove Ecosystem (AUSECM).

Due to socio-cultural dynamics, women and men have different experiences in terms of access to and use of natural resources, as summarized in the following table.

| Women | Men |
| --- | --- |
| They do not know the mangrove forest and it does not appear to them as a place to visit or get to know. They operate in populated areas. | Men know the mangrove forest, use it and decide which areas will be harvested for mangrove ghost crabs. |
| Women do not take part in decision-making processes and take primary responsibility for care-giving activities. | They sign the mangrove use agreements. |

The area is subject to a ban on crab and black clam harvesting for one month between February and March to enable the species to reproduce and grow. During this period, the population is able to fish for other species of crustaceans such as shrimp and prawns. In addition, there are a large number of men work in the community as service providers for the construction, agro-experts and shrimp farms. In comparison, this is more common in the south because in the north these economic activities are less frequent.

On the other hand, due to the geographical position of the Puerto Rosario and Balao area (an estuary with remnants of mangroves), the population devotes itself fully to harvesting crabs and black clams. The women spend their days collecting crabs and prawns in the mangrove forest on a daily basis to harvest. This work takes up about 4 hours of their day. They also fish for their own consumption.

Women, on the hand, are responsible for caregiving duties. Some attempt to earn an income from removing and selling crab pulp. They also work selling clothes or cosmetics or invest in small stores that serve the basic needs of the local population. Single women generally live

In terms of vulnerabilities or impacts, a lack of water for human consumption and difficulties securing healthy food that means the population's nutritional requirements are hard to see. Lastly, it is worth pointing out that the population is highly dependent on larger towns to obtain food, reducing opportunities for a full diet year round. The inhabitants see themselves cut off from the mangrove system as the coastal island communities have difficulty accessing services, workspaces, and life options (interview with a female leader from Guayas).

One other important subsistence activity is gathering honey for sale. For women, this remains difficult because they are not able to get involved in the process.

The problems cited include vulnerability to climate change including flooding and a loss of livelihoods caused by environmental pollution.

# Jambell

The mangrove forests of the province of El Oro are located in the Jambell estuary, a special management zone on the outskirts of Puerto Bolivar, an area accessed by boat and inhabited by a number of communities. Socioeconomically it is challenging for those living in the area, as services such as health care and education are not available to them so they are plainly dependent on the mainland. This situation directly affects gender dynamics.

The characteristics of the management zone complicate land ownership as there is no land titling system. The mangrove forests have been used by local communities for fishing, gathering resources and building houses and buildings that have been maintained for over 50 years since the first settlers arrived. In addition to the local population, men and women from the city of Machala come to the mangrove forests for their own benefit.

In this particular context, women have limited opportunities to engage in fishing; many work shucking crabs. Those living in the areas surrounding Jambell may fish and sell what they catch.

In the Puerto Bolivar area, black clam and crab harvesting takes place in the Guayas River estuary, where there is a lack of mangrove forests. Over the last 20 years, the local population has come under intense pressure from logging companies that have sought to cut down mangrove forests for their own benefit.

In this area, the productive labor carried out by men and women can be characterized as follows.


| Activity | Women | Men |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Caregiving | Mostly the responsibility of women. | Help without, out taking full responsibility. |
| Tourism | Food preparation. | Boat rides, transportation within and outside the estuary. Also include activities around the islands. |
| Mangrove ghost crab and lobster harvesting | Collect clams from the mangrove swamp and sell them. | Pulp removal, some sell to small traders. |
| Black clam harvesting | Some women fish with their husbands. This is not their explicit responsibility, rather they help. | Harvesting. |
| Fishing | Fishing in the estuary. Net or rod fishing by day; they only fish for a few hours in the morning. | |
| Oyster | Collection and sale. | |
| Group cleaning | Collect waste, sort it and sell whatever can be recycled. | Source: Survey on Perception of Environmental Standards in Mangroves, LESTEN-AM, August 2015.
