Women engage in artisanal fishing more frequently here than in other areas and, in fact, some are members of artisanal fishing organizations. As mentioned in the table, the type of fishing equipment used in the area allows women to access and take part in fishing activities. In this sense, women experience some difficulties in accessing, using, and controlling natural resources. Women's interests become invisible when they are not represented as actors or decision-makers in community mangrove management processes; their own specific interests become invisible, which may exacerbate divides in terms of access, use, and control.

Lastly, the Jambel River estuary has one association made up exclusively of women and some other associations have female leaders.

# Vulnerability and adaptation to climate change

It is clear that, as a result of gender norms, men and women are impacted differently by climate related events. Global evidence has shown that in places with high gender inequality, women and children are much more likely to die because of natural disasters. Rising sea levels and flooding from rivers have been cited as the most significant impacts of climate change on coastal areas. These also have consequences like an increase in water salinity and water-borne diseases. Both are more likely to directly affect women who tend to be responsible for collecting water for the household, cooking, and other responsibilities of caring for sick family members. Likewise, men, who are primarily responsible for sea fishing and agriculture are negatively impacted by increasingly unpredictable weather.

## 4.1 Impacts and response to past climate-related events

The project's area of influence is places of high climate risk and vulnerability. As islands, they run the risk of flooding and floods that would affect the human population, together with pollution from waste brought over by currents from the mainland.

In order to understand how people experienced climate change in the past, we asked displaced women and men in the project sites, we asked workshop respondents to describe their past experiences. The data collection process included identifying perceptions of climate change vulnerability. This exercise was devised based on the last 20 years and the characteristics identified.


| Project site | Northern (Muizen) | Southern (Gouves) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Year | Event | Differential impacts on women | Differential impacts on men |
| Northern (Muizen) | El Niño phenomenon | The women and they were unable to work. They emigrated to other areas for work (cacao, rubber, banana). | Men black clam harvesting. They moved to other areas to collect black clams. Some of the men who emigrated to work in Alcanzores (in Venezuela) did not return because they "discarded their children". |
| 1970 | La Niña phenomenon | Many emigrated to other countries (Spain, USA, and China) because they could no longer make a living. | Men could not find work or income; migration was very difficult for them. Some of the men who emigrated did not return because they "discarded their children". |
| 2000 | La Niña phenomenon | Good quality and quantity of crops on farms on the local market fell. | When fish were abundant, prices on the market fell. |
| Southern (Gouves) | El Niño phenomenon | Epidemics and diseases. Children had underweight and malnutrition. | Loss of livelihoods. Area of mangroves flooded. |

# Climate projections in project sites and implications for women and men

Climate change will result in significant impacts on local environmental conditions along the coast of Ecuador, including a rise in sea levels, El Niño events, and changes in the intensity and variability of rainfall, flooding, and atmospheric temperatures.

Based on models generated by NASA, climate change could cause sea levels to rise by up to 27 cm by 2050. In addition, with an increase in storm surges and wave setup due to a projected 18% increase in sea levels, periods of heavier rainfall are expected to worsen the rise in sea levels.

The risk of flooding is a major concern for the coast of Ecuador. The Dartmouth Flood Observatory (DFO) at the University of Reading (Dartmouth Flood Observatory, n.d.) Flooding was particularly severe during the El Niño events of 1972/73, 1982/83, 1997/98, and in 2002 and 2012, affecting between 184,000 and 222,000 ha (DFO). The increase in sea levels will lead to a rise in the level of land below sea level (SLR), particularly due to extreme sea-level (ESL) events, which in particular storm surges and wave setup (an increase in the mean water level due to the presence of breaking waves) are worsened by projected increases in wind speed. The combination of increased ESL levels and rising sea levels will lead directly to an increase in coastal flooding in Ecuador (Reguero et al., 2015).

Given their regular contribution to sea level rise, El Niño events are of particular concern. The most recent El Niño event was particularly severe, with a peak sea-level rise of 34 cm and changes experienced during the El Niño events. The increase in sea levels, together with exceptionally high rainfall associated with El Niño on the coast of Ecuador, limits the discharge of rivers into coastal areas and increases the risk of flooding. This is especially true for low-lying areas along the entire coastal region, which are not yet captured in existing models of coastal and river flooding. Based on current assessments, 59% of areas within 5 km of the Ecuadorian coast are already at risk from sea-level rise (Reguero et al., 2015).

Existing gender inequalities may be exacerbated by the dynamics of climate change in the project area. That said, the impacts and challenges are expected to be greater in the following areas:

| Year | Phenomenon | Description |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 2009 | Ocean current | They were left with no sources of income and had to sell their belongings or be evicted with household items. |
| 1998 | El Niño phenomenon | They were left with no sources of income and had to sell their belongings or be evicted with household items. |
| 2004 | Sweat | Women lost their financial resources due to the loss of crops, livestock, and other assets. |
| 2017 and Red tide | Red tide | Women who fished for these resources were unable to fish in this area, losing a source of work. |

Source: Field data collection through interviews and participatory workshops, August 2019.


---
**Page 168**
---

- Having access to water fit for human consumption. At present they only have rainwater or tap water, pointing to substantial difficulty in adequately securing this resource, especially if droughts become longer or unpredictable. The water problem affects the general population because they lack adequate water, both in terms of quantity and quality, which is a social responsibility that falls directly on their shoulders.
- Depending on the market for food of adequate quantity and quality. This may be a disadvantage to women who are not the main breadwinners because all the basics need to be purchased with money, and the money is usually controlled by men.
- Women in rural areas face additional challenges due to geographical constraints and situations of extreme poverty, and lack access to general resources: land, tools, and formal education for gainful employment. This situation hinders their access to options to improve their livelihoods or to participate in decision-making processes. This is true of both, as explained in previous sections; women do tend to be at higher risk and with fewer assets.
- Moreover, many lake women are usually forced to search for work in other municipalities and provinces they live, leaving behind their families behind. Main sources of work are banana plantations and fishing.
- The most vulnerable groups are older men, divorced, widowed or single women, who do not have the financial support of members of a community organization or of a family with income. These groups are more likely to be excluded from resources that ensure livelihoods. The problem of single, widowed and divorced women is complex and in this respect the interviews did not provide sufficient information. The elderly related to artisanal fishing activities are also vulnerable because they do not have social networks to rely on.
- The gap between formal employment may be worsened by a lack of access to formal education and natural resources. For example, the women in the communities visited have no formal education; making it difficult for them to access information to make appropriate decisions about their livelihoods or to participate effectively in other education settings. This occurs as a result of the lack of education available and because their relationship with the market is different.
- Artisanal fishing activities are also exclusive fisheries in particular, perpetuate conceptions of masculinity that, prevent fishermen from taking initiatives to break the cycle of poverty. The social pressure to conform to traditional gender roles, such as the social obligation to comply with the role of suppliers, pushes fishermen to irresponsible fishing behavior, justifying the non-observance of environmental regulations. Attitudes of marginalization and exclusion are also present in these communities, which lead staff into attitudes that show weakness or any trait considered feminine, repudiating feelings to manifest their masculine position, defining themselves as someone without fear or pain, with a heroic image.
- Furthermore, it should be borne in mind that women will continue to spend a substantial amount of time on caregiving, lessening their chances of being able to truly participate in decision-making and in managing the use and exploitation of mangrove resources.

# Conclusion and implications for project

This gender assessment highlights the complete set of historical, social, cultural, and economic factors that influence the project site’s current gender norms and inequalities. Firstly, men and women are vulnerable and susceptible to the impacts of climate change in differentiated ways; they also face differentiated measures of vulnerability. Secondly, there is a lack of equal participation by women in community-based mangrove/fishery decision-making processes. Thirdly, the data presented suggest that women are overburdened with work, especially reproductive labor, and this burden increases during times of climate change-induced stress or disasters. And finally, gender-based violence is quite high and further highlights the inequalities at the household level and an important additional barrier that keeps women from using their voice and shaping decisions in communities.

In these mangrove-dependent communities, while both men and women are vulnerable to climate change impacts, women often have higher vulnerability due to higher rates of poverty, fewer assets, and limited access to resources. Women also bear a disproportionate burden compared to men. Men tend to control the main economic activity in the communities: the harvesting and sale of seafood. It is mainly women who engage in subsistence farming, gardening within communities and fishing occupations. Women, on the other hand, take care of household tasks, caregiving for family members and cooking; very few are able to generate their own income.

Taking a holistic and proactive approach, the project’s gender action plan will ensure that the project’s goals and activities take into account the complex realities that women and men face and help to improve the resilience of the mangrove-dependent communities.

# Gender evaluation for the project

## Root causes

| Sociocultural norms regarding land access and ownership | Limited work opportunities for women in mangrove areas, made more difficult with climate change |
| --- | --- |
| Women are seen as dependent on men or male family members. | Lack of formal education for both sexes. |

## Implications/causes

| Women & men find it more difficult to generate income, turn to chemical fertilizers and pesticides. | Difficulty making informed, knowledgeable decisions on conservation and work-related issues. | Gender-based violence |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Women do not participate in decision-making. | Women devote long hours to caregiving duties, limiting their ability to participate in community environments and activities. |

## Immediate effects

# Gender Action Plan

Given the gender assessment above, there are several areas where this project can integrate gender-responsive actions to help close gender gaps in mangrove management in order to support more efficient, effective, and equitable mangrove conservation. These opportunities are largely concentrated in the support to mangrove associations where direct collaboration with the women and men who depend on, and manage, the mangroves will occur. Activities within the gender action plan include: (1) a gender advisor to provide technical guidance and oversight of the gender component; (2) two gender focal points within the project management unit, two CI field social specialists in the Guyas and Jambili estuaries, and one PUCSE staff in the northern estuaries (4 CI staff costs total USD 1,575,380); 1 PUCSE staff cost USD 65,540). Together, these individuals will drive the implementation and accountability of the GAP in collaboration with the gender focal points. The gender advisor will also provide technical guidance and oversight (USD 95,202). CI's global Gender Advisor will also support the project’s implementation and monitoring of the GAP (USD 66,531 in staff cost, USD 6,704 in travel).

# Appendix 1: Interview and data collection guide

## Objective:
- Identify the dynamics of participation, decision making.
- Show the impacts differentiated by gender.

## Agenda:

| Time | Activity | Methodology |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **30 min** | Presentation of the activity | The activity will be presented. Additionally, the ice will be broken. Participants will be asked to make a circle. A ball will be handed out at random and will request that when they receive the ball, they must say something about their life in relation to climate change. In addition, they are asked to mention something that they were not able to do because of climate change (e.g., they were women and "I never..."). The facilitator will make the first statement as an example. |
| **40 min** | Community map | Show the landscape in which they live changed and what are the effects of climate change? Show differences in women and men both in the illustration and in reality (e.g., water, food security). |
| **30 min** | Identification of benefits and risks with a gender perspective | Work will be done on identifying the main negative impacts of climate change for women and men, as well as identifying the main benefits and risks. They will be recorded on paper. The discussion will be closed based on the following guiding questions: - What are the differences and differences between the responses of the groups regarding heat, rain and wind? - What are the coincidences and differences between the responses of women and men in relation to the climate? Compare the responses of the different groups and analyze who has greater responsibility for maintaining crops (e.g., women or men) that are affected by changes in the climate, men or women? How does this affect their work of women related to taking care of the home and their diet? Do these impacts represent a threat to the food security of the family? |
| **40 min** | Prioritization of gender issues for women and men. | Once the problems and possible benefits and risks have been identified, the impact areas (community, productive, reproductive and community) will be identified. The criteria used will be used as a filter for prioritization. The prioritization will be based on: |

- Green: priority 3, nothing important to address and improve the impacts of climate change on women's lives.

# Appendix 2: Sample Job Description for Gender Specialist

## Summary:
Conservation International is looking for a Gender Specialist to be based in Guayaquil City. This position is responsible for oversight, coordination, and technical support for the implementation of the project's Gender Action Plan (GAP).

## Key Responsibilities:

- Coordinate/oversee the implementation and execution of the GAP within the framework of the GCF Gender Mainstreaming Policy.
- Develop and deliver appropriate capacity building for colleagues and delivery partners that is well-designed, clear, tailored to the context, and practical.
- Monitor gender-related outcomes through established project-specific indicators and through qualitative and quantitative data collection methods.
- Accompany project teams to the field to ground truth progress on gender outcomes; meet with women/women's groups, collect qualitative information, help project teams to problem solve, propose potential changes, participate in reports and other activities.
- Identify opportunities/entry points to support and encourage a diversity of women's leadership through project activities.
- Develop relationships with gender and women-focused NGOs/CSDs/government entities in order to learn and share lessons.
- Communicate internally externally to share materials, project results, and lessons learned among relevant internal stakeholders and external audiences.
- Be the point person for any gender, Sexual Exploitation and Abuse and Harassment (SEAH) or Gender-based Violence (GBV) related grievance that may come through the project's grievance mechanism.
- Collaborate with CI's global gender program to ensure consistency in gender approach across the institution.

## Required Qualifications:

- Experience in leading the implementation of gender/social analyses and gender action plans in conservation contexts/NHBI-related projects involving indigenous and rural populations.
- Work experience in implementing projects with a gender approach.
- Experience/understanding of adopting a survivor-centred approach to respond to GBV issues.
- Strong interpersonal skills.
- Bachelor's degree with a focus in social science, international development, or related discipline, ideally with postgraduate studies (specializations, diplomas, master’s degree, etc.).


---
**Page 183**
---

* Spanish and English language proficiency. (TOEFL, IELTS, TOEIC certificate etc.)
* Excellent skills in written and verbal communication.
* Essential analytical capacity to implement projects, manage multiple tasks and adapt to a dynamic environment.
* Ability to work well in an interdisciplinary, multicultural, diverse, and dynamic team environment.
* Strategic, creative, flexible, and able to work under general supervision and take initiative to solve problems.
* Competencies with Microsoft Office 365.
